Full Codebase Review — pilot

2026-06-09 · main @ c47e184 · ~55,200 lines reviewed (app 18k · backend 10k · scripts 17k · app_puller 3k · tests 7k) + deploy/CI config · multi-agent review with adversarial verification (run halted early — see notice)

⚠ Read this first — the verification phase was cut short

This review ran as a four-phase pipeline: 18 scoped reviewers (+4 spawned by a completeness critic) produced 183 raw findings; semantic dedupe merged 23; each of the 160 unique findings was then to be checked by two independent adversarial verifiers (an existence lens that re-reads the code, and an impact lens that hunts for mitigations/intentional design), with a third tiebreaker on splits. The run was stopped at the user's request partway through verification (168 of ~340 verifier passes finished). What that means for reading this report:

  • 80 findings are fully confirmed (both verifiers re-read the code and agreed). These are reliable.
  • 75 findings are unverified and 2 partially verified — they come from a single reviewer and were never adversarially re-checked. Calibration from the verified sample: of 83 findings that completed verification, only 1 was refuted (~1%) and 2 ended split — so most unverified findings are probably real, but expect a few false positives, overstated severities, or imprecise line references among them. Verification ran roughly in reviewer order, so tests, puller, deploy, deps-config, cross-duplication, data-handling and all 4 gap-reviewer findings are mostly unverified, while backend/frontend/preprocess findings are mostly confirmed.
  • 2 findings ended 1–1 split and never got their tiebreaker — both are flagged inline; read their verifier notes and judge yourself.
  • Severity calibration is missing for unverified items. On confirmed items, verifiers corrected severity where needed (shown as a purple badge); unverified items carry the original reviewer's uncalibrated severity.
  • Fix-suggestion review is missing for unverified items. Verifiers frequently refined the proposed fix or corrected line numbers in their notes; unverified items' "how to fix" is a single agent's proposal.

Static parcel-owner PII (owners_index.json, parcels.pmtiles) served by Caddy with no authentication

Caddyfile:31-40 · reviewer: backend-security
What it is
The Caddy `handle /data/processed/*` block reverse-serves the volume composite with `file_server` and no auth check of any kind — no JWT verification, no forward-auth to the backend, no Cloudflare Access (ADR-064 line 54 explicitly defers Access). fly.toml points OWNERS_INDEX_PATH at `/data/processed/composite/owners_index.json` (fly.toml line 22), the same path Caddy serves, so the full owners index — owner names, mailing addresses, holdings, assessed values — plus per-county `parcels.pmtiles` are directly fetchable by anyone who knows (or guesses) the URL. The JWT gate only protects `/api/*`; the bulk PII files sit beside it, ungated. The only reason it isn't visibly broken is that the SPA gates *rendering* client-side after login, which is not an access control.
Why it matters
ADR-064 (line 50) and docs/SECURITY.md assert this 4.29 GB of parcel-owner PII is 'behind the app's sign-in gate.' That property is not actually enforced: the data is reachable unauthenticated over the same origin the app runs on. A scraper hitting `/data/processed/composite/owners_index.json` exfiltrates the entire landowner dataset without credentials, which is exactly the PII-at-scale exposure the project treats as a pre-commercial blocker.
How to fix
Either gate `/data/processed/*` through a Caddy `forward_auth` to a lightweight backend endpoint that validates the session cookie/JWT, or move the tiles/index behind backend-issued short-lived presigned URLs (the option ADR-064 line 108 already names). At minimum, reconcile the code with the docs — if unauthenticated serving is the accepted tradeoff, ADR-064 and SECURITY.md must stop claiming the data is behind the login gate.
Expected improvement
Closes a direct, credential-free download path for the entire owner PII corpus and makes the deployed reality match the documented security boundary.
Evidence
handle /data/processed/* {\n\t\troot * {$DATA_WEB_ROOT:/}\n\t\theader Cache-Control "private, no-store"\n\t\tfile_server\n\t} — no auth directive; ADR-064: 'Keeps the 4.29 GB of parcel-owner PII behind the app's sign-in gate'
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): CONFIRMED real and material. Read: Caddyfile (handle /data/processed/* at lines 31-40 has root + Cache-Control + file_server, NO auth directive — verified via repo-wide grep for forward_auth/basicauth = zero hits); fly.toml (line 22 OWNERS_INDEX_PATH=/data/processed/composite/owners_index.json = exactly the path Caddy file-serves); docs/adr/0064 (line 50 "Keeps the 4.29 GB of parcel-owner PII behind the app's sign-in gate", line 54 Access deferred, line 108 presigned URLs named); docs/SECURITY.md (line 37 "Never publicly host composite outputs... access controls enforced"); app/src/lib/data.ts (composite fetches use bare fetch(url) with no Authorization header) + auth-token.ts/leads-api.ts (Bearer JWT attached ONLY to /api/* calls); tests/test_caddy_serving.py (asserts unauthenticated 200/206 for /data/processed/composite/05/parcels.pmtiles). The PII files (owners_index.json, parcels.pmtiles, parquet) are served by Caddy file_server with no auth; the JWT gate covers only /api/*. The frontend itself fetching these with no auth header proves the path must be open for the app to work. This is a genuine gap between documented intent ("behind the sign-in gate," "access controls enforced") and implementation — "same-origin" was conflated with "authenticated." Cited line numbers (Caddyfile 31-40, fly.toml 22, ADR-064 50/54/108) all check out. NOT a perf-audit duplicate: the perf audit's unauthenticated-server note (PERF_AUDIT line 72) concerns the dev :8765 server as accepted posture; its Caddyfile finding (Rank-19/PERF-019) touches only /assets/* caching and explicitly leaves /data/processed/* untouched as a PII rail. This is a distinct security finding. Severity high is appropriate, not inflated: full landowner PII dataset (names, mailing addresses, holdings, assessed values) at predictable URLs with no auth, contradicting a documented pre-commercial blocker. I did NOT bump to critical because the underlying records are individually public-record (sensitivity is in the aggregation/join), only 4 states, and Cloudflare hides the origin IP / provides WAF (perimeter, not access control). Correction/caveat on the proposed fix: the finding's forward_auth suggestion is non-trivial and slightly understated. The JWT is a localStorage Bearer token (not a cookie) attached only to /api/* requests; MapLibre PMTiles Range fetches, DuckDB-WASM parquet fetches, and the bare fetch(url) JSON loads in data.ts carry no auth header. A forward_auth on /data/processed/* would break the app unless the frontend is changed to inject the token into every static Range request (doable for MapLibre via transformRequest, harder for DuckDB httpfs). ADR-064 line 108 already flags presigned URLs as fighting MapLibre/DuckDB long-lived Range fetching. The finding's fallback ("reconcile docs with reality") is the sound minimum. None of this undermines exists=true.
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): CONFIRMED. Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/Caddyfile, fly.toml, docs/adr/0064-phase1-hosting-fly-sqlite.md, docs/SECURITY.md, tests/test_caddy_serving.py, docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md, and app/src/lib/{data.ts,owners-filter-index.ts,leads-api.ts}. Verified facts: (1) Caddyfile lines 31-40 are exactly as quoted — `handle /data/processed/*` with `root * {$DATA_WEB_ROOT:/}` + `file_server`, no auth directive of any kind; the only auth in the whole config is whatever the backend does behind the `/api/*` reverse_proxy (lines 26-28). Caddy is the Fly entrypoint (fly.toml internal_port 8080 = CADDY_PORT). (2) fly.toml line 22 sets OWNERS_INDEX_PATH = /data/processed/composite/owners_index.json with DATA_WEB_ROOT="/" (line 31), so the full owners index file sits inside the web-served subtree and is fetchable at that exact URL. (3) ADR-064 line 50 claims the static data is "behind the existing login / Keeps the 4.29 GB of parcel-owner PII behind the app's sign-in gate"; line 54 explicitly defers Cloudflare Access ("App login stays the authentication boundary"); line 108 names presigned URLs/Access as the alternative — all line cites accurate. (4) Positive confirmation that unauthenticated access is the tested behavior: tests/test_caddy_serving.py asserts a credential-less GET of /data/processed/composite/05/parcels.pmtiles returns 200 (lines 190-191) and 206 for Range requests (line 155); the test file contains zero auth/JWT/401 assertions. (5) The frontend fetches /data/processed/* with plain fetch() and no Authorization header (data.ts:67,77; owners-filter-index.ts:20) — the Bearer JWT is attached only to /api/* via leads-api.ts — so even the app's own traffic to these files is unauthenticated, and the sign-in gate is purely client-side rendering, as the finding says. Corrections: (a) SECURITY.md does NOT literally say "behind the app's sign-in gate" — that exact quote is ADR-064 line 50 only. SECURITY.md's relevant claim is line 37: "Never publicly host composite outputs... Hosted-private only (ADR-055) — access controls enforced," which the current serving violates in substance, so the attribution is loose but the contradiction is real. (b) "Guesses the URL" understates discoverability: the SPA bundle (served unauthenticated with index.html fallback) contains the /data/processed/composite/... paths in cleartext, so any anonymous visitor can read them from the JS. (c) Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit — that audit's only Caddy finding (PERF-019/Rank 19) is about Cache-Control/brotli and explicitly preserves the /data/processed PII posture; its "unauthenticated :8765 accepted posture" note refers to the local dev static server, not production Caddy. (d) Not one of the listed intentional designs — ADR-064 asserts the gate exists, so the code contradicts the documented decision rather than implementing an accepted tradeoff. Severity high is appropriate (parcel-owner names/addresses are largely public-record data and the audience is a 2-person internal tool, arguing against critical; but bulk anonymous exfiltration from the production origin directly contradicts SECURITY.md rule 3 and ADR-064's stated property).
highUnverified backend effort: small

Reusing a retired team name in users.txt silently grants the new team the old tenant's full board

backend/app/tenant_service.py:33-51 · reviewer: identity-tenancy-lifecycle
What it is
The flip side of name-keyed resolution: Tenant rows are never deleted (intentional, per the module docstring 'a team whose users are all removed from the file keeps its board'), and `get_by_name` re-attaches any future team with a matching normalized name to that row. Sequence: team 'alpha' owns tenant 1 with the production board; the operator renames it to 'alpha-llc' (new tenant minted, finding above); months later a contractor or second group is added with team 'alpha' — `find_or_create('alpha')` returns the ORIGINAL tenant 1 row, and those users immediately see every lead, landowner contact detail, note, and attempt history of the original board. Nothing distinguishes a deliberately-resurrected team from an accidental name collision, and no log line records the re-attachment.
Why it matters
This is the one lifecycle path where tenant isolation is breached by a plausible operator edit rather than code. The users.txt admin is trusted, but the grant is accidental and invisible: the new team's members are never told they inherited someone else's board, and landowner PII (docs/SECURITY.md scope) flows to people it was never intended for.
How to fix
Log at INFO when a login resolves a team name to an already-existing tenant that currently has zero users in the credentials file mapping to it (cheap to compute from the parsed file + Tenant lookup), and document in backend/README.md that retired team names must never be reused for a different group. A stronger option is a `created_from_file_hash`-style provenance column or an explicit `retired` flag set by an admin command.
Expected improvement
Accidental inheritance of a retired team's board becomes detectable at the moment it happens instead of after PII has been exposed.
Evidence
def get_by_name(self, name: str) -> Tenant | None:
    return self.session.scalars(select(Tenant).where(Tenant.name == name)).first()
... 'Tenant rows are never deleted — a team whose users are all removed from the file keeps its board.'
mediumConfirmed deploy effort: smallfound independently by 2 reviewers

No Content-Security-Policy, frame-ancestors, or nosniff headers anywhere (index.html + Caddyfile), while the bearer JWT sits in localStorage

Caddyfile:22-23 · reviewer: frontend-security
What it is
The only security header the production edge sets is HSTS (`header Strict-Transport-Security ...` in the Caddyfile); app/index.html carries no CSP meta tag either. There is no Content-Security-Policy, no X-Frame-Options/frame-ancestors (the app can be framed by any site — clickjacking on the lead-triage UI), and no X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff. This matters more than usual here because the session JWT is stored in localStorage (app/src/lib/auth-token.ts: `const STORAGE_KEY = 'pilot.authToken'`), so any future XSS — e.g. through a dependency or a new rendering path over county-sourced owner names — exfiltrates a long-lived bearer token with one `localStorage.getItem`. A CSP is unusually cheap for this app: I verified all script/wasm/worker assets are bundled same-origin (duckdb.ts imports the WASM + workers via `?url` from the local bundle, no CDN) and the only external origin is the CARTO raster tile CDN (`https://{a,b,c}.basemaps.cartocdn.com`, basemap.ts:45).
Why it matters
Today there is no XSS sink (no {@html}; the one Popup.setHTML call escapes its inputs), so the localStorage-token choice is currently safe — but it has zero defense-in-depth. One unescaped render of an owner name (hostile county-record input is plausible per the project's own threat framing) turns into full session theft and PII access, and the missing frame-ancestors leaves the production app frameable for clickjacking right now.
How to fix
Add to the Caddyfile site block: `header Content-Security-Policy "default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' 'wasm-unsafe-eval'; worker-src 'self' blob:; img-src 'self' data: blob: https://*.basemaps.cartocdn.com; connect-src 'self' https://*.basemaps.cartocdn.com; style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'; frame-ancestors 'none'"` plus `header X-Content-Type-Options nosniff`. ('wasm-unsafe-eval' is needed for DuckDB-WASM; blob: workers for MapLibre. Verify against the e2e suite.) Longer term, pairing this with the HttpOnly-cookie session from the /data auth fix removes the token from script-readable storage entirely.
Expected improvement
XSS blast radius drops from silent token theft + arbitrary same-origin fetch to near-nothing; clickjacking is blocked outright.
Evidence
Caddyfile: `# HSTS on every response ...\nheader Strict-Transport-Security "max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains; preload"` — the only header set. index.html <head> contains only charset/viewport/icon/title. auth-token.ts: `const STORAGE_KEY = 'pilot.authToken'; ... if (s) s.setItem(STORAGE_KEY, token);`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read Caddyfile (lines 22-23 correct: HSTS is the only header), app/index.html (no CSP meta), app/src/lib/auth-token.ts (JWT in localStorage 'pilot.authToken'), app/src/lib/duckdb.ts (WASM/workers bundled same-origin via ?url), app/src/lib/basemap.ts + MiniMap.svelte (CARTO CDN is the only external origin), app/src/lib/towers.ts + MapView.svelte (the one setHTML call escapes via escapeHtml; no {@html} in src), backend/app/main.py (only CORSMiddleware — no security headers server-side either). Repo-wide grep for CSP/X-Frame-Options/frame-ancestors/nosniff: zero hits. Refutation attempts failed: no edge-managed headers documented (Cloudflare config is orange/Full-strict + /data/* cache bypass only; Cloudflare adds none of these by default); not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (no header findings in that doc); not an intentional invariant in CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md. Corrections: (1) the fix says "verify against the e2e suite" but that suite is stale (#92) — the right verification hook is tests/test_caddy_serving.py, which boots the real Caddyfile and already asserts HSTS (test_hsts_header_is_set); (2) the proposed CSP directives are plausible (blob: workers genuinely needed for duckdb-wasm; connect-src needs CARTO since MapLibre fetches tiles via fetch; MapLibre inline style attributes need style-src 'unsafe-inline') but must be tested empirically before shipping. Medium severity is fair: pure defense-in-depth today (no live XSS sink), but the app is live in production with parcel-owner PII, a script-readable bearer token, and is frameable right now.
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Confirmed in current code. Caddyfile lines 22-23: HSTS is the only security header (other header directives are Cache-Control only at lines 38 and 61); repo-wide grep for CSP/X-Frame-Options/frame-ancestors/nosniff returns zero hits. app/index.html head has only charset/viewport/icon/title — no CSP meta. app/src/lib/auth-token.ts:7,32 stores the bearer JWT in localStorage under 'pilot.authToken' exactly as quoted. Supporting evidence all real: CARTO CDN is the only external origin (basemap.ts:45; also MiniMap.svelte:21, same origin), duckdb.ts:16-19 bundles WASM+workers same-origin via ?url, no {@html} anywhere in app/src, and the single Popup.setHTML call (MapView.svelte:386) escapes all inputs via escapeHtml in towers.ts:61-69 — so 'no current sink, zero defense-in-depth' is accurately framed. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit: the only header finding there is PERF-019 (immutable Cache-Control on /assets/*, already executed), a caching concern, not security headers. Minor caveats: Cloudflare fronts the app and could theoretically add headers via Transform Rules, but nothing in the repo configures that, so the repo-managed edge truly sets only HSTS; the proposed CSP string is plausible (wasm-unsafe-eval for DuckDB-WASM, blob: workers, cartocdn in img-src+connect-src) but must be validated against the running app before deploy, as the finding itself notes. Severity medium is right: live production app with PII, no exploitable sink today, but missing frame-ancestors leaves it frameable now.
mediumConfirmed backend effort: small

Login throttle keys on request.client.host, which in production is the proxy-chain IP (Cloudflare edge), never the end user's address

backend/app/main.py:215-216 · reviewer: backend-bugs
What it is
The brute-force throttle is keyed `(ip, username)` with `ip = request.client.host`. In production the request path is Cloudflare → Caddy (reverse_proxy, no trusted_proxies configured, so Caddy replaces X-Forwarded-For with its immediate peer = the Cloudflare edge IP) → uvicorn on 127.0.0.1 (supervisord.conf: `uvicorn app.main:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8000`, no explicit forwarded config; uvicorn's defaults trust XFF only from 127.0.0.1, which Caddy is). The 'ip' the throttle sees is therefore a Cloudflare edge/colo IP: shared by every visitor routed through that colo and varying across an attacker's connections. The throttle's two documented properties both degrade — an attacker can rotate edge IPs to get max_failures × N_edges guesses at one username, and the no-lockout-DoS guarantee ('an attacker who knows a username can't lock a real user out' — login_throttle.py module docstring) fails because attacker and victim share the same edge-IP key.
Why it matters
The throttle is the stated compensation for the deliberately specific unknown-user/wrong-password login errors (credentials.py docstring: 'the login throttle (#77) bounds that exposure'). With the IP dimension broken, both the brute-force bound and the username-enumeration bound are weaker than designed, and a password spray against the co-founder's username can 429-block his real logins.
How to fix
Derive the client IP from the trusted chain: read CF-Connecting-IP in the login route (the chain Cloudflare→Caddy→uvicorn is fully controlled), or configure Caddy `trusted_proxies` for Cloudflare's ranges so X-Forwarded-For carries the real client and uvicorn's proxy-headers middleware sets request.client correctly. Verify with a prod log of request.client.host.
Expected improvement
The (ip, username) key actually distinguishes attackers from the legitimate user, restoring both the brute-force bound and the no-lockout property the design documents.
Evidence
main.py: `ip = request.client.host if request.client else \"unknown\"\n    key = (ip, normalize_username(body.username))`; supervisord.conf: `command=uvicorn app.main:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8000`; Caddyfile: `handle /api/* {\n\t\treverse_proxy {$BACKEND_UPSTREAM:127.0.0.1:8000}\n\t}` (no trusted_proxies)
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Checked: backend/app/main.py:215-216 (exact quoted code confirmed: `ip = request.client.host if request.client else "unknown"`, key = (ip, normalized username)); backend/app/login_throttle.py docstring lines 6-8 (the no-lockout-DoS guarantee explicitly depends on IP being in the key); backend/app/credentials.py:19 ("throttle (#77) bounds that exposure" — throttle is the stated compensation for specific 401 messages); Caddyfile (reverse_proxy to 127.0.0.1:8000, NO trusted_proxies); supervisord.conf:16 (uvicorn --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8000, no --forwarded-allow-ips, and grep found no FORWARDED_ALLOW_IPS anywhere incl. Dockerfile/fly.toml); fly.toml ([http_service] internal_port=8080 — fly-proxy fronts Caddy); backend/README.md:213-224 + 273-277 (security model documents "Because the IP is part of the key, an attacker who knows a username can't weaponize it to lock a real user out" with no proxy-chain caveat anywhere — the trade-offs list covers per-process state but not this); docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md (only throttle item is PERF-040, a locking fix — not a duplicate); no CF-Connecting-IP / Fly-Client-IP / X-Forwarded handling exists anywhere in backend, docs, or tests. The finding is real but two details need correction. (1) Mechanism: the chain omits fly-proxy. fly.toml's [http_service] means the path is user → Cloudflare → Fly edge proxy → Caddy(:8080) → uvicorn. Caddy (v2.5+ default) strips X-Forwarded-* from its untrusted peer (fly-proxy) and sets XFF to that peer's address, so the IP uvicorn resolves (it does trust XFF from 127.0.0.1 — uvicorn CLI defaults --proxy-headers on, forwarded-allow-ips=127.0.0.1) is most likely the Fly-internal proxy address, not a Cloudflare colo IP — i.e., effectively ONE shared constant for all visitors, worse than "per-colo". Even in the most conservative reading (uvicorn ignoring XFF), request.client.host = 127.0.0.1 — still constant. Every branch yields "not the end-user IP, shared across all users". (2) Impact direction: the claim "attacker rotates edge IPs to get max_failures × N_edges guesses" is likely inverted — with a near-constant IP key the brute-force bound becomes a GLOBAL 5-failures/5-min per username (tighter, not looser). The real degradation is the lockout-DoS: 5 bad passwords from anywhere 429-blocks the co-founder's fresh sign-ins indefinitely (existing 30-day session JWTs keep working, which bounds the practical pain), directly violating the documented guarantee in both the module docstring and backend/README.md. The username-enumeration bound survives roughly intact. Fix caveat: reading CF-Connecting-IP raw is spoofable if the Fly app is reachable directly at its .fly.dev hostname (bypassing Cloudflare), which would let an attacker mint unlimited throttle keys — worse than today for brute-force. The right fix is the trusted-chain version: Caddy trusted_proxies for the fly-proxy/Cloudflare hops (or verify .fly.dev direct access is blocked) before honoring any client-IP header. Severity medium is fair: public login endpoint (morphyconsulting.com), documented security property broken, but 2-user internal tool and the brute-force bound actually tightens.
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/backend/app/main.py:201-234, backend/app/login_throttle.py (full), backend/app/credentials.py:19, Caddyfile, supervisord.conf, fly.toml, and docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md (PERF-040 + dedup ledger). CONFIRMED. main.py:215-216 is verbatim as quoted: `ip = request.client.host if request.client else "unknown"` / `key = (ip, normalize_username(body.username))`. supervisord.conf:16 runs `uvicorn app.main:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8000` with no --forwarded-allow-ips/--proxy-headers flags (uvicorn CLI default: proxy_headers=True, forwarded_allow_ips="127.0.0.1", i.e. trusts XFF from Caddy). Caddyfile:26-28 is `reverse_proxy {$BACKEND_UPSTREAM:127.0.0.1:8000}` with no trusted_proxies anywhere in the file, so Caddy (v2.5+ semantics) treats its peer as untrusted and replaces X-Forwarded-For with that peer's address. login_throttle.py:6-8 docstring states the no-lockout-DoS guarantee exactly as quoted; credentials.py:19 contains "throttle (#77) bounds that exposure". Not a perf-audit duplicate: PERF-040 is the throttle's *locking* (fixed in c47e184); no audit item touches IP derivation/header trust. ONE FACTUAL CORRECTION (doesn't change existence, sharpens impact): the finding's chain omits the Fly proxy. fly.toml uses [http_service] internal_port 8080, so the real chain is user → Cloudflare → Fly edge proxy → Caddy → uvicorn. Caddy's immediate TCP peer is fly-proxy (a Fly-internal address, e.g. fdaa::/private), not a Cloudflare edge IP, and with no trusted_proxies Caddy writes *that* internal address into XFF. So request.client.host is almost certainly a single (or tiny set of) Fly-internal proxy address(es) shared by ALL visitors — not a per-colo Cloudflare IP. Consequences: (a) the "max_failures × N_edges" guess-multiplication claim is overstated — with a near-constant key IP the attacker gets roughly one global per-username budget, not a multiplied one; (b) the lockout-DoS failure is *stronger* than stated — every visitor shares the key, so any password spray against the co-founder's username 429-blocks his real logins for the full window, a complete failure of the documented guarantee. Net severity: still medium (brute-force bound is not actually widened, but the DoS property and the credentials.py compensating-control rationale are broken). FIX CORRECTION: CF-Connecting-IP alone is insufficient — the app is also reachable directly via its fly.dev hostname (bypassing Cloudflare), where CF-Connecting-IP is attacker-spoofable. On Fly the trustworthy source is the Fly-Client-IP header set by fly-proxy (or Caddy trusted_proxies covering fly-proxy + Cloudflare ranges so XFF carries the real client); CF-Connecting-IP is safe only if direct-to-Fly access is blocked.
mediumConfirmed fetch effort: trivial

safe_extract_zip path-traversal guard uses str.startswith without a separator — sibling-prefix escape bypasses the check

scripts/fetch/sources/_helpers.py:49-51 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
The traversal guard is `target = (dest / name).resolve(); if not str(target).startswith(str(dest.resolve())):`. Because the prefix comparison has no trailing path separator, a zip member like `../r_tower_evil/x` extracted with dest `.../.partial/r_tower` resolves to `.../.partial/r_tower_evil/x`, which string-startswith-matches `.../.partial/r_tower` and passes — then `target.parent.mkdir(parents=True)` happily creates the escape directory. This is the classic prefix-match traversal bug; the helper is the project's sole zip-extraction path (fcc_asr, fcc_uls, faa_oeaaa, eia_860, uspvdb fetches, plus user uploads via app_puller/manual_install.py:134), so every consumer inherits the hole.
Why it matters
A crafted zip can write files outside its designated extraction directory into any sibling whose name shares the dest's leaf as a string prefix (e.g. out of `current/<zip-stem>/` into other dirs under `current/`, or out of `.partial/r_tower` into `.partial/r_tower_x`). Blast radius is contained to the source's data tree on this layout, so it is data-integrity (normalizers rglob the whole tree and would ingest planted files) rather than system compromise — but the helper exists specifically to provide this guarantee (AGENTS.md: 'Use safe_extract_zip for all zip extraction (path-traversal protection)') and its comment claims 'Reject absolute paths and any ".." component', which the code does not actually do.
How to fix
Replace the string-prefix test with `if not target.is_relative_to(dest.resolve()): raise ValueError(...)` (Python 3.9+), or equivalently compare `os.path.commonpath([target, dest.resolve()]) == str(dest.resolve())`. Add a regression test with a member named `../<destname>_evil/x` alongside the existing traversal tests.
Expected improvement
The documented path-traversal guarantee actually holds for all five auto-fetch zip sources and for user-uploaded zips; one-line fix plus a test.
Evidence
_helpers.py:49-51 `target = (dest / name).resolve()\n            if not str(target).startswith(str(dest.resolve())):\n                raise ValueError(f"unsafe path in zip: {name}")`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read _helpers.py:37-60; guard at lines 49-51 matches the quote exactly. Reproduced the bypass: dest=.../r_tower + member '../r_tower_evil/x' resolves to .../r_tower_evil/x, str.startswith(dest.resolve()) returns True (passes), is_relative_to returns False (correct). Classic missing-separator prefix-match bug — confirmed real in current code. Scope is correctly characterized, not inflated: I verified absolute-path members (/etc/passwd) and deep escapes (../../../../etc/evil) are STILL blocked by the startswith check, so the escape is genuinely constrained to sibling dirs sharing the dest leaf as a string prefix within the same parent. The finding's downgrade to data-integrity (rather than system compromise) is honest. Consumers verified: 6 fetch sources (fcc_asr x2, fcc_uls, faa_oeaaa, eia_860, uspvdb) + app_puller/manual_install.py:134 (user-uploaded zips). Sole zip-extraction path — no caller-side guard. Mitigation check: no tests cover traversal (grep of tests/ for safe_extract_zip/traversal/unsafe-path returned nothing). The helper's traversal-protection role is a documented invariant (CLAUDE.md §6 / scripts/fetch/AGENTS.md), so this is a violation, not intentional design. NOT a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit — that doc references this file only for arcgis_query_all (lines 81-132), a different function. Fix is valid: env is Python 3.11.9, so is_relative_to (3.9+) works; proposed commonpath alternative also fine. Minor correction to the finding's wording: the inline comment 'Reject absolute paths and any \"..\" component' is only half-wrong — absolute paths ARE incidentally caught by the startswith check; only the sibling-prefix '..' case slips through. Severity medium is defensible (broken security guarantee of a purpose-built guard); a low argument exists since the sources are trusted government publishers and manual uploads are operator-driven, with sibling-prefix-only blast radius rather than arbitrary write.
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read scripts/fetch/sources/_helpers.py — code exists verbatim at lines 49-51: `target = (dest / name).resolve(); if not str(target).startswith(str(dest.resolve())): raise ValueError(...)`. Empirically reproduced the prefix-match bypass: dest leaf `r_tower` + member `../r_tower_evil/x` → target `.../r_tower_evil/x` (outside dest), yet str.startswith returns True (guard passes) while target.is_relative_to(dest.resolve()) returns False (correct check rejects). Classic sibling-prefix traversal bug. Confirmed safe_extract_zip is the sole zip path; consumers fcc_asr.py:96,110, fcc_uls.py:233, faa_oeaaa.py:85, eia_860.py:123, uspvdb.py:114, app_puller/manual_install.py:134 all inherit it. The line-45 comment 'Reject absolute paths and any \"..\" component' is genuinely misleading — the code relies on resolve+startswith and does neither literally (absolute paths are caught incidentally; the sibling-prefix `..` escape is not). Proposed fix (is_relative_to / commonpath) is correct and verified. Minor non-material correction: the finding's `.partial/r_tower` dest example reflects the atomic-write layout; the actual dest passed in fcc_asr is `dest_dir/r_tower` and in manual_install is `cur/zp.stem` — the bug is layout-independent so this doesn't change validity. Severity scoping is honest: full traversal (../../etc/passwd) is blocked (won't prefix-match), so exploit is contained to sibling dirs sharing the leaf prefix — data-integrity not RCE; medium is appropriate. NOT a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (zero zip/extraction findings there).

Task assignee_user_id accepted from request body with no tenant-membership validation

backend/app/main.py:618-640, 651-679 · reviewer: backend-security
What it is
`create_task` and `update_task` pass `body.assignee_user_id` straight through to `TaskStore.create`/`update` (task_store.py lines 140, 198-199), which persists it verbatim. Nothing checks that the supplied user id is a `User` belonging to the caller's tenant — unlike `logged_by_user_id`/`created_by_user_id`, which are correctly derived server-side from `principal.user_id`. An authenticated user in tenant 1 can therefore POST `{"title":"x","assignee_user_id": <id of a user in tenant 2>}` (or any arbitrary integer), creating a task that references a foreign / non-existent user id. The prompt explicitly calls for hunting cross-tenant id references like this.
Why it matters
It is an authorization gap: the assignment field is the one place a task carries a user reference, and it trusts client input across the tenant boundary. The task row itself stays in the caller's tenant (so there is no direct read of tenant 2's data), but it lets a client fabricate references to other tenants' user ids and assign work to ids that fail the `_tenant_user_names` lookup and silently render blank, corrupting the assignment/attention model.
How to fix
Before persisting, validate that `assignee_user_id` (when not None) resolves to a `User` with `tenant_id == self.tenant_id`; reject with 422/404 otherwise. Put the check in `TaskStore.create`/`update` so both routes are covered by the same chokepoint.
Expected improvement
Guarantees task assignees are always real teammates of the same tenant, eliminating the only client-controlled cross-tenant id reference in the task surface.
Evidence
create: `assignee_user_id=body.assignee_user_id,` (main.py ~633); store: `task.assignee_user_id = assignee_user_id` (task_store.py 199) — no tenant check anywhere on the path
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Confirmed in current code. main.py:632 (create_task, route at 618-640) passes body.assignee_user_id straight to TaskStore.create; main.py:668-670 (update_task, route at 651-679) forwards it to TaskStore.update. task_store.py:140 persists it verbatim; task_store.py:198-199 assigns it with no validation. Cited lines are exact. Additional fact the finding missed: models.py:205 defines assignee_user_id as a plain nullable Integer with NO ForeignKey, so even user-existence is unenforced at the DB level — any integer persists. Schemas (schemas.py:300/315) have no validators. _tenant_user_names (main.py:930-937, used at 851/891) is tenant-scoped so a foreign id misses the lookup, matching the 'renders blank' claim; created_by_user_id is correctly server-derived (main.py:636). Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (perf-only). Severity correction: medium -> low. The task row stays in the caller's tenant; nothing is reflected back about the foreign id (no existence oracle since any integer is accepted identically), so there is no cross-tenant confidentiality leak — only the caller's own tenant's assignment data can be corrupted. It is an input-validation/referential-integrity gap, not an authorization breach; medium is defensible only under the project's strict tenant-hygiene posture. Fix as proposed (tenant-membership check in TaskStore.create/update as a single chokepoint) is correct; adding a real FK to users.id would also help.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read backend/app/main.py:590-700 + 840-995, backend/app/task_store.py (full), backend/app/models.py:185-216, backend/app/attention_service.py:70-105, backend/AGENTS.md (grep), docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md (grep), and grepped all assignee_user_id references incl. tests. The finding is factually correct: create_task passes body.assignee_user_id at main.py:632 (finding said ~633), update_task forwards it at main.py:668-670, TaskStore persists verbatim (task_store.py:140, 198-199), the column has no ForeignKey (models.py:205), and no tenant-membership check or rejection test exists anywhere (test_task_store.py:117 even uses assignee_user_id=7 with no User rows). Not intentional-by-design — the model comment (models.py:204) documents the intent as "a tenant User", and nothing in AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md sanctions unvalidated link fields. Not a perf-audit duplicate (that audit is perf-only). However, severity medium is inflated: there is no cross-tenant read/write (the row stays in the caller's tenant), no name leak (_tenant_user_names at main.py:930 is tenant-filtered, foreign ids render blank), and no existence oracle (responses are identical for valid/foreign/bogus ids precisely because nothing validates). The only attacker who benefits is an authenticated user corrupting their OWN tenant's assignment data — which the same API already lets them do via delete/rename/reprioritize. Only concrete downstream effect: a task assigned to a bogus id drops out of everyone's attention feed (attention_service.py:88). The proposed fix (tenant-membership check in TaskStore.create/update as the shared chokepoint, 422 on failure) is correct and harmless. Real but low-severity input-validation/data-integrity hygiene, not a meaningful authorization gap.

PII-bearing /api/* responses get no Cache-Control — the no-store 'PII rail' covers only /data/processed/*

Caddyfile:26-28 · reviewer: data-handling
What it is
The `handle /api/*` block only `reverse_proxy`s to uvicorn and sets no Cache-Control, and the FastAPI backend never sets one either (no `Cache-Control`/`no-store` anywhere in backend/app). Yet these endpoints return the same owner PII as the gated data files plus user-entered data: `/api/leads/{id}` returns mailing_address + contact_name/phone/email + notes (main.py:996-1012), `/api/candidates` + `/api/my-leads` return owner summaries, `/api/activity` + `/api/attention` + `/api/stats` return owner names. Only `/data/processed/*` (no-store) and `/assets/*` (immutable) get explicit cache directives. ADR-064's documented Cloudflare cache-bypass rule is scoped to `/data/*`, not `/api/*`.
Why it matters
Without `no-store`, PII-bearing GET responses are eligible for browser disk caching and any intermediary cache. On a shared machine the co-founder's lead list, mailing addresses, and contact notes can persist in the browser cache after logout, and the defense-in-depth the project applied to the static composite is silently absent on the API that returns the same data.
How to fix
Add `Cache-Control: private, no-store` to the /api/* handle in Caddy (or set it as a default response header on the FastAPI app / a middleware), mirroring the /data/processed/* rail. Confirm the Cloudflare cache-bypass rule also covers /api/* as belt-and-suspenders.
Expected improvement
Eliminates client-side and intermediary caching of owner PII and user notes returned by the API, matching the no-store posture already applied to the static composite.
Evidence
handle /api/* { reverse_proxy {$BACKEND_UPSTREAM:127.0.0.1:8000} }  // no Cache-Control; main.py: mailing_address=view["mailing_address"], contact_phone=..., notes=...

Litestream replicates the PII-bearing lead-state SQLite to R2 with no documented encryption posture

litestream.yml:19-29 · reviewer: data-handling
What it is
Litestream streams `lead_state.sqlite3` to a private R2 bucket. That database holds the most sensitive user-entered PII in the system: contact attempt notes, lead notes, and the manual per-antenna owner contact details (owner_phone, owner_email, address — main.py:543-551, AntennaStore). The replica config sets bucket/endpoint/keys only; there is no client-side encryption and no S3 SSE/KMS directive. SECURITY.md §3.2 promises 'Database ... encryption at rest' and 'backups daily ... encryption at rest', and §1 lists the lead/activity log as sensitive, but neither litestream.yml nor ADR-064 row 11 documents how the R2 replica is encrypted (it relies implicitly on R2's default server-side encryption).
Why it matters
The backup target is the durability anchor for the co-founder's entire triage history (the explicit reason Litestream was chosen). A copy of all lead notes + owner contact PII lives in R2; if the R2 credentials leak or the documented at-rest promise is assumed but not actually enforced (e.g. R2 default SSE uses a provider-managed key, not a customer key), the posture in SECURITY.md is overstated and there is no client-side protection of the most sensitive data.
How to fix
Document the actual R2 at-rest encryption posture for the Litestream replica (and the restic lease-PDF target) in SECURITY.md §3.2 / ADR-064, and decide whether provider-managed SSE is sufficient or a customer-managed key is required. If client-side encryption is wanted, Litestream supports `age` encryption on the replica — add it and rotate the keys via Fly secrets.
Expected improvement
Makes the backup encryption posture explicit and verifiable, closing the gap between the SECURITY.md promise and what the deploy artifacts actually configure.
Evidence
replicas: - type: s3 bucket: ${LITESTREAM_BUCKET} ... secret-access-key: ${LITESTREAM_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY} force-path-style: true  // no encryption directive; SECURITY.md §3.2: 'Database ... encryption at rest'

Gitignored secret files bypass both deploy guards: .dockerignore has no .env pattern and require_clean_tree deliberately ignores gitignored paths

.dockerignore:1-27 · reviewer: deploy-security
What it is
The two mechanisms meant to keep secrets out of the image have a shared blind spot. .gitignore covers `.env`, `*.env`, `.env.*` (lines 9-12), but .dockerignore has no env-file patterns at all — it excludes only data/, .git, .venv, caches, node_modules, backend/users.txt, backend/tests, docs, app_puller, tests. Meanwhile deploy.sh's clean-tree guard explicitly documents that it won't see such files: '--porcelain ignores gitignored paths (data/, .venv/), so this only trips on changes that would actually reach the image' (deploy.sh lines 53-54) — which is exactly backwards for gitignored secret files, since those DO reach the image. The Dockerfile runs `COPY backend/ /srv/backend/` (line 43), so any future `backend/.env` (a completely conventional place to park AUTH_JWT_SECRET or R2 keys during local dev) ships into the production image layer silently, with no guard tripping. Today no such file exists (backend/ contains only users.txt, which is excluded) — this is a latent hole, not a live leak.
Why it matters
fly deploy ships the working tree, not git HEAD, and the team's own comment shows they reasoned about this risk — but the reasoning has a gap that turns 'gitignored' into 'invisible to every guard'. One innocuous local .env created during debugging ends up in a registry image layer.
How to fix
Add `.env`, `*.env`, `.env.*`, and `**/*.sqlite3` to .dockerignore (matching the .gitignore patterns). Optionally tighten the Dockerfile to COPY backend/app and backend/requirements.txt explicitly instead of all of backend/.
Expected improvement
A future local secrets file or stray dev database physically cannot reach the build context or the image.
Evidence
.dockerignore full contents include `backend/users.txt` and `backend/tests` but no `.env` pattern. .gitignore lines 9-12: `.env / .env.local / *.env / .env.*`. deploy.sh comment: '--porcelain ignores gitignored paths (data/, .venv/), so this only trips on changes that would actually reach the image'. Dockerfile line 43: `COPY backend/ /srv/backend/`.

Removing then re-adding a username hands the new holder the predecessor's app_user identity, settings, and historical attribution

backend/app/auth_service.py:137-155 · reviewer: identity-tenancy-lifecycle
What it is
`remove_user` only deletes the users.txt line (users.py:67-72); nothing ever deletes or tombstones the app_user row. `provision_user` find-or-creates by normalized email (auth_service.py:144 `user = self.get_user_by_email(email)`), so when a username is later re-added — possibly for a different human (a generic name like 'intern', or a handle handed to a successor) — the new person resolves to the SAME User.id. They inherit the predecessor's display_name (shown until changed via PUT /api/me), every UserSetting row (saved filter defaults, home map view), and — because attribution is stored as bare user ids (ContactAttempt.logged_by_user_id models.py:93, Task.created_by_user_id/assignee_user_id models.py:205-211) resolved to the CURRENT display name at render time (main.py:930-937) — all of the predecessor's historical attempts and tasks now display under the new person's name once they set it.
Why it matters
Historical attribution is silently rewritten: stats, History, and task audit trails attribute the predecessor's outreach to the successor, and the successor inherits a stranger's saved views. In a small trusted team this is mostly a data-integrity wart, but it grows worse with every user added, and there is no warning at `add` time that the username has a prior life in the DB.
How to fix
At minimum document the behavior next to the `add`/`remove` CLI docs. A cheap guard: since the CLI cannot see the DB, have `provision_user` log when it re-activates a User row that has existing attribution but whose username was absent from the file for some period is undetectable today — so the practical fix is a DB-side admin check or making `add_user` warn on usernames that previously existed in the file's git-ignored history; alternatively reset display_name and UserSetting rows when a username transitions back into the file (detectable by recording a `last_seen_in_file` timestamp on User during resolve_principal).
Expected improvement
Re-issuing a username stops silently transferring a predecessor's identity, preferences, and outreach history to a different human.
Evidence
user = self.get_user_by_email(email)
if user is None:
    user = User(tenant_id=tenant_id, email=_normalize_email(email))
... (users.py remove_user: 'del lines[idx]' — file line only, app_user row persists)

Login username enumeration is not actually bounded by the throttle the docstring credits

backend/app/credentials.py:119-127 · reviewer: backend-security
What it is
`authenticate` returns `UNKNOWN_USER` before calling `verify_password` and `WRONG_PASSWORD` only after a full PBKDF2 verify, and the login route maps these to distinct 401 messages ('Unknown username' vs 'Incorrect password'). The credentials.py docstring (lines 16-19) says this enumeration exposure is bounded by the #77 throttle — but the throttle key in main.py is `(ip, normalize_username(body.username))` (main.py line 216), so probing one attempt each across many distinct usernames never increments any single key and never trips the limit. The PBKDF2-only-on-existing-user path also leaks the same bit via response timing. The distinct-message UX is an intentional product choice, but the claimed mitigation does not cover the enumeration-across-usernames case.
Why it matters
An attacker from a single IP can validate which usernames exist (by message and by timing) at the rate limit of the network, undeterred by the per-username throttle, building a target list for password spraying. The code's own comment asserts a protection that isn't there, which can lull future maintainers.
How to fix
If enumeration resistance is wanted: return a single generic 401 for both unknown-user and wrong-password, and run a dummy PBKDF2 verify on the unknown-user branch to equalize timing. If the specific-error UX is deliberately kept, add a per-IP (username-independent) login counter so distinct-username probing is rate-limited, and correct the docstring to state what is actually bounded.
Expected improvement
Either removes the enumeration oracle or makes the documented throttle genuinely cap it, instead of leaving a gap the comments deny.
Evidence
if entry is None: return AuthResult(AuthOutcome.UNKNOWN_USER)  # before verify_password; key = (ip, normalize_username(body.username))
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read backend/app/credentials.py (full), backend/app/login_throttle.py (full), backend/app/main.py:198-235, and grepped auth_service.py, password.py, config.py, docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md. Every cited element is accurate in current code: (1) credentials.py:124 returns UNKNOWN_USER before verify_password; WRONG_PASSWORD at :126 only after a full PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 verify (240k default iterations), so timing also leaks user existence; (2) docstring lines 16-19 claim "the login throttle (#77) bounds that exposure" verbatim; (3) main.py:216 keys the throttle on (ip, normalize_username(username)) with max_failures default 5 (config.py:162), so one probe per distinct username never trips any key — the throttle bounds per-username password guessing, not cross-username enumeration; (4) distinct 401 messages "Unknown username" / "Incorrect password" at auth_service.py:65-68, surfaced via HTTPException(401, str(exc)) at main.py:227. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit: the only throttle item there is PERF-040 (thread-safety lock, already fixed at login_throttle.py:53); no audit finding covers enumeration. Not an excluded intentional design: the specific-error UX is intentional and the finding does not contest it — its claim is the narrower, correct one that the docstring credits a mitigation that does not cover enumeration-across-usernames. Corrections: none material; the timing-leak point is secondary since the distinct bodies already leak the same bit (timing only matters if messages are unified). Severity low is appropriate (tiny user base, strong hashing, per-username spraying still throttled); the cheap fix is correcting the docstring, optionally adding a per-IP username-independent counter.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Confirmed against current code. (1) backend/app/credentials.py:119-127 — authenticate returns UNKNOWN_USER (line 124) before verify_password; WRONG_PASSWORD only after a full PBKDF2 verify (line 125; pbkdf2_hmac confirmed in password.py), so the timing side channel exists. (2) Docstring lines 16-19 claim the #77 throttle 'bounds that exposure'. (3) backend/app/main.py:216 — throttle key is (ip, normalize_username(username)); login_throttle.py blocks only when one key reaches max_failures in-window, so one probe per distinct username from a single IP never trips any key — the throttle bounds per-username brute force, not cross-username enumeration. The docstring's claimed mitigation is therefore materially inaccurate for the enumeration case. Corrections/nuances: the distinct 401 messages ('Unknown username' / 'Incorrect password') are raised in backend/app/auth_service.py:65-68 (login_user), not mapped in the route itself — the route passes str(exc) through (main.py:227); functionally as described. The specific-error UX is intentional and ADR-documented (ADR-062, reaffirmed ADR-063), but the finding correctly targets only the false mitigation claim, which no ADR corrects. Partial out-of-repo mitigation: docs/DEPLOYMENT.md:207 credits the Cloudflare perimeter with 'login rate-limiting' and the runbook (line 304) lists an OPTIONAL rate-limit on /api/auth/login — unverifiable from the repo and, even if configured, it (not #77) would be the real bound, so the docstring is still wrong. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (PERF-040 touched the throttle's lock only). Proposed fix is sound; the second branch (keep specific-error UX, add a per-IP username-independent counter, correct the docstring) is the one consistent with ADR-062. Severity low is appropriate: tiny user population (a handful of users in users.txt), enumeration is a target-list builder, app is internet-facing but per-username brute force is still throttled.
lowConfirmed backend effort: trivial

Self-service password change accepts any non-empty new password (no length/strength floor)

backend/app/main.py:314-333 · reviewer: backend-security
What it is
`POST /api/me/password` routes `body.new_password` to `users.change_password` → `set_password`, which only rejects an empty string ('password must not be empty', users.py line 83). The `PasswordChange` schema (schemas.py 257-261) declares `new_password: str` with no `min_length` or any constraint. A user can therefore rotate their credential to a 1-character password. Since `users.txt` is the sole credential source guarding the entire app and is not Litestream-replicated, this is the credential the whole gate depends on.
Why it matters
A trivially weak password materially lowers the value of the login throttle and PBKDF2 hashing — the front door can be set to a one-character secret with no warning. There is also no rate limit on this endpoint (it runs a 240k-iteration PBKDF2 verify per call), though that requires an already-valid session.
How to fix
Add a minimum-length (and optionally complexity) constraint to `PasswordChange.new_password` via Pydantic `Field(min_length=...)`, and enforce the same floor in `users.set_password`/`change_password` so the CLI path is covered too.
Expected improvement
Prevents the sole authentication credential from being weakened to a guessable value, with a one-line schema constraint.
Evidence
schemas.py: `new_password: str`; users.py set_password: `if not password: raise UsersFileError("password must not be empty")` — empty is the only rejected case
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read backend/app/main.py:314-333, backend/app/schemas.py:257-261, backend/app/users.py:80-110, and the frontend (app/src/components/SettingsAccount.svelte, app/src/lib/leads-api.ts:389). All citations are accurate as of current code. (1) main.py:314 is `@app.post("/api/me/password", status_code=204)`; the handler (314-333, exactly as cited) passes `body.new_password` straight to `users_change_password`. (2) schemas.py:257-261 `PasswordChange` declares `new_password: str` with no Field constraint. (3) users.py `set_password` rejects only the empty string (`if not password: raise UsersFileError("password must not be empty")`, line ~82-83); `change_password` verifies the current password then delegates to `set_password` with no further check. (4) A repo-wide grep for min_length/strength/len(password) in backend/app finds nothing outside tests; the frontend's only gate is `disabled={... || !current || !next}` (SettingsAccount.svelte:144), i.e. non-empty client-side too. So a 1-character password is accepted end-to-end. Supporting context claims also check out: no per-route throttle on this endpoint (the login throttle applies only to /api/auth/login), though it requires an already-authenticated principal, which correctly bounds the severity. This is a security finding, not in the 2026-06-07 perf audit's 42 items. Minor caveat: the "users.txt is not Litestream-replicated" assertion in the description is a docs/ops claim I did not independently verify, but it is not load-bearing for existence. Suggested fix in the finding (Pydantic `Field(min_length=...)` plus the same floor in `users.set_password` so the CLI path is covered) is apt; severity "low" is appropriate for an authenticated self-service path on a 2-person tenant, albeit on a production-exposed front door.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read backend/app/main.py:314-333, backend/app/schemas.py:257-261, backend/app/users.py:45-135, backend/app/password.py, app/src/components/SettingsAccount.svelte:26-60, and docs/adr/0068-self-service-password-change.md. Every factual claim checks out: (1) PasswordChange declares `new_password: str` with no Field constraint (schemas.py 257-261, exact lines as cited); (2) the endpoint routes to users.change_password -> set_password, whose only rejection is empty string ("password must not be empty", users.py line 83 — line number correct); (3) the frontend form (SettingsAccount.svelte changePassword) also checks only non-empty + confirm-match, so there is no floor at any layer; (4) PBKDF2 240k iterations confirmed (password.py DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 240_000); (5) the LoginThrottle is wired only to /api/auth/login — /api/me/password has no throttle, though as the finding itself concedes it requires a valid session (current_principal dependency), so the PBKDF2-per-call point is marginal; (6) the not-Litestream-replicated claim is confirmed verbatim by ADR-068's prod caveat. Refutation attempts failed: ADR-068 documents only the empty-string rejection as the policy ("An empty new password ... raises UsersFileError") — there is no documented decision to omit a strength floor, so this is not intentional-by-design; not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (security, not perf); no test asserts a length floor (test_change_password.py covers only empty/wrong-current/unknown-user). Mitigation that keeps severity at low rather than raising it: the login throttle does bound online brute force against a weak password, the app is a 2-user product, and the weak password must be chosen by the legitimate user themselves. One correction to the fix: enforcing the floor in set_password covers the `set-password` CLI subcommand and the endpoint, but `add_user` (users.py 56-57) has its own independent empty-check — to cover the full CLI surface the floor should be applied there too (or in a shared `_valid_password` helper, matching the existing _valid_username/_valid_team pattern). Severity low is appropriate; the fix as proposed is correct and non-harmful.
lowUnverified deploy effort: smallfound independently by 2 reviewers

Entire container process tree runs as root and base images use mutable tags

Dockerfile:16-61 · reviewer: deploy-security
What it is
There is no USER directive in the Dockerfile and supervisord.conf sets `user=root` with no per-program `user=` overrides, so uvicorn (parsing untrusted HTTP input), Caddy, and Litestream all run as root with write access to the volume holding users.txt and the SQLite DB. Caddy in particular is designed to drop privileges and the official image runs it unprivileged. Separately, all five base images are referenced by mutable tags (node:22-slim, python:3.11-slim, caddy:2.11, litestream/litestream:0.5.11, rclone/rclone:1.74) rather than digests — the comment says 'pinned to the versions the repo's tests were written against', but a tag can be re-pushed, so two deploys of the same commit can produce different binaries.
Why it matters
Fly machines are isolated microVMs so the blast radius is the VM, not the host — but a compromise of any of the three internet-facing processes currently yields root over the credentials file and the write-layer DB inside that VM. Mutable tags make builds non-reproducible and silently absorb upstream changes.
How to fix
Create an unprivileged user in the runtime stage, chown /srv and grant it write access to /data, and set `user=` per supervisord program (Caddy binds :8080, no privileged port needed). Pin base images by digest (`python:3.11-slim@sha256:...`).
Expected improvement
Process compromise no longer implies root over credentials and the DB; image builds become reproducible.
Evidence
Dockerfile: no USER directive; `CMD ["supervisord", "-c", "/etc/supervisord.conf"]`. supervisord.conf line 13: `user=root`. Dockerfile lines 29-31: `COPY --from=caddy:2.11 ... COPY --from=litestream/litestream:0.5.11 ... COPY --from=rclone/rclone:1.74 ...`.
lowUnverified fetch effort: trivial

regrid_parcels builds local paths directly from S3 keys — no traversal guard and a crash when an object key equals the prefix

scripts/fetch/sources/regrid_parcels.py:127-137 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
For each listed object, `rel = key[len(prefix):].lstrip("/"); target = dest_dir / rel` then `s3.download_file(..., str(target))`. Two issues: (1) S3 keys may contain `..` segments (keys are arbitrary strings); since nothing normalizes or validates `rel`, a key like `<prefix>/../../evil` writes outside the .partial dir via OS-level `..` resolution — the only fetch path in the package with zero traversal protection, in a layer that otherwise routes everything through safe_extract_zip. (2) If the prefix itself exists as a zero-byte object (common S3 'folder marker'), `rel` becomes "" and target equals dest_dir, so download_file fails against a directory and the whole mirror aborts.
Why it matters
Trust boundary is a paid vendor's bucket, so exploitation requires a compromised or misbehaving Regrid delivery — low likelihood, but the cost of the guard is two lines, and the folder-marker crash is a plain operational bug that would surface on the first real delivery if Regrid uses console-created folders.
How to fix
Skip empty rels (`if not rel: continue`) and reject escapes before downloading: `target = (dest_dir / rel).resolve(); if not target.is_relative_to(dest_dir.resolve()): raise ValueError(f"unsafe S3 key: {key}")` (mirroring the fixed safe_extract_zip check).
Expected improvement
The one unguarded write path matches the package's traversal posture, and a Regrid folder marker no longer aborts the mirror.
Evidence
regrid_parcels.py:129-131 `rel = key[len(prefix) :].lstrip("/")\n            target = dest_dir / rel\n            target.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)` — no validation of rel

duckdb.ts exposes the query engine as window.__duckDb in production builds with a stale 'local single-user app' justification

app/src/lib/duckdb.ts:210-214 · reviewer: frontend-security
What it is
The module unconditionally attaches the live DuckDB engine to `window.__duckDb` so E2E tests can drive it, with the comment "Harmless in a local single-user app." The app has since shipped to production at morphyconsulting.com (PRD #96). This is not a new privilege boundary — any same-origin script can already issue the same fetches — but it is a polished, documented handle for querying the PII parquet from the console, and the justification comment no longer reflects the deployment model.
Why it matters
Mostly hygiene: it slightly lowers the bar for anyone with console access (or a future XSS payload author) to bulk-query owner data, and the stale comment will mislead a future reviewer into thinking the app is still local-only.
How to fix
Gate the assignment on dev/test: `if (import.meta.env.DEV || import.meta.env.MODE === 'test')` (the Playwright suite runs against `vite preview` — if it needs the handle there, gate on a `VITE_E2E` env flag set only in playwright.config.ts instead). Update the comment either way.
Expected improvement
Production bundles stop shipping a console-ready PII query handle; the code comment matches the actual deployment posture.
Evidence
duckdb.ts: `// Test handle — lets E2E exercise the query engine directly. Harmless in\n// a local single-user app.\nif (typeof window !== 'undefined') {\n  (window as unknown as { __duckDb?: DuckDbEngine }).__duckDb = duckDb;\n}`

Correctness bugs (41)

highConfirmed backend effort: small

Attention feed groups replies by raw cluster id while dismiss writes to the merge-resolved id — un-clearable attention item after an owner merge

backend/app/attention_service.py:58-80 (plus backend/app/main.py 722-738) · reviewer: backend-bugs
What it is
AttentionService.unhandled_replies() walks the tenant attempt stream newest-first and treats 'the first row per display_cluster_id' as each Lead's latest activity, using the RAW cid stored on the attempt row (`cid = a.display_cluster_id`). Owner merges are never applied. After a merge, all new activity is written to the resolved target (log_attempt does `target = merges.resolve(display_cluster_id)`), so a reply logged on the source cluster BEFORE the merge stays the newest row under the source cid forever. The dismiss route makes it worse: `store.append_attempt(merges.resolve(body.cluster_id), channel=DISMISS_CHANNEL, ...)` resolves the attention item's source cid to the target, so the dismiss row lands on the target — the source cid's newest row remains the inbound reply and the item re-appears on every load. There is no API path that can append activity to a merged-away cid, so the item is permanently stuck (only splitting and re-merging clears it). The natural trigger order is common: get a reply, realize the two clusters are the same person, merge them.
Why it matters
The attention feed doubles as the nav badge ('the feed's length is the nav badge count'). One pre-merge reply produces a phantom, undismissable notification that survives every dismiss click and every newer touch, eroding trust in the feed for the co-founder whose daily-driver usage is the Phase-1 outcome bar.
How to fix
Resolve attempt cids through the tenant's merge map before grouping: pass the request's OwnerMergeService (already constructed in the /api/attention route) into AttentionService and key `seen` on `merges.resolve(a.display_cluster_id)`; emit the resolved cid on the item so dismiss targets the same id the grouping used. Same one-line resolve also fixes /api/activity's `lead=` filter missing merged-member rows (LeadStore.tenant_attempts filters raw equality, lead_store.py:73-74).
Expected improvement
Dismiss and the 'newer activity clears it' rule work for merged owners; no permanently stuck badge items; one merged Lead can no longer surface multiple duplicate reply items (one per member cid).
Evidence
attention_service.py: `cid = a.display_cluster_id` ... `seen.add(cid)  # only consider each Lead's latest activity row` / main.py dismiss: `store.append_attempt(\n        merges.resolve(body.cluster_id),\n        channel=DISMISS_CHANNEL,` vs main.py log_attempt: `target = merges.resolve(display_cluster_id)` before `store.append_attempt(target, ...)`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/backend/app/attention_service.py (whole file), backend/app/main.py lines 380-540 and 680-760 plus the /api/activity route (875-905), backend/app/lead_store.py (whole file), and grepped owner_merge_service.py / activity_service.py / all ContactAttempt writers. Every cited piece of evidence is real and in context, current as of main (post-c47e184): (1) AttentionService.unhandled_replies (attention_service.py:58-80) groups the newest-first tenant attempt stream by the RAW `a.display_cluster_id` (lines 66-69) — the class never receives an OwnerMergeService and no resolution happens anywhere in the service or in the /api/attention route (main.py:689-719; only owner_name_of resolves, for display names). (2) The dismiss route (main.py:722-738) appends the dismiss row at `merges.resolve(body.cluster_id)` (line 733), so after a merge S→T, dismissing the item whose cluster_id=S lands the clearing row on T while the grouping key for the stuck item remains S. (3) Every runtime writer of ContactAttempt rows resolves first: update_lead (main.py:394), log_attempt (main.py:441-442), pursue (main.py:483), dismiss (main.py:733); lead_store's _log_status_change/append_attempt are only ever invoked with those resolved targets. AttentionService.dismiss (attention_service.py:110) would also write unresolved but is dead code — the route appends directly. So the claim "no API path can append activity to a merged-away cid" holds, and the pre-merge inbound reply stays the newest row under the source cid permanently; the item re-emerges on every feed load and inflates the nav badge count (count=len(items), main.py:705-707; "feed's length is the nav badge count" per the module docstring). (4) The secondary claim is also correct: LeadStore.tenant_attempts filters `display_cluster_id == lead` by raw equality (lead_store.py:73-74) and /api/activity passes the lead query param through unresolved (main.py:901-902 via ActivityService.events → tenant_attempts), so a merged owner's member rows are invisible to the lead= filter. Corrections: minor only. The 2026-06-07 perf audit touches /api/attention solely for performance (PERF-008/009/022: per-row effective() rebuilds, SQL-pushdown filters) — it does not cover this merge-resolution correctness bug, so it is not a duplicate. One wording nit: the finding says "only splitting and re-merging clears it"; precisely, the sequence is split S out, then dismiss/log on S (which now resolves to itself), then optionally re-merge — split alone doesn't clear it. Severity high is fair: phantom undismissable badge item on the Dashboard, the daily-driver surface for the Phase-1 outcome bar, triggered by the natural reply-then-merge order; mitigated only by merges being an occasional manual action. The proposed fix (resolve cids through the request's OwnerMergeService before grouping and emit the resolved cid) is correct and also matches PERF-009's suggestion to pass by_id/merges into the route-local closures.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Confirmed by reading backend/app/attention_service.py (lines 58-80), backend/app/main.py (attention route 689-719, dismiss route 722-738, log_attempt 419-465, merge/split 492-535), backend/app/owner_merge_service.py, backend/app/lead_store.py (tenant_attempts 54-86, raw-equality lead filter at lines 73-74 as cited), app/src/lib/dashboard-store.svelte.ts + Dashboard.svelte, and the test suites. Severity high is fair: the feed length is the nav badge, the only escape hatch is the obscure split-then-re-merge dance, and the trigger sequence (reply arrives → user realizes duplicate identity → merges) is natural for the manual-merge feature. Two corrections: (a) the trigger requires the reply to be UNHANDLED at merge time (the source cid's newest row); if any newer activity was logged on the source before the merge there is no phantom — slightly narrower than the description implies, but the described order is the natural one; (b) the how_to_fix's claim that the 'same one-line resolve also fixes /api/activity's lead= filter' is wrong as stated — that is a separate, real gap (main.py:902 passes the raw lead param into the SQL equality filter at lead_store.py:73-74), but fixing it requires expanding lead → merges.members_of(lead) into an IN filter (like list_attempts_for), not the AttentionService change. The primary proposed fix (resolve cids when grouping in unhandled_replies and emit the resolved cid on the item) is correct and sufficient: the dismiss row on the target then becomes the merged group's newest row.

FilterPanel.pickAsr sets location.hash = '' which routes to the Dashboard, hiding the map it just flew

app/src/components/FilterPanel.svelte:137-143 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
Picking a tower from the ASR search popover runs `location.hash = ''` (comment: "jump to the map") and then `app.flyTo(...)`. But parseRoute('') in app/src/lib/nav.ts returns `{ name: 'dashboard', sub: 'home' }` (the explicit fallback so the app opens on the Dashboard), so the hashchange flips `section` to 'dashboard' and App.svelte renders the opaque `.surface` Dashboard panel over the still-mounted map. The map flies to the tower while completely hidden behind the Dashboard. The ASR search is only reachable from Explore (the sidebar is covered by the surface everywhere else), so this fires on every ASR pick. The line almost certainly predates the #104 routing overhaul, when an empty hash still meant 'the map'.
Why it matters
A core Explore workflow (search a tower by ASR, jump to it) yanks the user to the Dashboard instead. They have to click back to Explore, and by then the fly animation has finished invisible — the feature appears broken.
How to fix
Replace `location.hash = ''` with `location.hash = SECTION_HASH.explore` (already imported in sibling code paths), and consider also resetting the Explore lens to 'map' so a fly from list view is visible (e.g. via a small store flag the App lens toggle reads, since exploreView is App-local state).
Expected improvement
ASR search hits actually land the user on the map at the tower, as the comment intends.
Evidence
function pickAsr(f: TowerFeature): void {
  searchOpen = false;
  term = `ASR ${f.properties.asr}`;
  ...
  location.hash = ''; // jump to the map
  app.flyTo([lon, lat], 13);
}
— and nav.ts: `return { name: 'dashboard', sub: 'home' };` for an empty/unknown hash.

MapView's anchor fitBounds effect clobbers the restored per-user camera (pinned home / last view) and then persists the clobbered view

app/src/components/MapView.svelte:189-198, 623-627, 147-150 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
Two $effects fight over the camera. The one-shot restore effect (lines 190-198) runs first in creation order and `jumpTo`s the user's pinned-home / last view once `userSettings.loaded` is true. The framing effect (lines 623-627) then runs — every $effect runs once at mount — and calls `map.fitBounds(anchors, { padding: 28, duration: ready ? 700 : 0 })`, re-framing to the anchor-union bounds and overwriting the restored camera. Worse, because `ready` ($state) is read for the duration argument, the framing effect re-runs again when `ready` flips on map 'load', and again if `app.anchorBounds` resolves late — so even when the restore effect happens to run last, a later re-run clobbers it. Finally the `moveend` handler (lines 147-150) writes the post-fitBounds camera into the last-view cache, so the user's saved "where I left off" position is destroyed every session. The fitBounds at mount is also redundant: the Map constructor already received `bounds: app.anchorBounds ?? US_BOUNDS`.
Why it matters
The PRD #121 "default map view" feature (pinned home / where-I-left-off) is effectively non-functional in the common warm path: every map mount snaps back to the all-anchors framing and then overwrites the cached last view with it. The milestone log notes this wiring was left to (currently stale) e2e tests, so nothing catches it.
How to fix
Make the framing effect a true default-only fallback: hoist `const dur = untrack(() => ready) ? 700 : 0` (or read ready via untrack) so `ready` is not a dependency; skip fitBounds entirely once `initialViewApplied` resolved to a user view (e.g. share a `userViewApplied` flag); and don't fitBounds at mount when the constructor already framed the same bounds.
Expected improvement
Pinned home and last-view restore actually take effect on map open, and the last-view cache stops being overwritten by the default framing.
Evidence
$effect(() => {
  if (!map || initialViewApplied || !userSettings.loaded) return;
  initialViewApplied = true;
  ...
  if (view) map.jumpTo({ center: [view.lng, view.lat], zoom: view.zoom });
});
...
$effect(() => {
  const anchors = app.anchorBounds;
  if (!map || !anchors) return;
  map.fitBounds(anchors, { padding: 28, duration: ready ? 700 : 0 });

leads-store.loadLead/saveLead have no request sequencing — out-of-order responses render (and can save) the wrong lead

app/src/lib/leads-store.svelte.ts:124-136, 147, 167 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
LeadPage re-runs `void leads.loadLead(displayClusterId)` on every routed-key change (LeadPage.svelte:22-24), but the store assigns `this.lead = await fetchLead(...)` with no check that the response still matches the current route. Navigating lead A → lead B while A's (slower) fetch is in flight ends with `this.lead = A` after B already rendered: the page shows owner A's data under URL #/lead/B. The same unguarded `this.lead = await ...` assignment exists in saveLead (line 147), addAttempt (167) and #override (210) — a save fired just before navigating away lands the OLD lead's full detail into the store after the new lead loaded. The damage compounds through LeadPage's draft-seeding effect (LeadPage.svelte:68-80): when stale A arrives, `noteDraft`/`contactDraft` re-seed from A while `displayClusterId` is still B, so clicking "Save note" calls `leads.saveLead(B, { notes: <A's notes> })` — silently overwriting lead B's notes/contact with lead A's content.
Why it matters
Beyond showing the wrong owner's data under a given URL, this is a real data-corruption pathway: one save click can copy one lead's notes/contact fields onto another lead, with no error and no undo.
How to fix
Add a sequence guard: store the requested key (`this.leadKey = displayClusterId`) or a monotonically increasing request id when loadLead starts, and discard any resolution whose key/id no longer matches; apply the same guard to the saveLead/addAttempt/#override assignments (only assign `this.lead` if `result.owner.display_cluster_id` matches the currently routed/loaded key, or re-key the store by cluster id).
Expected improvement
Rapid lead-to-lead navigation can never display or persist one owner's data under another owner's key.
Evidence
async loadLead(displayClusterId: string): Promise<void> {
  this.leadStatus = 'loading';
  ...
  this.lead = await fetchLead(displayClusterId);  // no staleness check
}
// LeadPage.svelte: $effect(() => { void leads.loadLead(displayClusterId); });
// and the draft seeding: if (l && l.owner.display_cluster_id !== loadedFor) { noteDraft = l.notes; ... }
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Confirmed against current code. leads-store.svelte.ts: loadLead (lines 124-136) assigns `this.lead = await fetchLead(displayClusterId)` with no staleness/sequence guard; identical unguarded assignments in saveLead (line 147), addAttempt (line 167), #override (line 210) — all line cites exact. The finding slightly understates scope: `pursue` (line 184) has the same unguarded `this.lead = await pursueOwner(...)` pattern and should get the same guard. LeadPage.svelte: the reload $effect is at lines 22-24 as cited; the draft-seeding effect is at lines 68-80 (loadedFor declared line 67) and keys re-seeding on `l.owner.display_cluster_id !== loadedFor`, so a stale lead A landing while routed to B re-seeds noteDraft/contactDraft from A; saveNote() (lines 145-147) then calls leads.saveLead(displayClusterId /* = routed B */, { notes: noteDraft /* A's */ }) — the cross-lead notes/contact corruption pathway is real. leads-api.ts has no AbortController/cancellation, so nothing mitigates the race. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (grep of docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md found nothing on loadLead sequencing; that audit is perf-only). Mitigant on likelihood only: triggering requires out-of-order responses (fast successive navigation or a save racing a navigation), and the rendered header shows the stale owner's name, which an attentive user might notice — but the save path corrupts silently, so high severity stands. Suggested fix in the finding (request key/id guard, or compare result.owner.display_cluster_id to the routed key before assigning) is correct.
