Dashboard digest loads are gated on the mount-time `sub` prop, so navigating back to the hub from a deep-linked sub-page leaves digests unloaded

app/src/components/Dashboard.svelte:21-27 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
Dashboard's onMount only loads the attention feed, tasks, and activity when `sub === 'home'` at mount time. The component instance persists while the hash moves between #/dashboard and #/dashboard/<sub> (route.name stays 'dashboard'; only the prop changes), and onMount never re-runs. Entering via a deep link to #/dashboard/tasks (or /calendar etc.) and then clicking "‹ Dashboard" lands on the hub with `activity` never loaded — the Calendar digest shows "Last contact: —" / "Last reply: —" indefinitely (attention happens to be covered by the App-level sign-in effect, and tasks by the Tasks sub-page's own onMount).
Why it matters
The hub silently shows empty/placeholder digests on a perfectly normal navigation path (open a bookmarked sub-page, go back to the hub), making the data look missing.
How to fix
Replace the onMount gate with `$effect(() => { if (sub === 'home') { void dashboard.loadAttention(); void tasks.load(); void activity.load(); } })` so reaching the hub always (re)loads its digests.
Expected improvement
Hub digests are populated regardless of which dashboard URL the session entered through.
Evidence
onMount(() => {
  if (sub === 'home') {
    void dashboard.loadAttention();
    void tasks.load();
    void activity.load(); // for the Calendar digest (last contact / reply)
  }
});

TowerDetail never clears antError on a new antenna load or successful fetch — the previous tower's error keeps rendering

app/src/components/TowerDetail.svelte:66-87, 263 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
The per-antenna load effect resets `antStatus`/`saved` when `asr` changes but not `antError`. Its success path sets `antStatus = 'idle'` and also leaves `antError` untouched. Since the TowerDetail component instance is reused across tower routes (only the props change), navigating from a tower whose fetchAntenna failed to one that loads fine still renders the stale `{#if antError}` message under the Manual details card. (The effect's `if (a !== asr)` staleness guard for the fetch itself is done correctly — this is just the error field.)
Why it matters
A user browsing tower pages after one transient fetch failure sees a persistent, misleading error on every subsequent tower until a save or remount clears it.
How to fix
Set `antError = null` at the top of the load effect (alongside `antStatus = 'loading'; saved = false;`) and/or in the success branch.
Expected improvement
Error messaging always refers to the antenna actually on screen.
Evidence
$effect(() => {
  const a = asr;
  antStatus = 'loading';
  saved = false;          // antError is NOT reset
  fetchAntenna(a)
    .then((res) => { ...; antStatus = 'idle'; })   // still no antError = null
...
{#if antError}<p class="err" data-testid="manual-error">{antError}</p>{/if}

LinkedTasks effect has no out-of-order guard and swallows every fetch error into an empty list

app/src/components/LinkedTasks.svelte:13-25 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
The effect refetches whenever the `query` prop changes (it changes on every lead-to-lead or tower-to-tower navigation since LeadPage/TowerDetail instances are reused), but unlike TowerDetail's antenna effect there is no `if (q !== query) return` staleness check — a slower older response can overwrite `items` with the previous owner's/tower's tasks after the newer response rendered. The `.catch(() => { items = []; })` also converts every failure (including 401s, which the leads/tasks stores route to auth.signOut) into a confident "No linked tasks" empty state.
Why it matters
Task context shown on a Lead/Tower page can belong to the previously viewed entity, and genuine API failures masquerade as "no linked tasks", which misleads triage.
How to fix
Capture `const q = query` at effect start and discard resolutions/rejections where `q !== query` (same pattern as TowerDetail.svelte:72); distinguish an error state from an empty result, and route ApiError 401 through the shared handle()/auth.signOut path.
Expected improvement
Linked-task lists always match the entity on screen and failures are visible instead of disguised as emptiness.
Evidence
$effect(() => {
  loaded = false;
  void fetchTasks(query)
    .then((r) => { items = r.tasks; })
    .catch(() => { items = []; })
    .finally(() => { loaded = true; });
});

(4) 401 claim accurate: fetchTasks (tasks-api.ts:56) goes through the shared `request()` in leads-api.ts which throws ApiError carrying res.status (lines 234, 244); both leads-store.svelte.ts:27-28 and tasks-store.svelte.ts:23-24 define `handle()` that calls `auth.signOut()` on ApiError 401 — LinkedTasks bypasses that path, so a 401 here silently shows an empty list instead of signing out (though any other request via the stores would still trigger sign-out, so the practical 401 window is narrow). MiniMap snapshots holdings in onMount, so a merge/split leaves the Lead page footprint map stale

app/src/components/MiniMap.svelte:46-49 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
MiniMap builds its footprint FeatureCollection once from the `holdings` prop inside onMount and never reacts to prop updates. That is safe for lead-to-lead navigation because loadLead nulls `leads.lead` first (the `{#if leads.lead}` block unmounts and remounts MiniMap), but the merge/split path (`LeadsStore.#override`, leads-store.svelte.ts:206-219) and saveLead assign `this.lead = await call()` directly without the null round-trip — the Lead page keeps the same MiniMap instance while `l.holdings` changes underneath it. After merging another owner in (which adds their parcels to holdings, the whole point of the action), the footprint map still shows the pre-merge parcels; the holdings list right below it updates, so the two visibly disagree.
Why it matters
Merge/split is the manual data-correction workflow; the map contradicting the holdings list right after a merge makes the correction look like it failed.
How to fix
Either react to prop changes in MiniMap (rebuild/setData the 'footprint' source in a $effect keyed on `holdings`), or key the component at the call site (`{#key l.holdings} <MiniMap .../> {/key}` or key on merged_members length) so it remounts when the footprint changes.
Expected improvement
The footprint map reflects the post-merge/post-split holdings immediately.
Evidence
onMount(() => {
  const fc = footprint(holdings);   // one-shot snapshot of the prop
  if (fc.features.length === 0) return;

uls_to_asr spatial fallback attaches one ULS location to multiple towers on exact distance ties, violating the documented one-tower contract

scripts/preprocess/joins/uls_to_asr.py:211-216 · reviewer: preprocess-core
What it is
The module docstring promises "Each ULS ``location`` row is attached to exactly one tower", but gpd.sjoin_nearest returns every equidistant right-hand match. FCC ASR data contains co-located registrations (two registration numbers at identical coordinates — the same drift the #11 calibration audited), and for those the fallback emits one match row per tied tower, attaching the same carrier entry to both. The verified path is protected (asr_key drop_duplicates keeps one tower per ASR number) but the spatial path is not.
Why it matters
A small precision leak in a join that was deliberately tuned high-precision/low-recall: a co-located duplicate tower 'steals' a copy of the carrier instead of the match being unique, slightly inflating coverage_pct and showing the same likely/possible carrier on two map structures. Rare, but it contradicts the contract the rest of the file is built around.
How to fix
After sjoin_nearest, dedupe to one tower per location — e.g. `nearest = nearest.sort_values("_dist_m").groupby(level=0).head(1)` (or sort by (_dist_m, __tidx) for determinism) before building entries.
Expected improvement
The one-location→one-tower invariant holds in all paths; coverage stats stop double-counting ties.
Evidence
Docstring: "Each ULS ``location`` row is attached to exactly one tower"; code: `nearest = gpd.sjoin_nearest(left, right, how="inner", max_distance=POSSIBLE_M, distance_col="_dist_m")\n            for rec in nearest.to_dict("records"): ... matches.append(...)` — no tie-break dedup per left row
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/scripts/preprocess/joins/uls_to_asr.py in full, /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/tests/test_uls_to_asr.py (test list), docs/audits/uls_asr_number_coverage.md (tie references), and docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md. CONFIRMED EMPIRICALLY: reproduced the exact code path with the installed geopandas 1.1.3 — one left location vs two right towers at identical coordinates yields 2 rows from gpd.sjoin_nearest(how="inner", max_distance=500, distance_col="_dist_m") with the same left index and both __tidx values, so the loop at lines 214-216 appends one carrier entry per tied tower. The docstring contract (lines 19-20: "Each ULS ``location`` row is attached to exactly one tower") is real and the verified path does enforce it (asr_key drop_duplicates on _asr, line 179), so the spatial path's tie behavior is an oversight, not intentional. No mitigation exists: the step-3 dedup (lines 218-228) is per-tower by (carrier_name, callsign), so it cannot collapse the cross-tower duplicate; no test covers equidistant towers (test list has likely/possible/refused distance tests but no tie test); the left index is unique (locs gets a fresh RangeIndex from the merge at line 162), so the proposed groupby(level=0).head(1) fix is sound — the deterministic variant sorting by (_dist_m, __tidx) is the better form since _dist_m is equal within a tie group. NOT a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit: the audit's coverage note explicitly lists "uls_to_asr.py beyond line 130 (the EPSG:5070 spatial fallback matcher)" as NOT exhaustively read, and this is a correctness issue, not perf. Materiality caveats (severity stays low): I could not quantify against real data (data/normalized/fcc_asr does not exist on this machine), so the frequency of bit-identical projected coordinates among ASR towers is unverified here — exact float ties effectively require identical source lat/lon (plausible for co-located ASR registrations whose DMS-derived coordinates match exactly, but unquantified); and when it fires, the two affected towers are at the same physical point, so the user-visible harm (same carrier shown on two co-located map structures, slight coverage_pct inflation in summary.json) is small. Line citation 211-216 is accurate; evidence quote matches the file verbatim.

usda_persistent_poverty: comment says "latest year wins" but the code ORs the flag across every matching attribute/year row

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/usda_persistent_poverty.py:62-78 · reviewer: preprocess-normalizers
What it is
The filter comment reads `# Filter to persistent-poverty rows (latest year wins).` but the implementation is `grouped = pp.groupby("fips_canon")["val_bool"].any()` — a logical OR across every row whose Attribute matches the `persistent.poverty` regex, with no year ordering at all. If the ERS CSV carries more than one edition/vintage of the flag (the long-form file is one row per FIPS/Attribute/Year), a county that exited persistent poverty in the latest edition stays flagged True from the older row. Separately, `grouped[grouped["fips_canon"].notna()]` silently drops counties whose FIPS failed to parse, with no warning.
Why it matters
persistent_poverty feeds the parcel distress overlay and lead scoring; over-flagging counties from stale vintages overstates distress, and the comment actively misleads the next maintainer into believing recency is handled.
How to fix
If a year/edition column exists in the CSV, select the max-year row per county before reducing (and make the reduction `last` rather than `any`); if only one vintage is ever present, fix the comment to say so and assert that the matched Attribute values are a single label. Add a quality-report warning for FIPS-parse failures as in the ARC finding.
Expected improvement
The persistent-poverty flag reflects the latest ERS edition (or the single-vintage assumption is made explicit and enforced), and FIPS-parse failures become visible.
Evidence
Line 62 `# Filter to persistent-poverty rows (latest year wins).` vs line 77 `grouped = pp.groupby("fips_canon")["val_bool"].any().reset_index()`; line 78 `grouped = grouped[grouped["fips_canon"].notna()]`
Verifier notes (2)

tower-list.ts: countMatchingTowers and rankedTowerRows use different de-dup key separators despite a comment asserting they match

app/src/lib/tower-list.ts:45-57, 77 · reviewer: frontend-lib-bugs
What it is
countMatchingTowers() builds its de-dup key as `${p.cid}\0${p.asr}` and its doc comment states "The `\0` de-dup separator matches `rankedTowerRows` so the count and the list agree" — but rankedTowerRows() (added/kept with a plain-space key) uses `${p.cid} ${p.asr}`. Today this is harmless because `cid` is a 12-hex-char hash (never contains a space or NUL), so both keys partition identically. But the stated parity contract is false in the code, and the comment will mislead the next edit: if either keying scheme changes (e.g. cid becomes a raw owner name for unclusterable towers), the sidebar Towers tile count and the ranked list length can silently diverge — exactly the drift the shared derivation (explore-towers.svelte.ts) was extracted to prevent.
Why it matters
The count tile and the list are documented as 'the number a user reads is the number they act on'. A latent keying divergence guarded only by an incorrect comment is a textbook future-drift trap.
How to fix
Extract one `towerKey(p: TowerProps): string` helper (use the `\0` form) and call it from both functions; fix the comment.
Expected improvement
The count/list parity becomes structural instead of coincidental, and the comment stops asserting something untrue.
Evidence
countMatchingTowers: seen.add(`${p.cid}\0${p.asr}`);  // comment: "The `\0` de-dup separator matches `rankedTowerRows`"
rankedTowerRows:    const key = `${p.cid} ${p.asr}`;
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/app/src/lib/tower-list.ts in full. The finding is accurate in every particular: (1) countMatchingTowers builds its de-dup key as `${p.cid}\0${p.asr}` (line 57) and its doc comment (lines 44-45) asserts "The `\0` de-dup separator matches `rankedTowerRows` so the count and the list agree"; (2) rankedTowerRows uses `const key = `${p.cid} ${p.asr}`` (line 77, plain space). The comment's parity claim is false. Cited line ranges are correct. Also verified the "harmless today" qualifier: cid comes from ownerNameToClusterId → displayClusterId in app/src/lib/owner-cluster.ts:42-45, which returns either '' (unclusterable) or the first 12 hex chars of sha1 — never a space or NUL — so both keys partition (cid, asr) pairs identically right now; the issue is purely a latent-drift trap guarded by a wrong comment. Duplicate check against docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md: this is NOT one of the 42 audit findings — PERF-007 is the audit item whose execution (commit c47e184) CREATED countMatchingTowers; this finding is a defect in that execution. Notably, the PERF-007 work order said to key the count with cid+' '+asr (space) and to "Factor the keep-predicate so rows and count can't diverge" — the implementation instead used `\0`, duplicated the predicate, and wrote a comment falsely claiming the separators match. CORRECTED FINDING (different from the one stated, severity low): a raw 0x00 control byte is embedded in tower-list.ts at line 77 — it makes git treat the file as BINARY (c47e184 shows 'Binary files differ', so this file produces no readable diffs in PRs/git log), turns grep binary-mode, and is an invisible tooling hazard (it is precisely what generated this false finding). The proposed fix in the finding (extract a shared towerKey(p) helper using the \0 escape and call it from both functions) is still a good change because it would incidentally remove the raw byte and restore text diffs — but its rationale ('separators differ, comment false') is incorrect; do not reword the comment as if it were wrong. Not a duplicate of the 42 perf-audit findings: PERF-007 introduced countMatchingTowers, and the raw byte rode in with that commit, but no audit finding covers it.

PreprocessDialog re-run leaves stale per-state rows: removeChild() called on the wrong parent is a silent no-op

app_puller/preprocess_dialog.py:157-160 · reviewer: puller-review
What it is
On a second Run within the same dialog, `_on_run` tries to clear the previous run's per-state rows with `self.tree.invisibleRootItem().removeChild(item)`. But those items were added as children of the 'Compose (per state)' root via `comp_root.addChild(item)` (line 206, where `comp_root = self.tree.topLevelItem(1)`), not of the invisible root. QTreeWidgetItem.removeChild only removes an actual child of the receiver, so the call does nothing. `_state_items.clear()` then drops the mapping, and when `compose_start` fires again a duplicate row is appended for each state, while the orphaned old rows keep their stale '✓ ok' / '✗ FAIL' statuses forever.
Why it matters
After a failed compose, the natural action is to fix the input and click Run again in the same dialog — and the tree then shows two rows per state with conflicting statuses, making it ambiguous whether the re-run actually succeeded.
How to fix
Remove the rows from their real parent: `comp_root = self.tree.topLevelItem(1)` then `comp_root.takeChildren()` (and clear `_state_items`), or call `comp_root.removeChild(item)` per item.
Expected improvement
Re-runs show exactly one row per state with the current run's status.
Evidence
for item in self._state_items.values():
    self.tree.invisibleRootItem().removeChild(item)
...
comp_root = self.tree.topLevelItem(1)
...
comp_root.addChild(item)

Activity log renders error messages as unescaped HTML — urllib errors like '<urlopen error …>' silently vanish from the log

app_puller/main_window.py:1449-1454 · reviewer: puller-review
What it is
`_render_log_line` interpolates the raw message into rich-text HTML (`<span style='color:{color}'>{msg}</span>`) and `_append_log` feeds it to `QTextEdit.append`, which parses it as HTML. Fetch/check failures routinely contain angle brackets: http.py raises `HttpError(f"HEAD {url} → {e}")` where a urllib URLError stringifies as `<urlopen error [Errno 8] nodename nor servname provided…>`. Qt's rich-text parser treats `<urlopen …>` as an unknown tag and drops it, so the log line reads 'check failed for FCC ASR: HttpError: HEAD https://… → ' with the actual error gone. The details pane escapes correctly via its local `esc()` helper, but the log, preprocess dialog `_log_line` (preprocess_dialog.py:253-258), and walkthrough `_show_error` interpolations do not.
Why it matters
The activity log is the primary surface for diagnosing failed checks/pulls (the 'Errors only' filter exists for exactly this), and the most common network-failure message class is the one that gets truncated. Copy-log-to-clipboard copies the already-mangled plain text, so the information is lost there too.
How to fix
`html.escape(msg)` in `_render_log_line` (and in preprocess_dialog._log_line / walkthrough_dialog error paths) before interpolating into the rich-text span.
Expected improvement
Error text survives intact in the log and in copied output for the most common failure messages.
Evidence
def _render_log_line(self, ts: str, msg: str, error: bool) -> str:
    muted = self._palette().muted
    prefix = f"<span style='color:{muted}'>[{ts}]</span> "
    ...
    return f"{prefix}<span style='color:{color}'>{msg}</span>"
# http.py: raise HttpError(f"HEAD {url} → {e}") from e   # str(URLError) = '<urlopen error …>'

Rescan's 'user deleted the data' branch mutates the manifest but never saves it, and the result is miscounted

app_puller/main_window.py:214-222 · reviewer: puller-review
What it is
In `_detect_manual_installs`, the third branch (current/ now empty but the entry says installed) sets `entry.local_version = None`, `status = 'missing'`, `files = []`, `bytes = 0` — but does not increment `n_detected`, and the function only saves the manifest `if n_detected > 0`. So when a rescan's only discovery is a deletion, the change stays in-memory: the on-disk manifest still claims the source is installed until some unrelated save happens (next fetch, or the best-effort save in closeEvent). The log also reports 'Rescan: no new manually-installed sources found', hiding that the rescan just downgraded sources to missing.
Why it matters
If the app exits abnormally (e.g. the quit-during-pull abort found elsewhere) before another save, the next launch reads a manifest asserting data exists that the user deleted — `verify` and the attention panel then disagree with the status column, and 'Pull all missing' skips a source that is actually gone (until the startup rescan re-detects and again fails to persist).
How to fix
Track deletions in their own counter (or a `changed = True` flag like `_sync_cadence_from_sources` uses) and save when either counter is non-zero; include deletions in the rescan log message.
Expected improvement
Manifest on disk always reflects detected deletions immediately; rescan feedback tells the truth.
Evidence
elif not has_files and entry.local_version and entry.status == "ok":
    # User deleted the data manually — reflect that
    entry.local_version = None
    entry.status = "missing"
    entry.files = []
    entry.bytes = 0
if n_detected > 0:
    self._manifest.save()

Cancelling an assisted refresh doesn't clear the pending manual-walkthrough queue — modals still pop up after Cancel

app_puller/main_window.py:1134-1139, 1220-1222 · reviewer: puller-review
What it is
'Refresh all stale (with prompts)' stores the manual sources in `self._pending_manual_queue` and starts the auto fetches. If the user clicks 'Cancel current pull', `_cancel_active()` only calls `self._fetch_worker.cancel()` — it never clears `_pending_manual_queue`. When the cancelled fetch worker emits `all_done`, `_on_fetch_all_done` unconditionally runs `if self._pending_manual_queue: QTimer.singleShot(0, self._process_pending_manuals)`, so the user who just cancelled the bulk refresh immediately gets the full sequence of manual-walkthrough modals, one after another, each requiring an explicit close.
Why it matters
Cancel should stop the assisted flow the user started; instead it converts it into a chain of unwanted modal dialogs (one per stale manual source). It also blocks starting a fresh assisted refresh, since `_refresh_all_stale_assisted` refuses while `_pending_manual_queue` is non-empty.
How to fix
In `_cancel_active()` (and in closeEvent), clear the queue: `if self._pending_manual_queue: self._pending_manual_queue.clear(); self._append_log('Assisted refresh cancelled — manual walkthroughs skipped.')`.
Expected improvement
Cancel terminates the whole assisted refresh, matching user intent; no surprise modal chain.
Evidence
def _cancel_active(self) -> None:
    if self._fetch_worker:
        self._fetch_worker.cancel()
        self._append_log("Cancellation requested. Finishing current chunk…")
...
        # If an assisted bulk refresh queued manual walkthroughs, process them.
        if self._pending_manual_queue:
            QTimer.singleShot(0, self._process_pending_manuals)
lowUnverified preprocess effort: small

Tiles promote the non-state-unique parcel_id as the MapLibre feature id instead of parcel_id_full — duplicate ids cross-highlight unrelated parcels

scripts/preprocess/tiles.py:39 · reviewer: builder-to-browser-artifact-contract
What it is
TILE_ATTRIBUTES bakes `parcel_id` (and not `parcel_id_full`), and MapView promotes it to the feature id for setFeatureState highlighting (MapView.svelte:273, :564). But the pipeline itself distinguishes the two precisely because parcel_id is not state-unique: FL maps parcel_id to the per-county 'PARCEL_ID'/'PARCELNO' and parcel_id_full to the statewide composite key 'STATE_PAR_' (state_parcel_adapters/fdor_nal.py:17-18), AR maps them to different candidate columns (ar.py:11-12), and every internal join keys on parcel_id_full (state_parcels.py:440 `out["_source_row_id"] = parcel_id_full`; compose._reverse_index keys `attach.get("parcel_id_full")`). parcel_id can also be empty when a state's field map finds no source column (`_str_series(g, fm.get("parcel_id", []))`, state_parcels.py:396), in which case every such feature shares id ''. MapLibre setFeatureState applies state to ALL features sharing an id, so clicking one parcel outlines every same-id parcel in view.
Why it matters
In FL (11M parcels, county-scoped ids) and any state with blank parcel_id rows, the selected-parcel outline can light up multiple unrelated parcels, and the selection panel title shows an id that doesn't uniquely identify the parcel the user clicked.
How to fix
Bake parcel_id_full into TILE_ATTRIBUTES and switch promoteId/highlight to it (`promoteId: { parcels: 'parcel_id_full' }`), keeping parcel_id as a display property. Requires a tile rebuild; the panel title already falls back to parcel_id_full (schema.ts:246).
Expected improvement
Click-highlight outlines exactly one parcel everywhere, including FL and rows with blank per-county ids.
Evidence
tiles.py:39 `"parcel_id",` (no parcel_id_full); MapView.svelte:273 `promoteId: { parcels: 'parcel_id' }`; fdor_nal.py:17-18 `"parcel_id": ["PARCEL_ID", "PARCELNO"], "parcel_id_full": ["STATE_PAR_", "PARCEL_ID"]`

highUnverified deploy effort: small

No litestream restore on boot — uvicorn creates an empty DB before Litestream starts, so a volume loss makes the empty DB the newest backup state

supervisord.conf:15-44 · reviewer: deploy-security
What it is
supervisord starts uvicorn at priority=10 and litestream at priority=30 (lower starts first), and there is no `litestream restore -if-db-not-exists` step anywhere in the runtime path — I grepped supervisord.conf, Dockerfile, and deploy.sh; 'restore' appears only in the test drill (tests/test_litestream_restore.py) and DEPLOYMENT.md's reference to it. FastAPI's lifespan runs `init_db()` unconditionally on startup (backend/app/main.py line 104), which creates a fresh empty SQLite at /data/lead_state.sqlite3 if missing. So if the volume is ever lost or replaced (volume destroy, region move, fork to a new machine), the boot sequence is: uvicorn creates an empty DB → litestream starts replicating that empty DB to R2 as the newest state. `litestream restore` defaults to the latest replica state, so the documented recovery path now restores the empty DB; the real data survives only until retention/compaction prunes it, and only if the operator knows to dig for an older snapshot. This is the classic Litestream footgun the restore drill test does not cover (it restores to a separate path before any new process touches the DB).
Why it matters
Litestream exists precisely for the volume-loss scenario ('a volume loss costs seconds of work, not a day' — litestream.yml header), but the current boot ordering actively corrupts the recovery path in that exact scenario. One fresh-volume boot during an incident and the co-founder's triage notes, contact attempts, and merge overrides are at the mercy of retention windows and operator forensics.
How to fix
Add a boot wrapper (entrypoint script or a one-shot supervisord program at priority 1 with autorestart=false that the others depend on) that runs `litestream restore -if-db-not-exists -config /etc/litestream.yml "$LITESTREAM_DB_PATH"` before uvicorn starts. Alternatively run uvicorn under `litestream replicate -exec "uvicorn ..."`, which handles restore-then-run ordering natively. Document the change next to the 'don't restart mid-sync' gotcha.
Expected improvement
A machine booted against a fresh volume self-heals from R2 instead of silently replacing the newest backup with an empty database.
Evidence
supervisord.conf: `[program:uvicorn] ... priority=10` then `[program:litestream] command=litestream replicate -config /etc/litestream.yml ... priority=30`. backend/app/main.py line 102-104: `async def lifespan(_app: FastAPI) ... init_db()`. grep for 'restore' across supervisord.conf/Dockerfile/deploy.sh: zero hits.
highUnverified deploy effort: small

users.txt is runtime-mutable production state with no replication path — Litestream covers only the SQLite DB

litestream.yml:19-29 · reviewer: credentials-file-runtime-lifecycle
What it is
Since PRD #121, POST /api/me/password rewrites /data/users.txt at runtime in production (fly.toml line 17: APP_USERS_FILE = "/data/users.txt"). But the only continuous backup on the machine is Litestream, and litestream.yml's single dbs entry replicates only ${LITESTREAM_DB_PATH} (the SQLite lead DB). deploy.sh syncs only the composite (COMPOSITE_SRC/VOLUME_DEST). The only thing that incidentally captures users.txt is Fly's automatic daily volume snapshot (DEPLOYMENT.md step 10: 'Fly volume snapshots are automatic (daily); extend retention as desired' — default retention is short). ADR-068 explicitly acknowledges the gap ('users.txt ... is not Litestream-replicated like the DB ... snapshot it accordingly') but no mitigation was ever built. The 'app bricks' scenario is partially confirmed: on volume loss, Litestream restores the DB, but a missing/empty users.txt trips the fail-closed boot (config.py:117-129 raises RuntimeError at module import), uvicorn crash-loops and goes FATAL after supervisord's 10 startretries, and the app stays down until an operator runs `fly ssh console` + `python -m app.users add` for every user — which resets every password. Recoverable, not permanently bricked — but every UI-made password rotation since the last daily snapshot is silently and unrecoverably reverted.
Why it matters
A password rotation reverting is a security event, not just an inconvenience: if a user rotated because their old password leaked, a snapshot restore silently reinstates the compromised password and the user's new one stops working with no signal. And the disaster-recovery posture is inconsistent — ADR-064 called volume loss 'the one real risk' and bought Litestream specifically for it, yet the file that gates ALL access (fail-closed boot, per-request authority) has at best 24-hour-stale, 5-day-retained protection while the data behind it has point-in-time restore.
How to fix
Give users.txt a continuous replication path mirroring the DB's: simplest is a small supervised loop (or a hook after _write_atomic) that copies users.txt to the existing private R2 bucket whenever its mtime changes (rclone is already in the image), e.g. `rclone copyto /data/users.txt r2:morphy-prod/users.txt` keyed on mtime. Then add a users.txt-restore step to the documented recovery runbook next to the Litestream drill, and extend Fly snapshot retention as ADR-068 already suggests.
Expected improvement
Volume loss costs seconds of credential state instead of up to 24 hours (or everything); password rotations made through the UI survive restore; recovery becomes a documented two-file restore instead of manual re-provisioning that resets every user's password.
Evidence
litestream.yml: `dbs:\n  - path: ${LITESTREAM_DB_PATH}` (sole entry; fly.toml: `LITESTREAM_DB_PATH = "/data/lead_state.sqlite3"`, `APP_USERS_FILE = "/data/users.txt"`). ADR-0068:33: 'users.txt lives on the Fly volume and is **not** Litestream-replicated like the SQLite DB'. config.py:117-124: `if not credentials.load(credentials_path): problems.append(...)` → `raise RuntimeError("Auth misconfigured...")`.

Renaming a team in users.txt silently mints a new empty tenant and orphans the team's entire board

backend/app/tenant_service.py:33-48 · reviewer: identity-tenancy-lifecycle
What it is
Tenant resolution is keyed purely on the normalized team-name string: `find_or_create` looks up `Tenant.name == name` and creates a new row on a miss. If the operator changes a team's name in users.txt (the only way to 'rename' a team — the CLI at backend/app/users.py:204-257 has add/remove/list/set-password/set-team but no rename-team, so a rename means running set-team per user or hand-editing), the very next request from any member of that team resolves the new name, finds no Tenant row, and registers a brand-new tenant id. Every Lead, ContactAttempt, Task, OwnerClusterOverride, AntennaOverride, TenantSetting, ScoringPreset, and UserSetting row stays under the old tenant id. There is no `import logging` anywhere in tenant_service.py or auth_service.py — tenant creation is completely silent — and neither backend/README.md nor the CLI help mentions the consequence. The operator's only signal is a mysteriously empty board.
Why it matters
This is effective data loss from the user's perspective with zero detection and no migration path. For the current deployment the first team owns the production lead board; a well-intentioned cosmetic rename (e.g. 'morphy' -> 'morphy-consulting') wipes the co-founder's daily triage surface — leads, contact info, attempt history, merge overrides — until someone reverse-engineers the name-keying from the code and reverts the file.
How to fix
Minimal: log a warning whenever find_or_create mints a NEW tenant ('registered new tenant id=N for team X — if this was a rename, the old board is under a different tenant') and document the rename hazard in backend/README.md next to the set-team docs. Better: add a `rename-team <old> <new>` operation that updates the Tenant row's name in the DB (one UPDATE, name is unique) and rewrites the team field for every matching users.txt line atomically.
Expected improvement
A team rename becomes either a safe supported operation or at minimum a logged, diagnosable event instead of a silent board wipe.
Evidence
def find_or_create(self, name: str) -> Tenant:
    tenant = self.get_by_name(name)
    if tenant is None:
        tenant = Tenant(name=name)
        self.session.add(tenant)

"Today" is computed in UTC and frozen at module init — due-window filters, the Tasks digest, and the Calendar shift a day for US users every evening and drift in long-lived tabs

app/src/lib/tasks-store.svelte.ts:29-31, 42, 45 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
Three surfaces derive "today" from `new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10)`, i.e. the UTC date: (1) tasks-store sets `today = todayIso()` once at module load and `visible` filters every task against that frozen value; (2) Dashboard.svelte:29 computes `const today` the same way per mount for the digest; (3) Calendar.svelte:10-12 initializes `year`/`month` from `now.getUTCFullYear()/getUTCMonth()`. For users in US timezones (the product's anchor states are AR/MT/FL/WV), UTC "today" becomes local tomorrow from 5-8pm onward: tasks due today are prematurely classed overdue by the due-window filter (`dueBeforeFor('overdue') = today - 1`), the digest's due-today/tomorrow window shifts, and on the last evening of a month the Calendar opens to the next month. Additionally, because `tasks.today` is module-level and never refreshed, a tab left open across days — the explicit usage model — keeps filtering against the day it was opened.
Why it matters
The triage workflow leans on due dates (auto follow-ups, overdue attention, the Tasks digest); a day-shifted or frozen "today" produces wrong overdue/due-today buckets exactly during evening work sessions and in the always-open tab.
How to fix
Compute local-date ISO (e.g. `const d = new Date(); `${d.getFullYear()}-${pad(d.getMonth()+1)}-${pad(d.getDate())}``) in one shared helper; in tasks-store make `today` a $state refreshed on load()/a midnight interval instead of a one-shot module constant; initialize the Calendar from local `getFullYear()/getMonth()`.
Expected improvement
Due/overdue buckets and the calendar month match the user's wall clock at all hours, and stay correct in tabs left open across days.
Evidence
function todayIso(): string {
  return new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10);
}
...
 today = todayIso();   // module-singleton, never refreshed
 visible = $derived(this.tasks.filter((t) => matchesTask(t, this.filter, this.today)));
— Dashboard.svelte: const today = new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10);
— Calendar.svelte: let year = $state(now.getUTCFullYear()); let month = $state(now.getUTCMonth());

signOut leaves all tenant-scoped store data in memory — a second user from another team can transiently see the previous tenant's leads/tasks/attention

app/src/lib/auth-store.svelte.ts:67-73 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
auth.signOut() clears only token/email/team/displayName. The leads store (lead detail, myLeads, candidates), tasks store, dashboard attention items, and activity store all keep the signed-out tenant's data. Keeping state across sign-out is documented as intentional for re-sign-in by the same user (App.svelte comment), but teams are tenants (ADR-063): when a user from a *different team* signs in on the same tab, surfaces that render store data without checking a load status briefly display the previous tenant's records before their refetch resolves — e.g. the Dashboard Tasks digest renders `tasks.tasks` task titles directly (Dashboard.svelte:30, 115-120) and the Calendar digest renders `activity.lastContactOn` directly, while the App sign-in effect only refreshes counts/attention/settings. PRD #121 explicitly handled this two-users-one-pageload case for settings ("a second user signing in ... gets *their* prefs, never the first user's") but the data stores were not given the same treatment.
Why it matters
Tenant isolation is the backend's hardest invariant; the frontend undermines it at the seam by flashing one team's lead names, task titles, and attention items to a member of another team sharing a browser. Low likelihood (shared machine across teams) but it is exactly the class of leak the architecture promises not to have.
How to fix
On signOut (or on a sign-in whose team differs from the previous session), reset the tenant-scoped stores to their empty initial state (leads, tasks, dashboard, activity, archivedAntennas, scoring presets); alternatively gate the Dashboard digest renders on each store's status the way History/MyLeads already do.
Expected improvement
No cross-tenant data is ever rendered, even transiently, after an account switch in the same tab.
Evidence
signOut(): void {
  clearToken();
  this.token = null;
  this.email = null;
  this.team = null;
  this.displayName = null;
}
— no store resets; Dashboard renders `digestTasks` (= tasks.tasks.filter(...)) and `activity.lastContactOn` without status checks.

mediumConfirmed fetch effort: small

nc_onemap_parcels resume can stitch pages from two different upstream snapshots into one 'current' dataset stamped with the newer version

scripts/fetch/sources/nc_onemap_parcels.py:164-194 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
With `supports_resume = True`, prepare_partial_for_resume preserves all `page_NNNN.geojson` files from the previous (possibly days-old) run, and fetch() skips any page that exists with size > 1024 bytes (lines 187-194). But fetch() also re-runs the count query and re-captures `rv = self.remote_version()` fresh (line 166). If NC OneMap re-published between the interrupted run and the resume (counties refresh monthly; lastEditDate changes), the resumed output mixes old-snapshot pages with new-snapshot pages: objectid-ordered offset pagination over a changed table produces duplicated and/or missing parcels at every preserved/refetched page boundary, and the manifest then records the whole mixed directory as the NEW remote_version.
Why it matters
The resulting current/ directory silently contains parcels that are duplicated, missing, or attribute-inconsistent across page boundaries, and nothing downstream can detect it because the manifest says the data is the fresh version with status ok. NC parcel data feeds owner clustering — phantom or missing owners are exactly the kind of quiet corruption that erodes trust in the lead board.
How to fix
Write a small marker file (e.g. `.snapshot_version` containing rv and the count-query total) into the .partial dir at the start of a run. On resume, read it: if the stored version or total differs from the freshly probed values, wipe the preserved pages (fall back to prepare_fresh_partial semantics) instead of reusing them; if equal, resume as today. Stamp the manifest with the marker's version, not the freshly probed one.
Expected improvement
Resume keeps its 30-60-minute time savings in the common same-snapshot case while guaranteeing a promoted NC dataset is internally consistent and correctly versioned.
Evidence
nc_onemap_parcels.py:166 `rv = self.remote_version()  # capture once…`; :187-188 `if page_path.exists() and page_path.stat().st_size > 1024:\n                written.append(page_path)` — no check that preserved pages came from the same lastEditDate/count

io.py writers (catalog.json, summary.json, every composite parquet/geojson) write in place, violating the repo-wide atomic temp+rename convention

scripts/preprocess/io.py:62-80, 94-122, 237-242 · reviewer: preprocess-core
What it is
write_geoparquet, write_parquet, write_geojson and write_json all do `if path.exists(): path.unlink()` then write directly to the final path — no temp file, no os.replace. These are the writers behind every compose output: parcel shards, towers.parquet, views/*.parquet, catalog.json and summary.json (compose.py lines 502/537 call write_json on them). Every other writer in the subsystem follows the atomic convention — manifest.py save(), app_assets.write_composite_index, facets._write_parquet_atomic/_write_json_atomic, tiles (.partial→rename), and owners_index._atomic_write_json, whose docstring explicitly calls temp+os.replace "the repo-wide convention". CLAUDE.md §8 also mandates "Atomic writes everywhere."
Why it matters
Two concrete failure modes: (1) a crash/OOM mid-write (this pipeline has an OOM history on FL) leaves a truncated catalog.json/summary.json/parquet at the final path — rebuild_from_disk then silently drops the state (unreadable catalog → `continue`), and a truncated towers.parquet breaks owners_index._read_state_towers; (2) live readers race the writer: scripts/serve.py serves data/ over HTTP while preprocess runs, so the map app can fetch a file in the unlinked-or-half-written window.
How to fix
Route the four io.py writers through the same mkstemp-in-parent + os.replace pattern owners_index._atomic_write_json already uses (write to `.{stem}.tmp`, fsync optional, os.replace). For to_parquet/to_file, write to the temp path then rename.
Expected improvement
Crash- and reader-safe composite outputs, consistent with the rest of the codebase's stated invariant; removes a class of 'state disappeared from the app after an interrupted run' incidents.
Evidence
io.py write_geoparquet: `if path.exists(): path.unlink()\n    g = _prep_complex_cols(gdf)\n    g.to_parquet(path, index=False)`; owners_index.py _atomic_write_json docstring: "via temp-file + ``os.replace`` so a reader never sees a half-written file (the repo-wide convention)"
Verifier notes (2)

Solar normalizers pass county NAMES to canon_county_fips, which only parses numeric codes — county_fips is always None and CountyScoreToSolarJoin is a permanent no-op

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/eia_860.py:82 · reviewer: preprocess-normalizers
What it is
eia_860.py:82, eia_860m.py:63 and uspvdb.py:44 call `canon_county_fips(r.get("County")/r.get("p_county"), state_fips=...)`, but those source columns are county *names* ("Pulaski", "Maricopa"), not codes. canon_county_fips (fips.py:56-79) only handles digit strings / GEOIDs / numerics — `int(float("Pulaski"))` raises and it returns None — so county_fips is None on 100% of solar facility rows. The registered CountyScoreToSolarJoin merges on county_fips via the county_score annotator (annotators/county_score.py:56 `safe_merge(target, agg, on="county_fips", ...)`), so distress_tier/persistent_poverty never attach to any solar facility. state_billboards.py:88 has the same name-into-numeric-parser pattern for its County column.
Why it matters
A whole registered join silently does nothing for the solar layer: no solar facility ever carries a distress tier or persistent-poverty flag, and the failure is invisible (None is also the legitimate missing value, and no quality-report warning fires). Anything ranking solar leads by county distress is quietly wrong.
How to fix
Add a county-name→FIPS resolution path: the project already ships exactly this pattern as ParcelAdapter.county_resolver (MT_COUNTY_NAME_TO_FIPS, WV_COUNTY_NAME_TO_FIPS) — generalize it with a Census county-name lookup keyed by state_fips, or extend canon_county_fips with an optional name lookup. At minimum, emit a quality-report warning when the county column resolves to None on >90% of rows so the gap is visible.
Expected improvement
Solar facilities (and billboards) gain real county_fips, making CountyScoreToSolarJoin functional and county-level rollups correct.
Evidence
eia_860.py:82 `county_fips = canon_county_fips(r.get("County"), state_fips=state_fips)`; fips.py:66-68 `n = int(float(s)) except (TypeError, ValueError): return None`; county_score annotator: `merged = safe_merge(target, agg, on="county_fips", columns_added=COLUMNS_ADDED)`

set-team strands the user's UserSetting rows under the old tenant and degrades old-board attribution to 'Unattributed' / 'user N'

backend/app/auth_service.py:151-154 · reviewer: identity-tenancy-lifecycle
What it is
When `set-team` moves a user, `provision_user` re-homes them by flipping `user.tenant_id` (auth_service.py:151-154 `elif user.tenant_id != tenant_id: user.tenant_id = tenant_id`). Their UserSetting rows are PK'd `(tenant_id, user_id, key)` (models.py:246-248) and read via `session.get(UserSetting, (self.tenant_id, self.user_id, key))` (user_settings_store.py:60), so after the move every preference — theme, basemap, home view, saved filter defaults — silently resets to defaults; the old rows linger forever and resurrect only if the user moves back. On the OLD team's surfaces the departed user's id dangles: `_tenant_user_names` (main.py:930-937) correctly filters `User.tenant_id == tenant_id` (so no cross-tenant email leak), which means /api/stats renders their entire outreach history as 'Unattributed' (main.py:854 `names.get(uid, "Unattributed")` — the same label used for system-generated rows), /api/activity renders a blank member name (main.py:899), and tasks still assigned to them render as 'user 7' in the Tasks UI (app/src/lib/tasks-store.svelte.ts:48-51 `return u ? u.name : `user ${id}``). Those tasks also vanish from everyone's attention feed: AttentionService surfaces overdue tasks that are 'mine or unassigned', and the assignee never requests under the old tenant again.
Why it matters
A routine team move silently destroys the moved user's personalization and corrupts the old team's analytics: their real outreach work is lumped into the same 'Unattributed' bucket as auto-logged status rows, and their assigned tasks become orphans no one is nudged about. README's 'the old team's data is untouched' is true at the row level but misleading at the display level.
How to fix
In `provision_user`'s re-home branch, migrate the user's UserSetting rows to the new tenant_id in the same commit (UPDATE user_setting SET tenant_id=:new WHERE tenant_id=:old AND user_id=:uid). For old-board attribution, distinguish 'former teammate' from 'Unattributed' — e.g. fall back to the User row's email/display_name when the id exists in app_user but under another tenant, exposing only users who once wrote rows into this tenant; or null-out / reassign open assigned tasks as part of the move.
Expected improvement
Preferences follow the person across a team move, old-team stats stay honest about who did the work, and orphaned task assignments stop disappearing from the attention feed.
Evidence

    user.tenant_id = tenant_id
    self.session.commit()  # UserSetting PK (tenant_id, user_id, key) is not migrated
... names.get(uid, "Unattributed")  # main.py:854

PARCEL_SCHEMA reads ~12 keys the PMTiles never bake and the retired DuckDB row-fetch no longer supplies — permanent '—' rows and dead panel sections on every map parcel click

app/src/lib/schema.ts:243-384 · reviewer: builder-to-browser-artifact-contract
What it is
Since the per-state DuckDB enrichment was retired (MapView.svelte:557-558: 'baked PMTiles tile properties only — the per-state DuckDB enrichment was retired'; duckdb.fetchParcel/parcelRowQuery have zero non-test callers), the ONLY data a clicked parcel's DetailsPanel ever receives is the 12-field TILE_ATTRIBUTES set baked by tiles.py:38-51 (parcel_id, owner_name, owner_kind, acreage, assessed_value, land_use_desc, distress_tier, in_oz, has_tower, has_solar, has_billboard, interestingness_score). PARCEL_SCHEMA, however, still reads the full v3 row: owner_name_2, mailing_addr/_city/_state/_zip, situs_addr/_city, county_fips, year_built, land_value, improvement_value, owner_cluster_size, owner_cluster_value_total, tower_count/solar_count/billboard_count, in_qct, persistent_poverty, distress_overlays, infrastructure_summary, interestingness_components. DetailsPanel renders these as '—' rows (line 56) or never shows the 'Infrastructure on this parcel' / 'Score factors' breakouts (infrastructure_summary / interestingness_components never reach the tile), and the QCT tag (schema.ts:272 `p.in_qct === true`) can never appear — note the asymmetry: in_oz IS baked, in_qct is not. The existing schema-completeness test validates these keys against the composite catalog schema, not against TILE_ATTRIBUTES, so it green-lights the drift.
Why it matters
The click-a-parcel panel — a core triage surface — permanently shows dashes for mailing address, county, valuation breakdown, portfolio size and QCT/persistent-poverty context that the pipeline actually computes, with no error anywhere. The asymmetric in_oz/in_qct baking makes the Distress section look like missing data rather than a contract gap.
How to fix
Either extend TILE_ATTRIBUTES with the cheap scalar fields the panel reads (in_qct, persistent_poverty, county_fips, tower_count, solar_count, billboard_count — booleans/ints add little to tile size) and rebuild tiles, or re-wire the click path to fetch the full row from parcels_attr.parquet (parcelRowQuery/fetchParcel already exist, unused). Then trim PARCEL_SCHEMA of keys the chosen feed will genuinely never carry so the panel shows only real rows.
Expected improvement
Map parcel clicks show the distress/valuation/portfolio context the pipeline already computes; no permanently-dead '—' rows or unreachable tags.
Evidence
tiles.py:38-51 `TILE_ATTRIBUTES: tuple[str, ...] = ("parcel_id", "owner_name", … "in_oz", "has_tower", …)` (no in_qct/county_fips/counts); schema.ts:364 `{ label: 'Qualified Census Tract', keys: ['in_qct'], … }`, :288 `keys: ['mailing_addr', 'mailing_city', …]`; MapView.svelte:557-558 'baked PMTiles tile properties only — the per-state DuckDB enrichment was retired'

No transactional publish or shared generation stamp — rclone mirrors the artifact set file-by-file into the live-served directory, and an interrupted leg 2 leaves an undetectable mixed-generation volume

scripts/sync_composite.py:82-107 · reviewer: composite-publish-seam
What it is
sync() runs one `rclone sync src dst` per leg directly into the directory Caddy is serving (`/data/processed/composite`). The artifact set that must agree — index.json, owners_index.json, owners_filter.json, per-state parcels.pmtiles / parcels_attr.parquet / towers.parquet — flips file-by-file in unspecified order over a multi-minute leg-2 window, with no versioned directory, no pointer flip, and no completion marker. The producer stamps `generated_at` into owners_index.json and copies it into owners_filter.json (owners_index.py:588-589, 661-662), and index.json carries one too (parsed in app/src/lib/catalog.ts:168 via `requireString(raw.generated_at, ...)`) — but no consumer ever cross-checks them, so a browser mid-window can pair a gen-N+1 owners_filter.json with gen-N pmtiles/index.json silently. Worse, when leg 2 dies halfway — operator restart (the documented 'Don't restart mid-sync' gotcha, DEPLOYMENT.md:373-375), a Fly health-check-triggered machine restart (fly.toml:58-63 restarts on a 3s-timeout /api/health probe while rclone competes for the shared-cpu-1x/1GB machine), or the operator's SSH session dropping — the volume is left part-new/part-old with nothing distinguishing it from a completed publish. `deploy.sh smoke` only range-reads index.json (deploy.sh:111-113), which passes against any mixed state. One mitigation that does exist: rclone sync's default --delete-after skips deletions when transfers errored, so interrupted runs don't also delete files.
Why it matters
Mixed-generation state is silent and persistent: after an interrupted sync the app can serve a state listed in a new index.json whose pmtiles never arrived (404s / blank map), or owner records whose tiles disagree, until someone happens to re-run the sync. The only safeguards today are two human-memory gotchas in DEPLOYMENT.md.
How to fix
Publish into a versioned directory (`composite/<generated_at>/...`) and atomically flip a tiny pointer file (or symlink) that the app reads first — this makes every generation's URLs immutable and the cutover one rename. A cheaper interim step: have sync() write a `publish_complete.json` stamp (containing generated_at) as the final transferred file, and extend `deploy.sh smoke` to fetch it and compare against owners_filter.json's generated_at, so a partial mirror is at least detectable.
Expected improvement
A publish becomes atomic from the client's point of view; an interrupted sync is detectable by a one-line smoke check instead of being indistinguishable from success.
Evidence
sync_composite.py:99: "cmd = [rclone, 'sync' if delete else 'copy', str(src), str(dst)]" (destination is the live-served dir); DEPLOYMENT.md:373-375: "Don't restart mid-sync — a restart kills the in-machine fly ssh rclone leg and leaves the volume partial."; deploy.sh:112: smoke only checks "${APP_URL}/data/processed/composite/index.json"
mediumUnverified deploy effort: small

Mirror semantics + cwd-relative COMPOSITE_SRC + no --max-delete: a pruned local composite silently deletes whole states from production on --apply

deploy.sh:30, 91-101 · reviewer: composite-publish-seam
What it is
Mirroring (delete files absent at source) is the intentional contract, but it has no blast-radius guard. `COMPOSITE_SRC` defaults to the cwd-relative path `data/processed/composite` (deploy.sh:30) rather than being anchored to the computed `REPO_ROOT` (deploy.sh:37), and sync() passes no `--max-delete` to rclone (sync_composite.py:99-104). The repo's own history shows local state pruning is a real workflow — NC raw was deleted locally (CLAUDE.md §7: 'raw deleted 2026-05-20'). If the operator's local composite is partial (a single-state recompose after cleanup, or a different checkout's empty-but-existing dir), `./deploy.sh data --apply` mirrors that absence to R2 and then leg 2 mirrors it to the volume — deleting entire states' pmtiles/parquet from production. The deleted count is printed only after deletion (sync_composite.py:139), and the dry-run default is a separate invocation an operator under time pressure can skip; test_mirror_deletes_files_absent_at_source confirms the deletion path works exactly this way.
Why it matters
One --apply from the wrong local state takes whole states off the live map with no confirmation. The data is reproducible from the laptop, so it's recoverable — but recovery means a full multi-GB re-publish plus the manual-restart dance, and in the meantime the served index.json and the surviving artifacts disagree.
How to fix
Pass rclone's `--max-delete N` (e.g. fail the sync if more than ~50 deletions are pending) from sync(), overridable with an explicit flag for intentional state removals; and change deploy.sh:30 to `COMPOSITE_SRC="${COMPOSITE_SRC:-${REPO_ROOT}/data/processed/composite}"` so the source no longer depends on the invocation cwd. A dry-run-then-confirm flow inside `data --apply` (run --dry-run first, show the delete count, require 'y' if nonzero) would close the remaining gap.
Expected improvement
A partial or wrong-cwd local composite can no longer silently delete production states; intentional removals require an explicit override.
Evidence
deploy.sh:30: "COMPOSITE_SRC=\"${COMPOSITE_SRC:-data/processed/composite}\"" (relative, while REPO_ROOT is computed at line 37 but unused here); sync_composite.py:99-104 builds the rclone command with filters + --dry-run + logging flags only — no --max-delete.
lowConfirmed backend effort: small

SQLite silently drops the timezone from DateTime(timezone=True) columns — updated_at/created_at serialize as offset-less ISO strings in prod, with offsets on Postgres

backend/app/models.py:46-49 (serialized at backend/app/owners_index_service.py 275 and backend/app/main.py 990-992) · reviewer: backend-bugs
What it is
All timestamp columns are declared `DateTime(timezone=True)` with tz-aware UTC defaults (`datetime.now(UTC)`), but the production database is SQLite (fly.toml: DATABASE_URL=sqlite+pysqlite:////data/lead_state.sqlite3), whose SQLAlchemy dialect has no timezone support — the bind format stores wall-clock digits only and reads back NAIVE datetimes. The API then serializes them with `.isoformat()` (lead_view: `lead.updated_at.isoformat()`; _task_to_out: `task.created_at.isoformat()`), producing strings like '2026-06-09T01:23:45.678901' with no Z/offset. Per the ECMAScript spec, `new Date()` on such a string parses it as LOCAL time, so any client rendering would show times shifted by the viewer's UTC offset. On Postgres the same code emits '+00:00'-suffixed strings — the documented 'identical on either backend' claim (db.py docstring) doesn't hold for timestamp serialization. Today the SPA declares but barely renders these fields, so the bug is latent rather than visible.
Why it matters
The first feature that renders 'updated 2h ago' or sorts by created_at across a DST boundary inherits a 4-5 hour skew on prod-SQLite, and any future SQLite→Postgres migration changes the API's wire format for the same rows.
How to fix
Normalize at the serialization boundary: a small helper that does `dt.replace(tzinfo=UTC).isoformat()` when tzinfo is None (or always re-attach UTC, since _utcnow stores UTC wall time) used in lead_view and _task_to_out.
Expected improvement
Timestamps carry an explicit UTC offset on both backends; client Date parsing becomes unambiguous and backend-portable.
Evidence
models.py: `updated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(\n        DateTime(timezone=True), default=_utcnow, onupdate=_utcnow\n    )`; owners_index_service.py: `\"updated_at\": lead.updated_at.isoformat() if lead else None,`; fly.toml: `DATABASE_URL = \"sqlite+pysqlite:////data/lead_state.sqlite3\"`
Verifier notes (2)

What I checked: (1) /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/backend/app/models.py — `_utcnow()` returns tz-aware `datetime.now(UTC)` (line 24-25) and every timestamp column is `DateTime(timezone=True)` (lines 46-49 as cited, plus 94, 117, 152-154, 173, 212-216, 267, 287). (2) Serialization sites match: backend/app/owners_index_service.py:275 (`lead.updated_at.isoformat()`) and backend/app/main.py:990-992 (`_task_to_out` created_at/updated_at/completed_at). (3) Prod is SQLite: fly.toml:20 `DATABASE_URL = "sqlite+pysqlite:////data/lead_state.sqlite3"`. (4) No mitigation exists — grep for `tzinfo`/`astimezone`/`replace(tzinfo` across backend/app/ returns nothing, and no test asserts timestamp wire format. (5) Empirically reproduced in the repo venv: a `DateTime(timezone=True)` column round-tripped through sqlite+pysqlite returns `tzinfo: None` and `isoformat()` = '2026-06-10T00:06:26.178340' (no offset); the stored digits are the UTC wall time, so the proposed fix (re-attach UTC at the serialization boundary) is correct and safe. That does not change the substance: SQLite-vs-Postgres wire formats genuinely diverge ('2026-...678901' vs '2026-...+00:00'). User-supplied string fields persisted with no length validation (silent unbounded growth on SQLite, 500s on Postgres)

backend/app/main.py:259-274 · reviewer: backend-security
What it is
Request schemas declare plain `str` fields with no `max_length` (e.g. `MeUpdate.display_name`, `TaskCreate.title`, `LeadUpdate.contact_*`, and the opaque `home_view`/`filter_default`/scoring-preset `config` blobs), while the ORM declares bounded columns like `display_name String(255)`, `title String(255)`, and `Text` for notes/settings. SQLite (the production DB per fly.toml) does not enforce VARCHAR length, so oversized values are stored verbatim with no cap; on a Postgres deploy (supported via DATABASE_URL) a value exceeding the declared length raises a DataError that surfaces as an unhandled 500. The opaque Text blobs (`filter_default`, `home_view`, `ScoringPreset.config`) are persisted 'verbatim, never inspected,' so an authenticated user can store arbitrarily large payloads.
Why it matters
On the current SQLite prod backend this is a storage-exhaustion vector an authenticated user can drive (no size ceiling on notes/settings blobs); on a Postgres backend the same inputs become inconsistent 500 errors rather than clean 422s. Either way the validation contract differs by database engine, which is a fragile, dialect-dependent behavior.
How to fix
Add `max_length` constraints to the bounded string schema fields (display_name, title, contact_*, channel) matching the column widths so over-length input returns a clean 422 on every backend, and cap the size of the opaque JSON blobs (`filter_default`/`home_view`/`config`) with a server-side byte limit before persisting.
Expected improvement
Uniform input validation across SQLite and Postgres, no engine-dependent 500s, and a bound on attacker-controlled blob storage.
Evidence
MeUpdate.display_name: `str | None` (no max_length) → `user.display_name = body.display_name.strip()`; model `display_name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(String(255)...)`
Verifier notes (2)

MeUpdate.display_name is `str | None = Field(default=None)` (schemas.py:254), LeadUpdate.contact_*/notes/best_channel are unconstrained `str | None` (schemas.py:149-157), TaskCreate.title is bare `str` (schemas.py:296), UserSettingsUpdate.home_view/filter_default are unconstrained `str | None` (schemas.py:286-287), ScoringPresetIn.config is an unbounded `dict` (schemas.py:411). (2) ORM columns are bounded as claimed: User.display_name String(255) (models.py:286), Task.title String(255) (models.py:193), Lead.contact_name String(255)/contact_phone String(64)/contact_email String(320)/best_channel String(32) (models.py:42-45), and Text for Lead.notes, UserSetting.value, ScoringPreset.config. (3) Evidence quote is real: main.py:269-272 does `user.display_name = body.display_name.strip()` with no length check. (4) fly.toml sets DATABASE_URL to sqlite+pysqlite — prod is SQLite, which ignores VARCHAR lengths, so oversized values store verbatim; Postgres via DATABASE_URL would raise StringDataRightTruncation/DataError, and there are no exception handlers in main.py (only CORSMiddleware; no body-size limit middleware). (5) The 'persisted verbatim, never inspected' wording is a direct quote of the route docstring at main.py:299-301. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit — that report is performance-only (PERF-001..042); grep of docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md found no input-length/validation finding. Mitigating context supporting the existing 'low' severity: authenticated-only, two-person trusted team product behind Cloudflare (which caps per-request body size), so practical exploitability is minimal — the dialect-dependent validation contract is the real defect.

decodeFilters / deserializeFilterModel merge untrusted JSON into typed filter state with no type validation

app/src/lib/url.ts:44-50 · reviewer: frontend-security
What it is
Both deep-link filter decoding (url.ts `decodeFilters`) and the saved filter-default restore (filter-model.ts:284-299 `deserializeFilterModel`) copy raw parsed-JSON values into the typed state objects key-by-key with `target[key] = obj[key]` — only key names are validated, never value types. I verified there is no SQL-injection consequence: `sqlString` doubles quotes and `sqlNumber` throws on anything that isn't a finite number, and the live Explore filter path doesn't execute SQL at all (it runs the in-memory `matchesOwner` predicate over the owners index; `composeOwnerWhere` has no production caller). The actual effect of type confusion is silent misbehavior: e.g. a crafted `?filters={"scoreMin":"5"}` or a snapshot with `"states":"AR"` flows into predicates typed `as string[]` / `as number`, where `"AR".includes(x)` does substring semantics and string-vs-number comparisons quietly evaluate false — filters appear active but match the wrong set. In the (retired) parcel path, the same confusion makes `composeWhere` throw synchronously outside FilterEngine.run()'s try/catch (filter-engine.ts:90 computes `composeWhere(filters)` before the `try` at line 96).
Why it matters
A shared deep link or a corrupted saved default can put the filter UI into a state where the displayed filter chips don't match the actual narrowing — the user triages against a silently wrong candidate set with no error surfaced. The inline comment in url.ts ("composeWhere escapes / validates, so a hand-crafted URL cannot inject SQL") is true for injection but overstates the validation: types are never checked.
How to fix
In both merge loops, validate each value's shape against the default before assigning — e.g. accept only values where `typeof obj[key] === typeof defaults[key]` plus an `Array.isArray` check matching the default, and `Number.isFinite` for numerics; drop mismatches to the default. ~10 lines shared between url.ts and filter-model.ts.
Expected improvement
Malformed or adversarial filter params degrade to the unfiltered default instead of silently mis-filtering or throwing in legacy paths; the documented 'tolerant of junk' contract becomes actually true at the type level.
Evidence
url.ts: `for (const key of Object.keys(result)) { if (key in obj) { ... target[key] = obj[key]; } }`. filter-model.ts:291: `for (const key of Object.keys(result)) { if (key in obj) target[key] = obj[key]; }`. filter-engine.ts:90: `const where = composeWhere(filters);` precedes the `try {` at line 96.
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Code-level claim verified true in both spots: url.ts:44-50 (decodeFilters) and filter-model.ts:291 (deserializeFilterModel) copy parsed-JSON values key-by-key with no type validation; filter-engine.ts:90 composeWhere does run before the try at line 96. Tests (app/tests/unit/filter-model.test.ts:241-247, url.test.ts) cover malformed JSON / non-object but not type-mismatched values. HOWEVER, the impact story needs major corrections. (1) The deep-link half is moot: the ONLY production caller of readUrlState/decodeFilters is App.svelte:101, which uses the result solely as a null-check (urlHasFilters) to decide whether the personal filter default applies; the decoded values are discarded, and encodeFilters/buildUrl have zero production callers. A crafted ?filters={"scoreMin":"5"} never reaches any predicate — the "shared deep link puts the filter UI into a silently wrong state" scenario in why_it_matters cannot happen in current code. (Also a shape mismatch: decodeFilters returns the legacy parcel FilterState, which has no `states` field; the live Explore state is the separate FilterModel.) (2) url.ts + filter-engine.ts are already flagged as dead code slated for DELETION by the 2026-06-07 perf audit (docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md lines 596-600, one of the 42 findings, apparently unexecuted) — so the proposed fix (add validation to url.ts) is the wrong fix for that half; deletion/narrowing to a bare has-filters check is. (3) The live half — deserializeFilterModel — is real: App.svelte:103 feeds it into filterModel.updateFilter, driving matchesOwner over the owners index, and SettingsFilters.svelte:17 feeds it into summarizeFilterModel (where e.g. states:"AR" would throw TypeError on .join, breaking the Settings card). But the snapshot is the user's OWN filter_default settings blob, written only by the app itself via serializeFilterModel (always well-typed), scoped per (tenant_id, user_id), and explicitly documented on both sides as an opaque client-owned blob persisted verbatim (backend/app/user_settings_store.py:9, backend/app/main.py:300 — intentional design). Reaching the type-confusion requires the user PUTting a crafted blob to their own settings (self-attack, no boundary crossed), backend storage corruption, or future schema drift where a field changes type (the forward-compat comment at filter-model.ts:265-270 covers absent fields, not type-changed ones — the one genuine forward-looking gap, as snapshots carry no version). Net: finding exists as a minor robustness gap in deserializeFilterModel only; severity should drop from low to info, and the fix should target filter-model.ts alone (url.ts should instead be deleted per the perf audit).

lowConfirmed preprocess effort: trivial

run_normalize_all persists the manifest only after the entire multi-source sweep, so an interrupt loses every completed normalize record

scripts/preprocess/runner.py:131-148 · reviewer: preprocess-core
What it is
run_normalize_all loops every registered normalizer (a multi-hour job over ~15 GB of raw data) calling run_normalize_one, which records each outcome via manifest.record_normalize — in memory only — and saves the whole manifest once at the end of the loop. A KeyboardInterrupt or OOM kill mid-sweep (the FL parcel history shows this is realistic) discards every record for sources that already finished. is_normalize_fresh then sees no entry on the next run and re-normalizes them from scratch. The same long read-modify-write window also means a concurrent targeted run (`--source X` in another terminal saves at preprocess.py line 260) gets its entry clobbered when the long-runner finally saves its stale in-memory copy — Manifest.save() is atomic per write but last-writer-wins across processes.
Why it matters
Hours of completed normalize work become invisible after an interruption, and concurrent CLI invocations silently overwrite each other's manifest entries. No data corruption (outputs on disk are fine, --rebuild-manifest recovers), but it wastes long recompute cycles and makes the manifest unreliable as the freshness source it's meant to be.
How to fix
Call manifest.save() after each run_normalize_one inside the loop (the save is a cheap atomic JSON rewrite), or wrap the loop in try/finally with a save. For the cross-process clobber, a load-merge-save (reload before save and merge entries by source_id) or a simple lock file would close it.
Expected improvement
Interrupted runs resume where they left off instead of re-normalizing everything; concurrent runs stop losing each other's records.
Evidence
runner.py run_normalize_all: `for n in all_normalizers():\n        status, _ = run_normalize_one(...)\n        statuses[n.source_id] = status\n    manifest.save()` — single save after the whole loop
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/scripts/preprocess/runner.py (full file), scripts/preprocess/manifest.py (record_normalize at 251-308, save() at 225-240, is_normalize_fresh at 310-335), scripts/preprocess.py CLI paths (lines 240-290), and scripts/preprocess/pipeline.py (NormalizeAllStep at 127-157). Every load-bearing claim holds in current code: (1) run_normalize_all (runner.py 131-148, cited range exact) loops all_normalizers() calling run_normalize_one and calls manifest.save() once at line 147 after the loop — the quoted evidence matches verbatim. (2) record_normalize only mutates self.normalized in memory; no save inside it or inside run_normalize_one. (3) run_normalize_one's except clause catches Exception only, so KeyboardInterrupt (BaseException) or SIGKILL escapes past line 147 and all completed-in-memory records are lost. (4) is_normalize_fresh returns False when the entry is missing (manifest.py 320-322), so lost records force full re-normalize next run. (5) Cross-process clobber is real: save() serializes the entire in-memory payload via temp+rename — atomic per write but whole-file last-writer-wins; the targeted `--source X` CLI path saves at preprocess.py line 259 (finding said 260 — off by one, trivial), and a long-running sweep that saves later overwrites that entry with its stale loaded copy. The main `run_all` path goes through the same run_normalize_all via NormalizeAllStep (pipeline.py 145), so this is the default `python scripts/preprocess.py` behavior, not an edge path. Corrections/additions: the same end-of-loop-only save pattern also exists in run_compose_all (runner.py line 215) — the fix should cover both; the proposed per-iteration save is sound since save() is a small JSON rewrite. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit: its manifest findings (PERF-031/032/033) concern the puller UI re-statting raw-data bytes against the fetch manifest, a different file and concern, and the audit explicitly lists preprocess manifest.py among files not exhaustively read. The atomic temp+rename intentional design is not being miscounted as an issue — the finding correctly targets persistence timing and cross-process merge, not write atomicity. Severity low is right: outputs on disk stay intact and --rebuild-manifest (preprocess.py --rebuild-manifest, rebuild_from_disk) recovers, so the cost is wasted recompute time and manifest unreliability, not data loss. The FL-parcel-interrupt anecdote in the description is unverifiable from code but non-load-bearing.

Core claim holds: run_normalize_all (runner.py:131-148) records each source outcome in memory only (record_normalize, manifest.py:251-300 mutates self.normalized without persisting) and calls manifest.save() once at line 147 after the whole loop — no try/finally — so an interrupt/OOM mid-sweep discards every completed record, and is_normalize_fresh (manifest.py:320-321) then returns False for missing entries, forcing full re-normalization despite outputs existing on disk. The pipeline NormalizeAllStep (pipeline.py:143-145) just delegates, so run_all has the same exposure. Cross-process claim also checks out: preprocess.py --source saves at line 260 (line number in finding is correct); PreprocessManifest.save() is atomic temp+os.replace but has no reload/merge or lock, so a long sweep's end-of-run save clobbers a concurrent targeted run's entry (last-writer-wins) — though concurrent CLI runs are a speculative workflow, this is the weaker half of the finding. NOT a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit: its manifest findings (e.g., PERF-032) target the puller's fetch manifest / directory_bytes walks in app_puller, and the audit explicitly lists preprocess manifest.py as not exhaustively read. Mitigation exists and is acknowledged in the finding: --rebuild-manifest (rebuild_from_disk, manifest.py:384) recovers from disk in one documented command, and outputs are never corrupted — which is why low severity is correctly calibrated, not inflated. Proposed fix (save per-source inside the loop or try/finally; load-merge-save or lock for the cross-process case) is sound, cheap (small JSON, already atomic), and consistent with the repo's atomic-write convention; no invariant requires a single end-of-run save. Minor scope correction: run_compose_all (runner.py:200-216) has the identical single-save-after-loop pattern (line 215), so the finding slightly understates the affected surface.
lowConfirmed preprocess effort: small

dms_to_decimal abs()'s the degrees and only negates on an explicit S/W direction — a blank/missing direction silently mirrors US coordinates into the eastern hemisphere

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/fcc_dat.py:34-37 · reviewer: preprocess-normalizers
What it is
`val = abs(d) + m/60 + s/3600; if direction in ("S", "W"): val = -val` destroys any sign carried on the degrees field and produces a positive value whenever the direction field is blank, NaN, or lowercase. A US longitude row with a missing/blank direction yields e.g. +92.3 instead of -92.3 — which passes both downstream range checks (fcc_asr.py:294 `-180 <= lon <= 180` and fcc_uls.py:_validate_coords) and is minted as a Point in Asia with coord_status "ok". Neither caller counts or flags rows whose direction field was blank, so there is no way to tell how often this fires.
Why it matters
A wrong-hemisphere point isn't filtered as invalid — it silently lands in the composite and, for ULS locations, is eligible for the spatial carrier-match fallback (it just never matches, masking the data loss as "no nearby tower"). Sign-flipped towers would also render on the map in the wrong hemisphere.
How to fix
Treat a missing/unrecognized direction as unparseable: return None (or return the signed value without abs() when the degrees field itself carries a sign). Normalize direction with .strip().upper() before the membership test, and surface a per-file count of blank-direction rows in the quality report.
Expected improvement
Hemisphere-flipped coordinates become explicit nulls/coord_status='missing' instead of plausible-looking wrong geometries.
Evidence
fcc_dat.py:34-37 `val = abs(d) + m / 60.0 + s / 3600.0\n    if direction in ("S", "W"):\n        val = -val\n    return val`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Verified against current code. (1) Evidence is real and exact: scripts/preprocess/normalizers/fcc_dat.py:34-37 reads `val = abs(d) + m / 60.0 + s / 3600.0; if direction in ("S", "W"): val = -val; return val` — abs() discards any sign on the degrees field and negation happens only on a strict membership test against ("S", "W"). (2) Both call sites confirmed: fcc_asr.py:220-237 passes direction fields raw from read_pipe_dat (which uses na_values=[""], so a blank direction arrives as float NaN — never matches the tuple); fcc_uls.py:421-433 passes direction through safe_str (pandas_coerce.py:42-54), which strips and nulls "nan" but does NOT uppercase, so blank → "" and lowercase "w" both skip negation. Either way a present-degrees/blank-direction US longitude yields a positive value. (3) Downstream checks confirmed exactly as cited: fcc_asr.py:294 is the `-90<=lat<=90 / -180<=lon<=180` gate (a +92.3 lon passes and is minted as Point(lon, lat) at line 298); fcc_uls.py:305-317 _validate_coords returns status "ok" for the same value. (4) No counting/flagging of blank-direction rows in either caller — fcc_asr's coord_errors counts only None/out-of-range; ULS coord_status would be "ok". (5) The why-it-matters claim about the spatial fallback is confirmed: scripts/preprocess/joins/uls_to_asr.py:198 gates the sjoin_nearest proximity fallback on coord_status == "ok", so a hemisphere-mirrored point is eligible and silently never matches. Fix nuance: the proposed alternative "return signed value without abs()" is wrong for this unsigned-degrees format; the right fix is .strip().upper() + return None on unrecognized direction, which routes rows into the already-reported coord_errors/"missing" buckets. Severity low is appropriate — real silent-corruption hazard, low expected incidence.
