eia_860m emits one solar_facility row per GENERATOR (no plant-level dedupe), duplicating facilities and inflating per-parcel solar_count

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/eia_860m.py:56-81 · reviewer: preprocess-normalizers
What it is
The EIA-860M Operating sheet is per-generator (Plant ID + Generator ID), but the loop `for _, r in df.iterrows()` emits a canonical row per sheet row with `facility_id`/`_source_row_id` both taken from Plant ID and no drop_duplicates — so a 5-generator solar farm becomes 5 canonical solar_facility rows at identical coordinates with identical ids. Contrast eia_860.py, which dedupes the Solar sheet on Plant Code before merging (line 66 `drop_duplicates(subset="Plant Code")`).
Why it matters
Duplicate facility rows multiply through SolarToParcelJoin: the hosting parcel's solar_count counts each generator as a separate facility, and any per-facility rollup (county summaries, interestingness components) double-counts. `_source_row_id` also stops being unique, which other normalizers treat as a row identity key.
How to fix
Aggregate to one row per Plant ID before building canonical rows (sum Nameplate Capacity across generators, first() the identity/coordinate columns), or at minimum drop_duplicates on Plant ID and document that capacity is the first generator's only.
Expected improvement
One canonical row per physical solar plant; parcel solar_count and capacity figures stop being inflated by generator multiplicity.
Evidence

eia_860 remote_version granularity is the bare year — in-place revisions of the same reporting year are invisible to staleness detection

scripts/fetch/sources/eia_860.py:96-112 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
remote_version returns `f"{y}"` (e.g. "2024") from _latest_year_url, or the constant string "current" for the CURRENT_URL fallback. base.py's contract is 'Return a string that changes whenever the publisher releases new data.' EIA republishes the same year's eia860{Y}.zip in place at least once (Early Release ~June, Final ~September, plus corrections), so a user who pulled the Early Release will show status ok all year and never be prompted that the Final revision exists. The "current" fallback can additionally never go stale at all. The file's own Last-Modified is available and already used by sibling sources.
Why it matters
The whole point of the check sweep is catching publisher updates; for EIA-860 it can only catch year rollovers, so the solar layer can silently run on superseded Early Release data for months.
How to fix
After _latest_year_url picks the winning URL, combine the year with the file's Last-Modified date (one extra HEAD on the already-chosen URL, or reuse the probe's headers by having _probe_url return them): e.g. `f"{y}/{last_modified_date}"`. Same one-line treatment for the "current" fallback.
Expected improvement
In-place EIA revisions flip the source to stale on the next check instead of never.
Evidence
eia_860.py:104 `return url, f"{y}"` and :107 `return CURRENT_URL, "current"` — version string contains no content-derived component
lowUnverified backend effort: trivial

_write_atomic never fsyncs — its no-corruption guarantee does not hold across a host crash, and a zero-length users.txt fail-closes the boot

backend/app/users.py:148-161 · reviewer: credentials-file-runtime-lifecycle
What it is
_write_atomic writes the temp file via os.fdopen(...).write and immediately os.replace()s it over users.txt, with no f.flush()+os.fsync(fd) on the temp file and no fsync of the parent directory. The docstring claims 'a crash mid-write can never truncate or corrupt the live file' — true for a process crash (the rename is atomic), but not for a machine/power crash: the rename can be journaled before the data blocks reach disk, leaving users.txt present but empty or partial after recovery (ext4's auto_da_alloc heuristic mitigates the replace-via-rename case but is a mount-option heuristic, not a guarantee, and the Fly volume's filesystem options aren't under this repo's control). Because credentials.load treats an empty file as zero entries and config.py's fail-closed boot raises RuntimeError when the file has no valid entry, the post-crash state is the app refusing to start until an operator ssh-es in and recreates the user — with finding 1 meaning there is no fresh copy to restore from.
Why it matters
Fly machines do get host-level crashes and forced migrations; this is exactly the moment the fail-closed posture turns a small durability gap into a full outage of the only login surface. The window is small (a password change must have happened shortly before the crash) but the code's own comment promises a guarantee it doesn't deliver.
How to fix
Before os.replace: f.flush() then os.fsync(fd) inside the os.fdopen block; optionally open the parent directory and fsync it after the replace for rename durability. Three lines, no behavior change on the happy path.
Expected improvement
The atomic-write guarantee actually holds across power loss/host crash; a crash immediately after a password change can no longer leave an empty credentials file that bricks the fail-closed boot.
Evidence
users.py:156-158: `with os.fdopen(fd, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:\n    f.write("\n".join(lines) + ("\n" if lines else ""))\nos.replace(tmp, path)` — no fsync anywhere; docstring line 149: 'a crash mid-write can never truncate or corrupt the live file'.

Error handling & robustness (22)

mediumConfirmed frontend effort: smallfound independently by 2 reviewers

AppStore boot error is unrecoverable: ensureIndex caches the failed promise and bootMap latches mapBooted before success, so the Retry button does nothing

app/src/lib/stores.svelte.ts:115, 128-152, 157-161 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
ensureIndex memoizes `this.indexPromise` and returns it on every subsequent call — including after the inner async IIFE caught a load failure and set `status = 'error'`. The promise is never cleared on failure, so the error-screen Retry button in App.svelte (line 296: `onclick={() => app.boot()}`) just returns the already-settled promise and re-fetches nothing; the error screen persists until a full page reload. Compounding it, bootMap sets `this.mapBooted = true` before awaiting ensureIndex and returns early if the index is missing, so even if the index were retried successfully, towers and parcel-tile probes would never load for the rest of the session.
Why it matters
The only recovery affordance the app offers for a boot failure (data server briefly unreachable, transient network error) is guaranteed to be a no-op; the instruction text tells the user to start the server "then retry", which silently fails.
How to fix
In ensureIndex's catch branch, reset `this.indexPromise = null` so a retry re-runs the load; move `this.mapBooted = true` after the index successfully resolves (or reset it on failure) so bootMap can be re-attempted.
Expected improvement
Retry actually retries; a transient data-server outage no longer requires a hard page reload.
Evidence
private async ensureIndex(): Promise<void> {
  if (this.indexPromise) return this.indexPromise;  // never cleared on error
  ...
  } catch (e) {
    this.status = 'error';
    this.errorMessage = errMsg(e);
  }
...
async bootMap(): Promise<void> {
  if (this.mapBooted) return;
  this.mapBooted = true;          // latched before ensureIndex resolves
  await this.ensureIndex();
  if (!this.index) return;
Verifier notes (2)

(2) The Retry button (App.svelte:296, inside the status==='error' branch at :287) calls app.boot(), which only awaits ensureIndex — guaranteed no-op; only a full reload recovers. (3) bootMap (157-161) sets mapBooted=true before awaiting ensureIndex and never resets it on the !this.index early return, so towers/parcel probes are dead for the session even if the index were somehow retried successfully. Cited line numbers are exact. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (grepped the doc for retry/ensureIndex/indexPromise/mapBooted — no hits; this is error-handling, not perf). Fix suggestion is correct; additionally move the status='loading' reset into the re-runnable path so retry shows progress. Medium severity is right: plausible trigger (data server down at boot, exactly the scenario the error-hint text describes), sole recovery affordance dead, but page reload works.

towers.ts / solar.ts: 'unreachable' probe results are silently treated as 'absent', so a down data server yields an empty map reported as success

app/src/lib/towers.ts:136-144 · reviewer: frontend-lib-bugs
What it is
data.ts deliberately distinguishes probeResource() outcomes: 'absent' (a real 404, degrade silently) vs 'unreachable' (server down/CORS/DNS, "warn ... rather than pretending the resource simply doesn't exist"). But loadTowerPoints() in towers.ts keeps only `=== 'present'` sources and drops both other outcomes identically; loadSolarPoints() in solar.ts (lines 75-79) does the same and additionally swallows GeoJSON load errors with `.catch(() => null)`. If the data server is unreachable when the map first boots, every probe returns 'unreachable', `sources` is empty, the loader resolves with an empty FeatureCollection, and the store marks `towersStatus = 'ready'`. There is no error and no notice — and even when loadTowers() does throw, `app.towersError` is never rendered anywhere in the UI (only `towersStatus === 'loading'` is consumed, App.svelte line 337).
Why it matters
Towers are the primary map layer of the triage product. A partial outage or misconfigured `/data` proxy produces a confidently blank map with status 'ready' — the user has no signal that anything failed, and there is no retry path because 'ready' short-circuits loadTowers() for the rest of the session. The parcel-tile path already sets `app.notice` when zero sources are reachable; the tower/solar paths violate the same contract data.ts documents.
How to fix
Propagate the probe outcome: when every probe returns 'unreachable' (or any probe does while none are 'present'), throw or surface a notice the way bootMap() does for parcel tiles, instead of resolving with an empty collection and 'ready'. Separately, render `app.towersError` somewhere (e.g. the existing notice slot) so a thrown tower load is visible and the error status (which permits a re-run of loadTowers) can actually be retried.
Expected improvement
A down data server produces an actionable on-screen message instead of a silent, permanently empty tower layer.
Evidence
towers.ts: return (await probeResource(url)) === 'present' ? { fips, url } : null;
  ...
  const sources = probes.filter((s): s is TowerSource => s !== null);

data.ts:56: /** ... `unreachable` ... is kept distinct from `absent` (a real 404) so the caller can degrade silently on a genuine 404 but warn when the server is down */

solar.ts: const collection = await loadGeoJson(url).catch(() => null);

All mechanical claims are correct: both loaders collapse 'unreachable' into 'absent' against data.ts's documented contract; empty sources → empty FeatureCollection → towersStatus='ready' locked for the session; towersError is set but never rendered anywhere (grep confirms only stores.svelte.ts references it); loadTowers' sole caller is the mapBooted-guarded bootMap, so the 'error' state also has no retry path. Proposed fix is sound and mirrors the existing parcel-path pattern. Real contract violation, but the common failure mode is signaled via the parcel notice and reload-recoverable, so medium is inflated → low.

Lazy-route {#await import(...)} blocks in App.svelte have no :catch — a failed chunk load renders a silent blank surface

app/src/App.svelte:331-335, 343-355, 363-379 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
MapView, TowerDetail, ExploreList, Settings, Leads, and LeadPage are all mounted via `{#await import('./components/X.svelte') then m}` with no `:catch` branch (and no pending block). If the dynamic import rejects, nothing renders and the rejection is unhandled — the user gets a permanently empty surface with no message. This is the standard post-deploy failure mode for a long-lived SPA tab: the app is explicitly designed to stay open for the session (ADR-061 map persistence; the cofounder's primary daily tool), Vite emits content-hashed chunk filenames, and the production deploy (Fly + Cloudflare) replaces old chunks — an open tab from before a deploy that first visits Leads/Settings after the deploy will 404 the chunk and show nothing.
Why it matters
After every production deploy, any already-open tab can hit a blank Leads/Lead/Settings/Tower page with zero feedback, which reads as data loss to the user; the only fix is a manual reload they're never told to do.
How to fix
Add `{:catch}` blocks rendering a small "This page failed to load — the app was likely updated. Reload to continue." card with a `location.reload()` button (a tiny shared component covers all six sites).
Expected improvement
Stale-deployment chunk failures degrade to an explicit one-click recovery instead of a silent blank screen.
Evidence
{#await import('./components/LeadPage.svelte') then m}
  <m.default displayClusterId={route.key} />
{/await}
— no `{:catch}` on any of the six dynamic-import sites.
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): CONFIRMED. Read app/src/App.svelte (753 lines): exactly six {#await import(...) then m} sites at lines 332 (MapView), 345 (TowerDetail), 351 (ExploreList), 365 (Settings), 371 (Leads), 375 (LeadPage); zero :catch blocks in the file (cited line ranges 331-335/343-355/363-379 are accurate). No mitigation anywhere: no <svelte:boundary>, no unhandledrejection or vite:preloadError listener, no service worker (app/src/main.ts is a bare mount; greps over app/src and index.html came up empty). A rejected import renders nothing and the user gets a silent blank surface. NOT a duplicate of the 42 perf-audit findings — PERF-012 (executed in c47e184) is what INTRODUCED this pattern, and its paste-back work order specifies the await/then shape without :catch; this is a follow-on error-handling gap, not a perf finding. Two corrections: (1) Mechanism detail is slightly wrong — the Caddyfile SPA block uses `try_files {path} /index.html`, so a missing post-deploy chunk under /assets/* returns 200 + index.html, not a 404; the import() then rejects with a module/MIME parse error instead. Same end state (rejected promise, blank surface), so materiality is unchanged. (2) Partial, unreliable mitigation exists: /assets/* are served `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable` behind Cloudflare (orange-cloud), so an already-open tab's browser cache (and sometimes the edge cache) can still satisfy old chunk URLs after a deploy — but only for chunks previously fetched; a tab that first visits Settings/Leads/LeadPage after a deploy (the exact scenario in the finding) has no cached copy and Cloudflare edge caching is per-datacenter/evictable, so the failure mode stands. The proposed fix (shared :catch card with a reload button across all six sites) is correct and low-risk; a window 'vite:preloadError' listener would be a reasonable complement since Vite fires it for preload failures of chunk dependencies. Severity medium is appropriate: daily-driver production tool with active deploys, real silent-failure UX, but recoverable by manual reload and no data loss or security impact.

iter_state_parcel_shards_columns silently drops a whole shard from the owner-cluster pre-pass when any projected column is missing

scripts/preprocess/compose_helpers.py:106-123 · reviewer: preprocess-core
What it is
The column-projected shard iterator passes the requested columns straight to pd.read_parquet: `df = pd.read_parquet(shard_path, columns=cols)`. pyarrow raises when a requested column (e.g. owner_name_norm / mailing_addr_norm on a shard written before those columns existed, or by a future source that lacks one) is absent from the file, and the blanket `except Exception` mislabels that as "skipping unreadable shard" and moves on. The shard is then still fully composed by the main loop (iter_state_parcel_shards reads all columns), so the owner-cluster pre-pass aggregate is computed over a strict subset of the parcels that get written. facets.py already solves this correctly by intersecting with the file schema first (`present = [c for c in columns if c in pf.schema_arrow.names]`).
Why it matters
Silent correctness degradation rather than a loud failure: owner_cluster_size and owner_cluster_value_total understate every cluster touching the skipped shard, and clusters living entirely inside it fall back to size 1 / value 0 at annotate time (owner_cluster.annotate's no-match default). cluster.normalized_owner_cols has a per-row fallback specifically so frames missing the *_norm columns still resolve — but this read path never gets that far because the read itself throws.
How to fix
Mirror facets.py: open ParquetFile(shard_path), intersect the wanted columns with schema_arrow.names, read the present subset, and let cluster.normalized_owner_cols's raw-column fallback handle the absent ones. Keep the except for genuinely corrupt files, but log a distinct message when the failure is a column mismatch.
Expected improvement
Owner-cluster aggregates stay correct across heterogeneous/older shards; a schema mismatch becomes a handled case instead of a mislabeled warning.
Evidence
compose_helpers.py: `df = pd.read_parquet(shard_path, columns=cols)\n            except Exception as e:  # noqa: BLE001\n                log.warning("skipping unreadable shard %s: %s", shard_path, e)` vs facets.py: `present = [c for c in columns if c in pf.schema_arrow.names]`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Verified in scripts/preprocess/compose_helpers.py (precise lines 113-117, finding's 106-123 spans the whole function). Reproduced the failure mode with the project venv: pd.read_parquet(path, columns=[...]) raises ArrowInvalid for a missing column, so the blanket except logs "skipping unreadable shard" and drops it from the owner-cluster pre-pass. Confirmed the divergence: compose.py:_build_owner_cluster_lookup (97-114) uses the columns iterator while the compose chunk loop (compose.py:330) uses the full-read iterator, so the shard is still composed; owner_cluster.annotate defaults unmatched clusters to size 1 / value 0 (annotators/owner_cluster.py:119-120). The raw-column fallback in cluster.normalized_owner_cols (cluster.py:140-148) plus OWNER_COLS deliberately carrying raw fallback columns shows missing _norm columns were meant to be tolerated — the projected read defeats that, so this is not intentional-by-design. Proposed fix is correct and mirrors facets.py:143's schema_arrow intersection (keep state_fips mandatory — line 119 indexes it; the full-read iterator already special-cases that). Not a duplicate of the 42 perf-audit findings (PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md never mentions this iterator or column mismatches), and no test covers iter_state_parcel_shards_columns. Severity correction medium→low: the bug is latent, not active — every parcel source routes through state_parcels.py which writes all five OWNER_COLS unconditionally (lines 398-414), and the _norm columns have existed since the repo's baseline commit (51ad5cb), so no shard produced by current code triggers it; genuinely corrupt shards are skipped consistently by both iterators (no divergence). The realistic trigger is future OWNER_COLS growth over stale shards — a real pattern here (PERF-028 added geometry_valid the same way and compose.py:_valid_parcel_mask explicitly tolerates stale shards lacking it), which keeps this worth fixing but below medium today. Could not inspect data/ (permission denied) to rule out pre-baseline stale shards on disk.

arc_distressed silently drops rows with unparseable FIPS and silently maps unknown tier labels to "" — normalize-time filtering with no status column or warning

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/arc_distressed.py:67-86 · reviewer: preprocess-normalizers
What it is
Two silent failure modes in the canonical loop: (1) `if not fips: continue` drops any workbook row whose FIPS doesn't parse, with no counter, no log, no quality-report warning, and no status column — a normalize-time row drop, which the pipeline invariant says should instead be kept and flagged; (2) `tier = _TIER_ALIASES.get(tier_raw, "")` maps any tier string not in the five known aliases to empty, so `arc_distressed`/`arc_at_risk` become False. If ARC renames a label in a future FY workbook (e.g. adds an asterisk or changes "At-Risk" formatting beyond the two handled spellings), every affected county silently loses its distress tier and compose proceeds normally. The USDA normalizer has the same silent-FIPS-drop shape (`grouped[grouped["fips_canon"].notna()]`, usda_persistent_poverty.py:78).
Why it matters
ARC economic status is a core distress input to lead scoring. Upstream format drift — the known failure mode for this fragile xlsx source — would degrade the data to all-blank tiers without any signal, the exact "silently work around problems" pattern the repo conventions forbid. Dropped FIPS rows are likewise invisible (NormalizeResult.canonical_rows differs from raw_rows, but nothing surfaces why).
How to fix
Count FIPS-parse failures and unknown tier labels during the loop; append both to the quality report's warnings (the parameter already exists and is used by other normalizers) and log.warning with the distinct unmatched tier strings. Optionally keep unparseable rows with county_fips=None plus a parse_status column instead of `continue`, consistent with the keep-all-rows posture.
Expected improvement
ARC label drift or FIPS format changes become a loud quality-report warning on the next preprocess run instead of a silent scoring degradation.
Evidence
arc_distressed.py:72-74 `tier = _TIER_ALIASES.get(tier_raw, "")` / `if not fips:` / `continue`; quality_report called with no warnings: `qr = quality_report(self.source_id, {"raw": df, "county_scores": scores})`

Not a perf-audit duplicate — PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md explicitly lists arc_distressed and usda_persistent_poverty as "NOT exhaustively read". fcc_asr swallows d_tower failures with a bare except-pass, can promote a half-extracted d_tower/ directory as status ok, and never logs despite claiming to

scripts/fetch/sources/fcc_asr.py:100-114 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
The secondary d_tower.zip block wraps download AND extraction in `try: … except Exception: pass` with the comment 'Determinations are optional for the prototype — log and continue'. Nothing is logged (source modules have no logger and fetch.log only sees puller-level events). Worse, the failure window is not only the download: if `safe_extract_zip(d_zip, dest_dir / "d_tower")` raises midway (truncated zip that passed expected_min_bytes, disk full), the partially extracted `d_tower/` directory and the leftover `d_tower.zip` remain in .partial, are NOT added to `files` (so validate never inspects them), and promote_partial moves them into current/ where the manifest records status ok. The module docstring also misnames the file ('a_tower.zip — applications') while the code fetches d_tower.zip.
Why it matters
A user gets a green 'Pulled' result with either silently-missing FAA determinations or a corrupt half-extracted d_tower/ tree in current/ that the preprocess layer may rglob into. There is no breadcrumb anywhere (fetch.log, manifest.error, UI) that the optional half failed — exactly the 'silently work around problems in ways that hide them' pattern the project rules forbid.
How to fix
On exception: (1) clean up `d_zip` and `shutil.rmtree(dest_dir / "d_tower", ignore_errors=True)` so a partial extraction never gets promoted; (2) record the failure — simplest is appending a `d_tower_FAILED.txt` marker or returning the error in FetchResult so fetch_one can log_event it; at minimum re-raise non-network errors. Fix the docstring's a_tower/d_tower mismatch while there.

current/ can never contain a torn d_tower extraction labeled ok, and an operator can see from fetch.log that the determinations file was skipped this week.

Reserved activity channels ('status'/'flag'/'dismiss') are unvalidated on POST /attempts and duplicated as magic literals across five backend modules + the frontend

backend/app/main.py:436-456 · reviewer: cross-duplication
What it is
The backend reserves three sentinel channel values for machine-written ContactAttempt rows: 'status' (auto-logged transitions, lead_store.py), 'flag' (pursue trigger, lead_store.latest_flags matches `ContactAttempt.channel == "flag"` at line 114), and 'dismiss' (attention bookkeeping, attention_service.py:29 DISMISS_CHANNEL). But log_attempt only validates `if not body.channel.strip()` and direction — it accepts a client-supplied channel='status', 'flag', or 'dismiss'. The sentinel set is also re-spelled independently in main.py:456 `("status", "flag", "dismiss")`, stats_service.py:23 `_NON_TOUCH = {"status", "flag", "dismiss"}`, activity_service.py:22 `_EXCLUDED_CHANNELS = {"dismiss"}` plus the `("status", "flag")` tuple at line 125, attention_service.py:29, and in the frontend (LeadPage.svelte:128/136 `a.channel === 'status'` / `'flag'`). The legitimate channel vocabulary (phone/mail/email/text/in-person) exists only client-side in lead-attempt.ts CHANNELS.
Why it matters
Any authenticated user can POST channel='flag' with outcome='<anything>' and overwrite a Lead's trigger_asr everywhere it renders (latest_flags keys purely on the channel string), or POST channel='dismiss' to silently clear that Lead's unhandled-reply attention item while the row stays invisible in History/Calendar (excluded) and uncounted in stats — corrupting the tenant's own derived state with no trace in any user-facing surface. Separately, five hand-rolled copies of the sentinel set will drift the first time a fourth sentinel is added (the digest's `("status", "flag")` at activity_service.py:125 already omits 'dismiss' only because the stream pre-excluded it).
How to fix
Define RESERVED_CHANNELS = frozenset({'status', 'flag', 'dismiss'}) once (e.g. next to the append logic in lead_store.py or a small channels.py), import it in main.py/stats_service/activity_service/attention_service, and reject reserved channels in log_attempt with a 422 (mirroring the existing direction check at main.py:438-439).
Expected improvement

Litestream death is invisible: after 10 start retries supervisord marks it FATAL and the machine keeps serving with backups silently stopped

supervisord.conf:36-44 · reviewer: deploy-security
What it is
All three programs have autorestart=true and startretries=10, with no eventlistener for PROCESS_STATE_FATAL. The Fly health check (fly.toml lines 58-63) only probes /api/health through Caddy→uvicorn, so a dead Caddy or uvicorn eventually restarts the machine — but a dead Litestream affects nothing the check sees. If Litestream enters FATAL (e.g. a missing/rotated LITESTREAM_* secret makes it exit quickly 10 times in a row, or R2 credentials are revoked), the app keeps serving normally with zero replication, and nothing surfaces it. litestream.yml expands ${LITESTREAM_BUCKET} etc. from env, so a single unset secret after an ops change is a realistic trigger for exactly this fast-crash loop.
Why it matters
The write-layer SQLite is the 'one real risk' the hosting ADR called out; the failure mode here is the worst kind — backups stop while everything looks healthy, and the gap is only discovered when a restore is needed.
How to fix
Add a supervisord eventlistener subscribed to PROCESS_STATE_FATAL that kills supervisord (container exits → Fly restarts the machine, which also surfaces the crash loop in `fly status`). Alternatively or additionally, have /api/health check Litestream liveness (e.g. probe its metrics port or check the process), so the existing Fly check covers it.
Expected improvement
A dead replication pipeline becomes a visible, self-healing machine restart instead of a silent durability gap.
Evidence
supervisord.conf: `[program:litestream] command=litestream replicate -config /etc/litestream.yml autostart=true autorestart=true startretries=10` — no eventlistener section in the file. fly.toml: `[[http_service.checks]] ... path = "/api/health"` is the only check.
mediumUnverified frontend effort: small

duckdb.ts loadTowerPoints hard-codes the join-produced optional column parcel_owner — one state's towers.parquet missing it aborts the tower layer for ALL states

app/src/lib/duckdb.ts:191-200 · reviewer: builder-to-browser-artifact-contract
What it is
The map's tower query selects `CAST(parcel_owner AS VARCHAR) AS owner` from every state's towers.parquet, but parcel_owner is not a normalizer column — it is grafted on at compose time by the tower→parcel sjoin rename (compose.py:121-126 `"owner_name": "parcel_owner"`) via `_apply_lookup`, which returns the frame unchanged when the lookup is empty: `if infra is None or len(infra) == 0 or not lookup: return infra` (compose.py:198-199). A state composed with towers but zero tower→parcel matches (parcels not yet fetched/normalized — exactly the NC re-onboarding path, or a wholesale sjoin failure) writes towers.parquet with no parcel_owner column at all. The frontend probes the file for existence only (towers.ts:136-141), then joins all per-state SELECTs with `UNION ALL` into one query (duckdb.ts:200), so a binder error on one branch fails the whole statement — loadTowerPoints throws and AppStore.loadTowers sets towersStatus='error' (stores.svelte.ts:188-191). The Python consumer of the same file is deliberately tolerant (`wanted = [c for c in TOWER_ATTR_COLUMNS if c in cols]`, owners_index.py:544); the browser consumer is not.
Why it matters
One partially-composed state silently present in index.json removes every tower from the map and the Explore list — the app's primary layer — with an error that points at DuckDB rather than at the offending state's artifact. The duckdb.ts docstring itself acknowledges the blast radius ('a state whose query fails aborts the union') but nothing prevents it.
How to fix
Make the per-state SELECT schema-tolerant: read each registered file's schema first (DuckDB `parquet_schema` or `DESCRIBE SELECT * FROM parquet_scan(...)`) and emit `NULL AS owner` when parcel_owner is absent; or run each state's query separately and drop failing states (mirroring the probe-and-degrade posture of loadSolarPoints). Alternatively fix the producer: have compose always create the parcel_* columns (as nulls) on the tower table even when the lookup is empty.
Expected improvement
A degraded state yields 'fewer towers with unknown owners' instead of an empty map across all anchors.
Evidence
duckdb.ts:192 `CAST(parcel_owner AS VARCHAR) AS owner,` … :200 `conn.query(selects.join('\nUNION ALL\n'))`; compose.py:198 `if infra is None or len(infra) == 0 or not lookup:\n        return infra`; owners_index.py:544 `wanted = [c for c in TOWER_ATTR_COLUMNS if c in cols]`

sync_composite.sync() swallows rclone's error output on failure — operator gets a bare CalledProcessError with zero diagnostics

scripts/sync_composite.py:105-107 · reviewer: composite-publish-seam
What it is
sync() runs `subprocess.run(cmd, capture_output=True, text=True, check=True)`. On a non-zero rclone exit, CalledProcessError propagates uncaught through main(), and because stderr was captured, the traceback shows only "Command '[rclone, ...]' returned non-zero exit status N" — every rclone JSON-log error line explaining *what* failed (which file, auth error, network timeout, R2 throttle) is held in the un-printed `e.stderr`. `_parse_stats` runs only on success, so there is no partial-state report either: when leg 2 dies halfway, the operator knows neither how many files transferred before the failure nor which leg's destination is now mixed-generation. The test suite (tests/test_sync_composite.py) covers only the happy-path filter/mirror contract — no failure-path test exists.
Why it matters
deploy.sh aborts via set -e at the first failed leg, leaving R2 updated but the volume not (or half-updated), and the only signal is an opaque Python traceback. Diagnosing requires re-running rclone by hand inside the machine — exactly the kind of friction that produces the 'just re-run it and hope' operations the gotcha notes already hint at.
How to fix
Wrap the subprocess call: on CalledProcessError, print the last ~20 lines of e.stderr (the rclone error log) plus whatever stats line exists, then exit with rclone's return code. Optionally always print the parsed transferred/deleted counts even on failure so the operator can see how far the leg got.
Expected improvement
A failed publish tells the operator what broke and how far it got, instead of requiring an SSH session and a manual rclone re-run to find out.
Evidence
sync_composite.py:105: "proc = subprocess.run(cmd, capture_output=True, text=True, check=True)" — no try/except anywhere in the file; main() at 137 calls sync() bare.

PUT /api/settings commits auto_followup_enabled before validating window_days — partial write persists on a 422 response

backend/app/main.py:750-759 · reviewer: backend-bugs
What it is
update_tenant_settings applies the enabled flag first (`settings.set(AUTO_FOLLOWUP_ENABLED, ...)` — SettingsStore.set commits immediately) and only then validates `auto_followup_window_days >= 1`, raising 422 afterwards. A request carrying both fields with an invalid window (e.g. {"auto_followup_enabled": true, "auto_followup_window_days": 0}) returns 422 to the client but has already flipped and committed the enabled flag — the response says nothing was accepted while half the update persisted.
Why it matters
An error response should leave state untouched; here the client's settings UI will believe the save failed and render stale values while the backend silently changed behavior (the auto-followup rule turning on/off affects task creation on every subsequent lead touch).
How to fix
Validate all fields before any settings.set call (move the `< 1` check above the first set), or batch both sets and commit once.
Expected improvement
422 responses are side-effect free; the settings surface can trust that a failed save changed nothing.
Evidence
main.py: `if body.auto_followup_enabled is not None:\n        settings.set(AUTO_FOLLOWUP_ENABLED, \"1\" if body.auto_followup_enabled else \"0\")\n    if body.auto_followup_window_days is not None:\n        if body.auto_followup_window_days < 1:\n            raise HTTPException(status_code=422, ...)`

Line citation 750-759 is accurate; evidence snippet matches verbatim. Fix suggestion is sound; an even simpler alternative is `Field(default=None, ge=1)` on the schema field so FastAPI rejects before the handler runs. OwnersIndexStep runs even when AppAssetsStep failed, baking stale parcels_attr.parquet into a fresh-timestamped owners_index.json

scripts/preprocess/pipeline.py:221-243 · reviewer: preprocess-core
What it is
The owners index is built from the facets output (owners_index.py: "Reads ``parcels_attr.parquet`` (the facets output) ... so ``--app-assets`` must have run first"). In the pipeline, AppAssetsStep is AUXILIARY — a failure is logged at WARNING and the run continues — and OwnersIndexStep.is_applicable only checks `bool(outcome.composed_ok)`, not whether app_assets succeeded. So when facets generation fails after a recompose, the owners index is rebuilt from the *previous* run's parcels_attr.parquet but stamped with a fresh generated_at, and the backend serves Lead data that silently lags the new composites (scores, carriers, holdings).
Why it matters
The one consumer-facing cross-state artifact can look freshly regenerated while actually reflecting the prior compose — the exact 'stale index must still serve' contract exists for graceful degradation, but here the staleness is invisible because the timestamp says otherwise.
How to fix
Track app-assets success on RunOutcome (e.g. outcome.app_assets_ok set by AppAssetsStep.run) and have OwnersIndexStep.is_applicable require it; or have AppAssetsStep record per-state facets failures and skip the index when any composed_ok state's facets failed. Alternatively, embed each parcels_attr.parquet's mtime/compose timestamp into owners_index.json so staleness is at least detectable.
Expected improvement
A facets failure can no longer produce a misleadingly fresh owners index; the dependency between the two auxiliary steps is enforced rather than implied by ordering.
Evidence
pipeline.py OwnersIndexStep: `def is_applicable(self, outcome: RunOutcome) -> bool:\n        return bool(outcome.composed_ok)`; owners_index.py build_owners_index docstring: "so ``--app-assets`` must have run first"; Pipeline.run: auxiliary failures only `log.warning(msg)` and continue

No mitigation found: the backend (OwnersIndexService) does no freshness validation, and the "stale index must still serve" invariant in CLAUDE.md §8 is schema-field tolerance, not timestamp truthfulness — the OwnersIndexStep docstring only reasons about its OWN failure leaving a detectably-old index, not the inverse (stale inputs, fresh output). Proposed fixes are sound, but only the per-state variant (track facets_failed on the outcome and skip/flag the index when any composed_ok state's facets failed) covers the dominant path; gating solely on step-level app_assets success would miss per-state failures since the step reports ok. Embedding per-state parcels_attr mtimes (or compose timestamps) in owners_index.json is a good detectability fallback either way.

Inconsistent validation-error mapping: unknown lead status returns 409, unknown task status/priority returns 422; update_task duplicates identical except clauses

backend/app/main.py:406-408, 671-678 · reviewer: backend-quality
What it is
A malformed enum value is mapped differently per resource: lead_status.validate_transition raises InvalidTransition for both 'unknown status' and 'illegal transition', and update_lead maps both to 409 Conflict (main.py:406-408) — so sending status='contactedd' (a typo, a request-shape error) yields 409, while the equivalent typo on a task ('unknown task status') yields 422 via InvalidTaskStatus. In update_task, the two except clauses for InvalidTaskStatus and InvalidTaskPriority have byte-identical bodies (main.py:673-676).
Why it matters
Clients can't rely on a consistent status-code contract for the same class of mistake (422 = fix your request vs 409 = re-fetch state and retry), and the frontend's error handling has to special-case per resource. The duplicate except clauses are trivial noise but invite a third copy.
How to fix
Split the unknown-status case in update_lead: validate `body.status in STATUSES` first (422 on failure, matching tasks), keeping 409 only for genuine ladder violations — or raise a distinct UnknownStatus error from lead_status. Collapse the task excepts to `except (InvalidTaskStatus, InvalidTaskPriority) as exc:`.
Expected improvement
One predictable rule — malformed value = 422, legal-value-illegal-state = 409 — across leads and tasks, plus four fewer duplicated lines.
Evidence
lead_status.py:75-76: `if not is_valid_status(to): raise InvalidTransition(f"unknown status {to!r}")` mapped by main.py:406-408 to `HTTPException(status_code=409, ...)`; main.py:673-676: `except InvalidTaskStatus as exc: raise HTTPException(status_code=422, detail=str(exc)) from exc` followed by an identical InvalidTaskPriority clause
Verifier notes (2)

promote_partial has a kill-window where current/ ceases to exist, violating storage.py's stated previous-good-or-new-good guarantee, with no startup recovery for stranded .old

scripts/fetch/storage.py:58-92 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
promote_partial does two sequential renames: `cur.rename(old)` then `part.rename(cur)`. The OSError rollback handles in-process failures, but a process kill (or power loss) between the two renames leaves NO current/ — the previous-good copy is stranded in `.old` and the new copy still sits in `.partial`. The module docstring promises 'current/ either contains the previous-good copy or the new-good copy — never a half-finished one', which this window breaks. Nothing at startup looks for a stranded `.old` to restore; the next fetch of that source even deletes it (`if old.exists(): shutil.rmtree(old)`) before its own promote, and for non-resumable sources prepare_fresh_partial wipes `.partial` too — so after a crash the only recovery is a full refetch, while the manifest still lists the old files as status ok.
Why it matters
The window is microseconds for the rename pair, but the consequence is a source directory that the preprocess pipeline suddenly finds empty while the manifest says ok — a confusing state with no breadcrumb, and for the ~10 GB NC fetch the 'just refetch' recovery costs an hour.
How to fix
Add cheap crash recovery at puller startup (or at the top of promote_partial): if `current/` is missing but `.old` exists, rename `.old` back to `current/` before doing anything else. That single check restores the documented invariant after any kill in the window.
Expected improvement
The previous-good-or-new-good guarantee survives process kills; a stranded .old self-heals instead of being deleted on the next run.
Evidence

Verifier notes (1)

Confirmed: (1) promote_partial (lines 58-92, cited range exact) does the two sequential renames at lines 79-81 (`cur.rename(old)` then `part.rename(cur)`); a kill between them leaves no current/, previous-good stranded in .old, new-good in .partial. Proposed fix (restore .old → current/ at startup or top of promote_partial when current/ is missing) is sound; restoring .partial instead would be even better since it was already validated, but either restores the documented invariant. Severity low is appropriate: microsecond window, dev-tooling context, hand-recoverable.

Unguarded manifest.save() inside worker run() loops — a disk error silently aborts remaining pulls while the UI reports completion

app_puller/workers.py:89-120 · reviewer: puller-review
What it is
FetchWorker.run wraps the per-source fetch in `fetch_one`'s broad exception handling, but the `self._manifest.save()` call at the end of each loop iteration sits outside any per-source guard: if it raises (disk full, permission change on data/), the exception escapes run(), PySide prints a traceback to stderr, and the `finally: self.all_done.emit()` makes the UI tear down normally — '_on_fetch_all_done' logs 'All pulls complete.' even though the remaining queued sources were never fetched and the manifest wasn't persisted. CheckWorker has the same shape: `fut.result()` (line 49) and `self._manifest.save()` (line 59) can raise out of the loop with only the bare `all_done` in the finally, so the sweep ends looking finished with no error surfaced.
Why it matters
The failure mode is silent in exactly the way the worker design tries to avoid (fetch_one's comment: 'never let one source kill the whole UI'): a 'Pull all missing' over 10 sources that dies on source 3's save shows a clean completion, and the user only finds out when the data isn't there.
How to fix
Add an `error = Signal(str)` (or reuse one_done with a synthetic failure) and wrap the loop body — or at minimum the save() — in try/except that emits the error to the UI log before the finally; same for CheckWorker's result/save path.
Expected improvement
Worker-level failures become visible in the activity log instead of only on stderr, and a partial bulk pull is clearly reported as partial.
Evidence
                else:
                    self.one_done.emit(s.id, False, outcome.error or "Failed.")
                self._manifest.save()
        finally:
            self.all_done.emit()

Manifest.load silently discards a corrupt manifest and the next save() permanently overwrites it

scripts/fetch/manifest.py:144-149 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
On JSONDecodeError/OSError, Manifest.load returns a fresh empty Manifest ('Corrupt manifest — start fresh rather than crash the UI'). Because the puller saves after every check sweep and every fetch (workers.py calls manifest.save() routinely), the corrupt-but-possibly-recoverable file is overwritten with the empty state within seconds of opening the app, destroying all fetched_at/version/cadence history for ~14 sources with no log line or UI notice. save() itself is atomic, so corruption implies an external cause (manual edit, disk issue) — exactly the case where preserving the original for inspection matters.
Why it matters
Freshness badges, staleness detection, and 'what needs my attention' all derive from manifest history; silently zeroing it means every source shows unknown/missing until manually re-verified, and the operator never learns why. The data/ tree is fine, but the metadata loss is irreversible once the first save lands.
How to fix
In the except branch, rename the unreadable file to `manifest.json.corrupt-<timestamp>` before returning the fresh Manifest, and emit a log_event so fetch.log records the reset. _detect_manual_installs will then rebuild entries from disk as it does today.
Expected improvement
A corrupt manifest becomes a diagnosable event with a preserved artifact instead of a silent total reset of fetch history.
Evidence
manifest.py:147-149 `except (json.JSONDecodeError, OSError):\n            # Corrupt manifest — start fresh rather than crash the UI.\n            return cls()`

POST /api/me/password returns a bare 500 on any credentials-file write failure (read-only volume, ENOSPC)

backend/app/main.py:314-333 · reviewer: credentials-file-runtime-lifecycle
What it is
The route catches only UsersFileError (→ 422) and maps WRONG_CURRENT → 401. Any OSError from _write_atomic — PermissionError from mkstemp if /data is read-only or remounted ro, ENOSPC during f.write, or a failed os.replace — escapes the handler entirely and surfaces as an unhandled exception → generic 500, after the user's current password already verified. I confirmed the failure leaves no inconsistency (the `except BaseException` in _write_atomic unlinks the temp file, the live file is untouched by os.replace's atomicity, and the CredentialsFile (mtime_ns, size) signature therefore stays valid — the speculated cache/disk disagreement does not occur). But contrast with `credentials.load`, which deliberately logs-and-degrades on OSError for the read side; the write side has no equivalent handling, no specific log line, and the operator gets nothing distinguishing 'credentials write failed' from any other 500.
Why it matters
On a single-machine prod box where /data filling up or going read-only is a realistic Fly-volume failure mode, the first symptom would be users reporting 'password change shows an error' with only a generic traceback in fly logs — and a full volume also means Litestream is failing simultaneously, so a clear, specific signal from this path matters.
How to fix
In change_my_password, catch OSError around users_change_password, log it with the credentials path (mirroring the credentials.load warning style), and return a 503 with a non-leaky detail like 'credential store temporarily unavailable'.
Expected improvement
A failed write becomes a diagnosable, correctly-classified service error (503 + a specific log line) instead of an anonymous 500, with the user told to retry rather than shown a crash.
Evidence
main.py:323-333: `try: outcome = users_change_password(...) except UsersFileError as exc: raise HTTPException(status_code=422, ...)` — no OSError handling; users.py:159-161: `except BaseException: Path(tmp).unlink(missing_ok=True); raise` re-raises the OSError to the route.

scripts/preprocess/tiles.py:79-82 · reviewer: builder-to-browser-artifact-contract
What it is

lowUnverified deploy effort: small

Leg 2 (R2 → volume) runs tethered to the operator's interactive fly ssh session — a laptop sleep or network blip kills the multi-minute mirror mid-flight

deploy.sh:95-101 · reviewer: composite-publish-seam
What it is
sync_data runs the in-machine leg as `fly ssh console --app "${FLY_APP}" -C "${remote_cmd}"` (deploy.sh:101). The remote python/rclone process is a child of the SSH session: if the operator's connection drops, the laptop sleeps, or the terminal closes during the multi-GB R2→volume transfer, the remote command is killed — producing exactly the 'volume partial' state DEPLOYMENT.md:373-375 warns about, through a vector the 'Don't restart mid-sync' gotcha doesn't cover. There is no nohup/detach, no remote log file to check afterward, and (per the previous findings) no completion marker to tell a killed run from a finished one.
Why it matters
The fragility window for a partial publish isn't just deliberate restarts — it includes ordinary connectivity hiccups on the operator's side during the longest leg of the publish. Combined with the absent completion marker, the operator may not even know it happened.
How to fix
Detach the remote leg from the session: `fly ssh console -C "sh -c 'nohup python3 /srv/scripts/sync_composite.py ... > /data/sync.log 2>&1 & echo started'"` and poll the log/exit-status file from deploy.sh, or run leg 2 as a supervisord-managed one-shot triggered by a touch file. The doc's 'a re-run repairs a partial' stays true either way, but the run stops depending on the operator's wifi.
Expected improvement
A publish completes (or fails with a readable on-volume log) regardless of the operator's connection, shrinking the partial-volume window to genuine machine failures.
Evidence
deploy.sh:97-101: "local remote_cmd=\"python3 /srv/scripts/sync_composite.py ${R2_REMOTE} ${VOLUME_DEST}\" ... fly ssh console --app \"${FLY_APP}\" -C \"${remote_cmd}\"" — foreground, session-bound, no log redirection.

CLI add against a mistyped --file/APP_USERS_FILE path silently creates a brand-new credentials file

backend/app/users.py:141-145, 53-64 · reviewer: credentials-file-runtime-lifecycle
What it is
_read_lines returns [] for a missing file, and add_user happily appends to that empty list and _write_atomic mkdir-parents + creates the file. So an operator on the live machine who typos the path (`python -m app.users --file /data/user.txt add ...`) or runs the CLI in a shell where APP_USERS_FILE didn't propagate (DEFAULT_FILE then resolves to /srv/backend/users.txt inside the image) gets a cheerful 'added <user> to team <team> in <path>' while the real /data/users.txt is untouched — the new user can't log in, and nothing on screen flags that a second credentials file now exists on the ephemeral container layer. The mutating commands that require an existing user (remove, set-password, set-team) fail safe with 'no user' errors; only add has this silent-create behavior, which is also load-bearing for legitimate bootstrap (config.py's first-deploy flow).
Why it matters
On the production machine the failure is confusing rather than dangerous (the stray file in the container is discarded on redeploy), but the operator burns time debugging 'I added the user and they still get 401' — and a stray file containing a real password hash can linger at an unintended path.
How to fix
Have the CLI print a distinct notice when add is about to create a file that does not yet exist (e.g. 'creating new credentials file at <path>'), so a typo'd path is visible at the moment it happens while keeping the bootstrap flow one command.
Expected improvement
Path mistakes during live user management surface immediately instead of as unexplained 401s, with zero friction added to the documented first-deploy bootstrap.
Evidence
users.py:142-144: `if not path.is_file(): return []` (in _read_lines) and add_user → `_write_atomic` → `path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)` + mkstemp/replace creates the file; users.py:36: `DEFAULT_FILE = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "users.txt"` (the in-image /srv/backend path when APP_USERS_FILE is absent).

mediumConfirmed fetch effort: small

remote_version() probe-walkers (eia_860m up to 48 requests, arc_distressed up to 8 full HTML GETs, eia_860 up to 4 HEADs) violate the documented one-cheap-request contract

scripts/fetch/sources/eia_860m.py:66-85 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
The contract (base.py docstring + scripts/fetch/AGENTS.md: 'remote_version() must be cheap (one HEAD / one small JSON GET) — slow probes block the parallel "Check for updates" sweep') is violated by three sources. eia_860m._latest_month_url walks back up to 24 months × 2 URL patterns = 48 sequential HEAD probes (`for _ in range(24): … for pattern in (CURRENT_PATTERN, ARCHIVE_PATTERN)`), each with a 15 s timeout; typical case is ~4-6 probes (2-month publication lag), but when eia.gov is unreachable-by-timeout the single check takes up to ~12 minutes. arc_distressed._latest probes up to 4 fiscal years × 2 page patterns = 8 full HTML page GETs (not HEADs) via _xlsx_on_page. eia_860._latest_year_url probes up to 4 HEADs. The check sweep runs check_one on a ThreadPoolExecutor(8) (app_puller/workers.py:41) and only completes when every future finishes — cancellation cannot interrupt a running probe — so one degraded publisher stalls the whole sweep and the UI's all_done. (Distinct from PERF-041, which covered only the duplicate probe at the top of fetch().)
Why it matters
EIA placeholder pages and URL migrations are a documented fragile point (CLAUDE.md §6); precisely when EIA misbehaves, 'Check for updates' degrades from seconds to minutes with no way to cancel the in-flight probes, and the manifest's last_full_check stamp is held hostage by one source.
How to fix
Bound the walkers: cap eia_860m at ~6 months back (publication lag is ~2 months; raise after that — the source is genuinely broken then), and short-circuit the walk on the first connection-level failure (distinguish a 404/placeholder from a network timeout: a timeout on month N will also time out on month N-1, so stop probing). For ARC, HEAD the FY page first and only GET on 200, and try only the current+next FY before falling back to the manifest's last-known version with check_failed.
Expected improvement
Worst-case 'Check for updates' time for a degraded EIA goes from ~12 minutes to ~30-45 seconds; the sweep stays responsive and the cheapness contract holds again.
Evidence
eia_860m.py:74-76 `for _ in range(24):  # up to 2 years back\n        m_name = MONTHS[month - 1]\n        for pattern in (CURRENT_PATTERN, ARCHIVE_PATTERN):`; arc_distressed.py:99-101 `for fy in (cy + 1, cy, cy - 1, cy - 2):\n            for page_pat in (FY_PAGE_PATTERN, LEGACY_PAGE_PATTERN):\n                xlsx = self._xlsx_on_page(page_pat.format(fy=fy))`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Verified every element of the claim against current code. (1) Contract: scripts/fetch/base.py:97 ("Implementations should be cheap — typically a single HEAD request") and scripts/fetch/AGENTS.md:25 ("remote_version() must be cheap (one HEAD / one small JSON GET) — slow probes [block the parallel sweep]") both state the contract. (2) eia_860m: /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/scripts/fetch/sources/eia_860m.py:66-85 — `_latest_month_url` does `for _ in range(24)` × `for pattern in (CURRENT_PATTERN, ARCHIVE_PATTERN)` = up to 48 sequential `_is_real_file` → `head()` probes; `remote_version()` (line 128-130) calls it directly. http.py:26 sets PROBE_TIMEOUT = 15s, so the all-timeouts worst case is indeed ~48×15s ≈ 12 min; `_is_real_file` swallows HttpError → False (eia_860m.py:62-63), so timeouts do NOT short-circuit the walk — the ~12-minute scenario is real, though it requires timeout-style failure (fast 404s/refusals would walk quickly). (3) arc_distressed: arc_distressed.py:95-104 — 4 FYs × 2 page patterns = up to 8 calls to `_xlsx_on_page`, each a full HTML GET via `fetch_text` (http.py:94-102, GET not HEAD, PROBE_TIMEOUT 15s, up to 5 MB body). Corrections: minor — workers.py line is 40 (executor) / 17 (parallelism constant), not 41; and the worst case is conditional on timeout-type failure (the finding states this correctly). Severity medium is fair: desktop puller only, but EIA placeholder/URL fragility is a documented known failure mode (CLAUDE.md §6), making the degraded path realistic rather than theoretical. Suggested fix in the finding is sound; one addition: `_is_real_file`/`_xlsx_on_page` must stop swallowing HttpError uniformly to distinguish 404 from timeout, since that conflation is what makes the walk unboundedly slow.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read scripts/fetch/sources/eia_860m.py, arc_distressed.py, eia_860.py in full, plus scripts/fetch/puller.py (check_one, lines 63-95), scripts/fetch/http.py (PROBE_TIMEOUT=15s, head/_head_via_get/fetch_text), app_puller/workers.py (CheckWorker), scripts/fetch/AGENTS.md line 25, and docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md (PERF-041/042 + coverage section). NOT a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit: PERF-041 explicitly covers only the redundant remote_version() call at the top of fetch() (recommend-no-change), PERF-042 covers arcgis_query_all memory, and the audit's coverage section explicitly lists eia_860/860m and arc_distressed source files as NOT exhaustively read. Corrections/tempering: (a) typical healthy-case probe count is ~4-8 HEADs for eia_860m (2-month lag x 2 patterns), slightly above the finding's '~4-6'; (b) the 12-min worst case requires timeout-style degradation — DNS failure / connection-refused fails much faster; (c) the sweep is parallel across 8 workers and one_done emits per source, so other sources' results still render progressively — only all_done + the last_full_check stamp stall, which slightly tempers UX impact; (d) the ARC fix suggestion (HEAD-then-GET) is marginal since 404 response bodies are tiny — the FY-window cap is the part that matters there. None of these invalidate the finding. Medium severity is appropriate for a desktop-tool check sweep that degrades uncancellably for minutes exactly when EIA misbehaves (a documented fragile point, CLAUDE.md §6); the proposed eia_860m fix (cap the walk, distinguish connection failure from placeholder) is sound and non-harmful.
mediumUnverified preprocess effort: medium

fcc_asr normalizer builds canonical rows via iterrows() plus two row-wise .apply DMS conversions over the national ASR bulk

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/fcc_asr.py:220-331 · reviewer: perf-new
What it is
FccAsrNormalizer.normalize converts coordinates with two full row-wise passes — `co_use["lat"] = co_use.apply(lambda r: dms_to_decimal(...), axis=1)` and the same for lon (lines 220-237) — and then builds every canonical tower row inside `for _, r in merged.iterrows():` (line 281), doing ~25 per-row Series lookups (`safe_get(r, ...)`, `r.get(...)`), a per-row `canon_county_fips` call, and a per-row Point construction. `merged` is the full national FCC ASR registration table (the RA.dat bulk — all states, all statuses, kept deliberately by the no-filtering invariant), so this is hundreds of thousands of iterations at iterrows' ~30-100 µs/row Series-materialization overhead, putting this normalizer's canonical pass in the minutes range. This is not one of the 42 audit items: the audit's vectorization findings were PERF-015 (interestingness scoring), PERF-020 (geom_bbox) and PERF-029 (distress single pass) — none touched normalizer-level row construction, and PERF-039 (_apply_lookup per-column map) is about the state-parcels lookup helper, a different module and mechanism.
Why it matters
This runs on every fcc_asr refresh and every `--force` rebuild, and FCC ASR is one of the largest non-parcel inputs — it likely dominates the 'normalize all sources' wall-clock in scripts/preprocess.py and the puller's Preprocess dialog, making data refreshes feel much slower than they need to be.
How to fix
Vectorize: compute lat/lon with column-wise arithmetic on the four DMS columns (or `np.where`-based sign handling) instead of axis=1 apply; build the canonical frame by selecting/renaming columns from `merged` and using `.map(STATUS_DESC)` / `.map(STRUCTURE_TYPE_DESC)` for the label columns, `gpd.points_from_xy` with a validity mask for geometry, and a `_map_unique`-style per-distinct-value map (as state_parcels.py already does, lines 386-403) for `canon_county_fips` and `_parse_date`.
Expected improvement
The fcc_asr normalize step should drop from minutes to seconds (typically 10-50x for iterrows-to-vectorized conversions), directly shortening every full preprocess run.
Evidence

What it is

In the walkthrough finalize + mark_manual_install path, do one rglob, derive both the file list and the byte total from it, and pass them into mark_manual_install instead of re-walking.
Expected improvement
Selection and per-source completion updates become O(1); walkthrough finalize does one walk instead of four, removing multi-second UI freezes on large manual installs.

lowConfirmed preprocess effort: small

normalize_state_parcels pins the first wide raw layer in memory for the whole run via qr_sample_raw, contradicting the ADR-050 bounded-memory claim

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/state_parcels.py:574-578, 601 · reviewer: preprocess-normalizers
What it is
Inside the streaming layer loop, `if qr_sample_raw is None: qr_sample_raw = g` keeps a reference to the first mapped layer's full-width raw GeoDataFrame, so the subsequent `del g # free the wide raw frame before the next layer` only frees layers 2..N — layer 1 stays resident until the quality report is built after the loop. Similarly `qr_sample_parcels = gdf` keeps the previous canonical layer alive across each iteration. The function docstring and ADR-050 promise "Peak memory stays bounded to a single layer"; in reality peak is first-raw-layer + current-raw-layer + previous-canonical-layer.
Why it matters
For the large statewide feeds (FL's ~10M-parcel FDOR set, WV's 1.39M-parcel gdb) the retained wide frame can be hundreds of MB to GBs, eroding the 16 GB-machine headroom the streaming design exists to protect. It only needs the layer for column *stats*, not the data itself.
How to fix
Compute the quality-report TableStats for the sample layer eagerly inside the loop (quality_report already profiles a frame; extract the per-table profiling so it can run on `g` before `del g`), or keep a small slice (e.g. g.head(10_000).copy()) for profiling instead of the whole frame; same for qr_sample_parcels.
Expected improvement
Peak memory genuinely bounded to ~one layer as ADR-050 states, restoring headroom on the FL/WV runs.
Evidence
state_parcels.py:574-575 `if qr_sample_raw is None:\n            qr_sample_raw = g`; line 578 `del g  # free the wide raw frame before the next layer`; line 601 `qr_sample_parcels = gdf` followed by `del gdf`

Walkthrough finalize + mark_manual_install perform four redundant recursive tree walks synchronously on the Qt UI thread

app_puller/walkthrough_dialog.py:263-289 · reviewer: perf-new
What it is
Clicking "I'm done — verify & finish" in the manual-download walkthrough runs `_finalize`, which walks the source's `current/` tree four separate times: (1) `directory_bytes(cur)` (a full `rglob("*")` + `stat` per file, scripts/fetch/storage.py:95-106), (2) `files = [p for p in cur.rglob("*") if p.is_file() ...]` to build the validate list, then `mark_manual_install` (app_puller/manual_install.py:154-160) does (3) another identical `cur.rglob("*")` list comprehension and (4) another `directory_bytes(cur)`. All four walks run synchronously on the Qt event loop. For manual sources whose extracted payload is thousands of files (NC OneMap's paginated per-page GeoJSONs, multi-zip FCC extracts), that is repeated blocking filesystem I/O on a single click, freezing the dialog. This is not one of the 42 audit items: PERF-031 (QTimer raw-tree walk) and PERF-033 (cheap existence probe) addressed the main window's periodic status refresh — a different call path; this is the one-shot walkthrough/upload finalize path, which still re-walks.
Why it matters
On a large manual source the dialog visibly hangs for seconds after the click (no event processing, beachball on macOS), right at the moment the user is waiting for a verify verdict — the worst time for the UI to freeze. The duplication also quadruples cold-cache disk time.
How to fix
Walk once: in `_finalize`, build `files` with a single rglob and derive the byte total from it (`sum(p.stat().st_size for p in files)`, replacing the separate `directory_bytes(cur) == 0` emptiness check with `not files`); extend `mark_manual_install` to accept the already-computed `(files, total_bytes)` and only walk itself when called without them (the details-pane upload path). Optionally move the single walk + validate onto a worker thread like the fetch path already does.
Expected improvement
4 full tree walks become 1 per finalize click — a roughly 4x cut in the click-to-verdict freeze, and a much smaller window of blocked UI on multi-thousand-file sources.
Evidence
walkthrough_dialog.py: `if directory_bytes(cur) == 0:` ... `files = [p for p in cur.rglob("*") if p.is_file() and not p.is_symlink()]` ... `mark_manual_install(self._source, self._manifest)`; manual_install.py: `files = [p for p in cur.rglob("*") if p.is_file() and not p.is_symlink()]` ... `entry.bytes = directory_bytes(cur)`

list_my_leads scans the entire ~16k-owner index per request when the result is bounded by the tiny persisted-lead set

backend/app/owners_index_service.py:193-218 · reviewer: perf-new
What it is
`OwnersIndexService.list_my_leads` (the /api/my-leads kanban read path) iterates every owner in the score-sorted index (`for owner in owners:`) and keeps only those whose status is off New — but by definition only owners present in the persisted `status_map()` can be off New, and that set is tiny (the leads the team has actually advanced). The method also calls `store.latest_flags()`, which scans every `channel='flag'` activity row for the tenant on each request. The audit applied exactly this insight to the counts endpoint only — `counts()` at line 232-236 carries the PERF-005 comment and iterates `status_map` instead of owners — while the list endpoint kept the full-index scan. So this is the un-applied sibling of PERF-005, not a re-derivation of it (PERF-005's text names counts; no audit item names list_my_leads).
Why it matters
Today the cost is a few milliseconds (a Python loop + dict lookups over ~16k owner dicts) per Leads-page load, so it is not user-visible yet — but it scales with index size (every new anchor state grows the loop) rather than with the user's actual worklist, and the Leads page is a daily-driver surface for the co-founder.
How to fix
Mirror the counts() approach: iterate `status_map().items()`, skip INITIAL_STATUS, look each cid up in the `by_id` map returned by `effective(merges)` (preserving the orphan-lead guard), then sort the small result by score (or by the precomputed `self._order` position) before applying the limit — O(persisted leads · log) instead of O(all owners). `latest_flags()` is already one query; it stays as-is.
Expected improvement
The kanban read becomes proportional to the worklist (tens of rows) instead of the index (16k+), keeping /api/my-leads flat as more states are composed into the owners index.
Evidence
`owners, _ = self.effective(merges)` ... `for owner in owners:  # already score-sorted\n            cid = owner["display_cluster_id"]\n            status = status_by_id.get(cid, INITIAL_STATUS)\n            if status == INITIAL_STATUS:\n                continue` — contrast counts() at line 232: `# ... an index owner off New (PERF-005) ... O(persisted leads) instead of O(all owners)`

FetchWorker emits a queued cross-thread Qt signal for every 64 KiB chunk — tens of thousands of events per pull

app_puller/workers.py:97-98 · reviewer: puller-review
What it is
http.py streams downloads in 64 KiB chunks (`CHUNK = 1024 * 64`, http.py:27) and invokes `on_progress` after every chunk; FetchWorker's `_progress` closure re-emits each callback as a queued cross-thread Qt signal (`self.progress.emit(_sid, done, total ...)`), and `_on_fetch_progress` on the UI thread then does setRange/setValue/setText/setFormat plus several human_bytes string formats per event. A 3 GB FCC pull generates ~48,000 queued events; on a fast connection that is hundreds to over a thousand posted events per second competing with all other UI work in the event loop.
Why it matters
During large pulls the event queue fills with progress updates far faster than the screen can usefully show them, making the tree, log, and buttons feel laggy precisely when the user is watching a long download — the classic progress-signal flood.
How to fix
Throttle in the worker: track `last_emit` (time.monotonic) in `_progress` and emit only if ≥100 ms elapsed or `done == total` (so the final 100% always lands). One small closure change; no UI changes needed.
Expected improvement
Progress signal volume drops from per-64-KiB to ~10 Hz (3-4 orders of magnitude fewer events) with no visible loss of smoothness.
Evidence
def _progress(done: int, total: int | None, fname: str, _sid=s.id) -> None:
    self.progress.emit(_sid, done, total if total is not None else -1, fname)
# http.py: CHUNK = 1024 * 64  # 64 KiB streaming chunk

Three normalizers carry a diverged copy of the ensure-EPSG:4326 check that raises on CRS-less input, while geom.py and state_parcels hold the correct versions

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/uspvdb.py:37-38 · reviewer: preprocess-quality
What it is
The reproject-to-WGS84 guard exists in five places with three different behaviors. geom.py:72-75 does it right (`set_crs` when crs is None, `to_crs` otherwise); state_parcels.py:227-249 does it carefully (_to_4326 with a bounds sanity check before assuming 4326). But uspvdb.py:37-38, hud_overlays.py:52-53, and hifld_towers.py:42-43 all inline `if gdf.crs is None or gdf.crs.to_epsg() != 4326: gdf = gdf.to_crs("EPSG:4326")` — which is wrong for the crs-is-None branch: geopandas' to_crs() raises ValueError ("Cannot transform naive geometries") on a frame with no CRS, so the exact input the check was written to handle crashes the normalize run instead of being coerced.
Why it matters
A USPVDB shapefile delivered without its .prj sidecar (or any manually-dropped HIFLD/HUD file whose CRS pyogrio can't resolve) fails normalization with an opaque ValueError instead of being handled — the intent of the duplicated guard. This is classic copy-drift: the correct logic was written twice elsewhere, and the three inline copies diverged into a latent bug. It also makes the EPSG:4326 output invariant (preprocess AGENTS.md) depend on five separately-maintained code paths.
How to fix
Add one `ensure_wgs84(gdf)` helper to scripts/preprocess/geom.py mirroring its existing add_polygon_derivatives logic (set_crs when None — optionally with state_parcels' _looks_like_lonlat bounds check — to_crs otherwise) and replace the three inline blocks in uspvdb.py, hud_overlays.py, hifld_towers.py with calls to it.
Expected improvement
The crs-is-None crash path disappears, and the project-wide 4326 invariant is enforced by one audited function instead of five copies in three behavioral states.
Evidence
uspvdb.py:37-38 `if gdf.crs is None or gdf.crs.to_epsg() != 4326:\n    gdf = gdf.to_crs("EPSG:4326")` (identical in hud_overlays.py:52-53, hifld_towers.py:42-43) vs geom.py:72-75 `if gdf.crs is None: ... gdf = gdf.set_crs("EPSG:4326")\nelif gdf.crs.to_epsg() != 4326: gdf = gdf.to_crs("EPSG:4326")`
Verifier notes (2)

Six copies of the atomic temp-file+os.replace JSON write — including two helpers with mirror-image names — despite typed_artifact.py existing as the consolidation point

scripts/preprocess/typed_artifact.py:148-158 · reviewer: preprocess-quality
What it is
The ~12-line atomic-JSON-write block (tempfile.mkstemp in the parent dir → os.fdopen → json.dump(indent=2, sort_keys=True) → os.replace → unlink-on-error) is open-coded six times: fetch/manifest.py:176-188 (Manifest.save), preprocess/manifest.py:233-247 (PreprocessManifest.save), app_assets.py:177-187 (write_composite_index), facets.py:106-114 (_write_json_atomic), owners_index.py:669-683 (_atomic_write_json — whose docstring even calls it "the repo-wide convention"), and typed_artifact.py:148-158 (TypedArtifact.write, whose own comment says it "mirrors the atomic-write pattern used elsewhere in the pipeline (e.g. app_assets.write_composite_index)"). Two of the copies are private helpers with swapped names (_atomic_write_json vs _write_json_atomic). The same drift applies to the timestamp helper: utcnow_iso (system_columns.py:14), _utcnow_iso (app_assets.py:94), and _now_iso (fetch/manifest.py:64) are three identical one-liners. On the broader fetch-vs-preprocess manifest question: the two manifest schemas are legitimately separate domain objects (raw fetch state vs normalize/compose outcomes) and should stay separate — the only real overlap and drift surface is this shared write/load mechanics.
Why it matters
typed_artifact.py was explicitly created to "consolidate what was previously duplicated across several modules" (its module docstring), yet five other sites still carry their own copy. Any hardening of the write path — fsync before replace, a different tmp naming scheme, Windows-safe error handling — now has to be found and applied in six places, and the two same-purpose-different-name helpers invite a seventh copy next time someone can't find the existing one.
How to fix
Extract a module-level `atomic_write_json(path, payload, *, default=None) -> None` (typed_artifact.py is the natural home for preprocess; a tiny scripts-level util module would also cover fetch/manifest.py, which can't import preprocess). Replace the six blocks with calls to it; TypedArtifact.write and facets._write_parquet_atomic (which writes parquet, not JSON) keep their own bodies or delegate the rename/cleanup part.
Expected improvement
~60 lines deleted, one audited implementation of the durability-critical write path, and the duplicate-named helpers gone.
Evidence

Every normalizer hand-copies the same normalize() tail (raw parquet → derivatives → system columns → write → quality report → NormalizeResult) that base.py should absorb

scripts/preprocess/normalizers/eia_860m.py:33-104 · reviewer: preprocess-quality
What it is
Eight normalizers (arc_distressed.py, eia_860.py, eia_860m.py, uspvdb.py, hifld_towers.py, hud_overlays.py, usda_persistent_poverty.py, state_billboards.py — and fcc_asr/faa_oeaaa with minor variations) repeat the identical sequence: locate the first raw file, call write_raw_parquet(df, out_dir, name="raw.parquet"), build canonical rows, call add_point/polygon_derivatives + add_system_columns, write via dual_write_spatial or write_parquet, build quality_report + write_quality_report, then assemble the same NormalizeResult literal (raw_rows/canonical_rows/bytes/output_files=files+[out_dir/"raw.parquet", qr_path]/canonical_types_written/quality_report). On top of that, the file-locator helper is re-implemented six times under four names: ArcDistressedNormalizer._find_xlsx (arc_distressed.py:107), Eia860MNormalizer._find_xlsx (eia_860m.py:100), Eia860Normalizer._find_first (eia_860.py:125), UspvdbNormalizer._find_first (uspvdb.py:86), UsdaPersistentPovertyNormalizer._find_csv (usda_persistent_poverty.py:115), and module-level _find_geojson in hud_overlays.py:32 and hifld_towers.py:104 — all the same `sorted(raw_dir.rglob(pattern))[0] if any else None` with the same raw_dir.exists() guard.
Why it matters
Each new source (the CLAUDE.md §5 checklist explicitly anticipates more) copies ~40 lines of ceremony, and any fix to the shared sequence (e.g. a change to how output_files is assembled, or adding a new system column step) must be applied in 8+ places. The hud_overlays comment at line 132-134 ("basename must be 'distress_overlays' — the composer's _load_state_data looks up the canonical type via OUTPUT_BASENAMES") shows the basename contract is already enforced only by per-file discipline; a template method would make it impossible to get wrong.
How to fix
Add a template method to Normalizer in scripts/preprocess/base.py: a concrete normalize() that calls two abstract hooks — read_raw(raw_dir) -> DataFrame and to_canonical(raw_df) -> (GeoDataFrame|DataFrame) — and owns the raw.parquet write, derivative/system-column stamping (keyed off SPATIAL_TYPES), the OUTPUT_BASENAMES-derived write, the quality report, and the NormalizeResult assembly. Add one shared find_first(raw_dir, *patterns) helper next to it and delete the six clones. Migrate the simple normalizers first; fcc_uls/state_parcels (multi-output, sharded) can keep overriding normalize() outright.
Expected improvement
Roughly 250-300 lines of copy-paste deleted, the OUTPUT_BASENAMES/state_fips output contract enforced in one place, and a new source shrinks to its genuinely source-specific field mapping.
Evidence
eia_860m.py:88-97 `qr = quality_report(self.source_id, {"raw": df, "solar_facilities": gdf})\n qr_path = write_quality_report(out_dir, qr)\n return NormalizeResult(... output_files=files + [out_dir / "raw.parquet", qr_path], ...)` — byte-for-byte the same shape in uspvdb.py:74-83, hifld_towers.py:92-101, hud_overlays.py:113-123, arc_distressed.py:94-104, eia_860.py:113-122, usda_persistent_poverty.py:102-112; plus six clones of `cand = sorted(raw_dir.rglob(...)); return cand[0] if cand else None`.

Metres→feet, score, coordinate and money formatting reimplemented per surface — same Holding field renders three different ways

app/src/components/TowerDetail.svelte:172-216 · reviewer: frontend-quality
What it is
Display formatting that format.ts claims as 'the single typed home' is forked across four places. Metres→feet exists three times: format.ts fmtMeters (line 38, `n * 3.28084`), lead-format.ts feet (line 15, `metres * 3.28084` with '—' fallback), towers.ts heightFeet (line 40-46, `heightM / 0.3048`); ExploreList.svelte additionally wraps heightFeet in a local fmtHeight (lines 33-36). Score formatting: format.ts fmtScore plus a local fmtScore in ExploreList.svelte (30-32) plus inline `score.toFixed(2)` in TowerDetail (137, 177) and LeadPage (203, 285). Coordinates: format.ts fmtLatLng exists but TowerDetail inlines `${t.lat.toFixed(5)}, ${t.lon.toFixed(5)}` twice (176, 187). Money is worst: the same Holding.assessed_value renders as abbreviated `money()` ('$1.2M') on LeadPage:240, as full `$${h.assessed_value.toLocaleString()}` inline on TowerDetail:213, and parcel assessed values render via format.ts fmtMoney in DetailsPanel (through schema.ts) — three formats for one concept across the three detail surfaces.
Why it matters
The co-founder sees the same parcel's assessed value formatted differently depending on which page he reads it from, and any rounding/locale fix must be repeated in 3-4 places (the TowerDetail inline variant already lacks the rounding and null-tolerance of fmtMoney — a 0 value renders '—' via truthiness, and a float renders with decimals).
How to fix
Make lead-format.ts (or format.ts) the single formatter module for the lead/tower surfaces: keep one feet() built on towers.heightFeet, one score formatter (s == null ? '—' : s.toFixed(2)), one latLng formatter, and decide one money convention per context (abbreviated for pills, fmtMoney for detail rows) — then replace the inline expressions in TowerDetail (137, 176-177, 187, 213), ExploreList (30-36) and LeadPage (203, 285) with the shared helpers.
Expected improvement
One source of truth per formatter; assessed value and coordinates render identically on DetailsPanel, TowerDetail and LeadPage; future format changes are one-line edits.
Evidence
TowerDetail.svelte:213: `<dd>{h.assessed_value ? `$${h.assessed_value.toLocaleString()}` : '—'}</dd>` vs LeadPage.svelte:240: `{money(h.assessed_value)}`; lead-format.ts:15: `return `${Math.round(metres * 3.28084)} ft`;` vs towers.ts:40: `const M_PER_FT = 0.3048;`
