401→signOut handling copy-pasted at 11 sites across 9 files instead of living in the shared request() wrapper

app/src/lib/leads-api.ts:221-249 · reviewer: frontend-quality
What it is
leads-api.ts's request() is documented as the one shared fetch path ('Reused by the tasks client … so every backend call shares one auth + error path'), yet the `if (e instanceof ApiError && e.status === 401) auth.signOut()` reaction is duplicated at every call layer: leads-store.svelte.ts:28, tasks-store.svelte.ts:24 and scoring-store.svelte.ts:27 each define a private identical handle() helper; dashboard-store.svelte.ts (46, 57), user-settings-store.svelte.ts (112, 136), activity-store.svelte.ts:74, auth-store.svelte.ts:59, Stats.svelte:36 and SettingsAccount.svelte:54 inline it. The companion `e instanceof Error ? e.message : String(e)` message extraction is likewise repeated at 10 sites even though stores-helpers.ts already exports errMsg() for exactly this.
Why it matters
Any store that forgets the boilerplate (a new digest, a new settings call) silently leaves the app in a signed-out-but-rendered state on token expiry instead of falling back to the sign-in gate — the failure mode is invisible until a session actually expires. Eleven copies also guarantee drift (scoring-store's handle() returns void while tasks-store's returns the message — already two shapes).
How to fix
Centralise in leads-api.ts: have request() invoke a registered `onUnauthorized` callback when res.status === 401 (auth-store registers auth.signOut at module init, avoiding the import cycle), then delete the three handle() helpers and eight inline checks, and route the remaining message extraction through stores-helpers.errMsg.
Expected improvement
Token-expiry behaviour becomes guaranteed-uniform for every current and future API call; ~30 lines of boilerplate deleted; new stores cannot forget the 401 path.
Evidence
leads-store.svelte.ts:27-30: `function handle(e: unknown): string { if (e instanceof ApiError && e.status === 401) auth.signOut(); return e instanceof Error ? e.message : String(e); }` — identical helper in tasks-store.svelte.ts:23-26; grep 'status === 401' → 11 hits in 9 files.
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Verified in current code. Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/app/src/lib/leads-api.ts:208-249 — request() is exactly as cited, with the doc comment "Reused by the tasks client (tasks-api.ts) so every backend call shares one auth + error path" (lines 218-220), and it has no 401-specific handling. Grep for 'status === 401' returns exactly 11 hits in 9 files at the cited lines: leads-store.svelte.ts:28, tasks-store.svelte.ts:24, scoring-store.svelte.ts:27 (each in a private handle() helper), dashboard-store.svelte.ts:46+57, user-settings-store.svelte.ts:112+136, activity-store.svelte.ts:74, auth-store.svelte.ts:59, Stats.svelte:36, SettingsAccount.svelte:54. The quoted leads-store handle() (lines 27-30) matches verbatim; tasks-store's is identical (23-26); scoring-store's returns void (26-28), confirming the claimed drift. errMsg() exists at stores-helpers.ts:41-42 and is used only by stores.svelte.ts, while the inline `instanceof Error ? .message : String(...)` pattern appears at 10 other sites (leads/tasks/scoring/user-settings stores, filter-model:110, TowerDetail:84/100/110, SettingsAccount:22, SignIn:20). docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md contains no mention of 401/signOut/errMsg — not a duplicate of the executed perf findings. Two corrections: (1) SettingsAccount.svelte:54 is NOT the signOut reaction — it maps 401 → 'Current password is incorrect.' for the password-change form and deliberately does not sign out (POST /api/me/password returns 401 for a wrong current password), so the copy-pasted-signOut count is 10 sites, not 11, and the proposed fix of unconditionally invoking onUnauthorized inside request() would wrongly sign the user out on a typo'd current password — the centralized handler needs an opt-out for that endpoint. AttentionService.dismiss() has already drifted from the production dismiss route (no merge resolution, no user attribution)

backend/app/attention_service.py:107-110 · reviewer: backend-quality
What it is
There are two implementations of 'dismiss a reply'. The production route main.py:722-738 (`dismiss_attention`) appends the dismiss row with `merges.resolve(body.cluster_id)` and `logged_by_user_id=principal.user_id`. AttentionService.dismiss() (the method that looks like the intended seam, exercised by tests/test_attention_service.py) appends with neither: `self.leads.append_attempt(cluster_id, channel=DISMISS_CHANNEL, outcome="dismissed")`. Grep confirms no production caller of `.dismiss()` or `.count()` — only tests use them.
Why it matters
The duplication has already drifted: the service method silently lacks the teammate attribution the route's own docstring calls out ('Attributed to the dismissing user so it carries a teammate dimension in activity/stats') and would write the dismiss row under an unresolved cluster id, where it would fail to clear a merged lead's reply. Anyone who later wires the service method (the natural refactor) reintroduces both regressions, and the tests assert the weaker behavior.
How to fix
Make one implementation: extend AttentionService.dismiss(cluster_id, *, logged_by_user_id) to take attribution, have the route resolve the merge and delegate to it (matching how /api/attention already builds the service), and update the test to assert attribution. Alternatively delete dismiss() and count() and let the route be the single implementation.
Expected improvement
Single dismiss code path; the attribution and merge-resolution behavior can no longer fork between the tested seam and the deployed route.
Evidence
attention_service.py:110: `self.leads.append_attempt(cluster_id, channel=DISMISS_CHANNEL, outcome="dismissed")` vs main.py:732-737: `store.append_attempt(merges.resolve(body.cluster_id), channel=DISMISS_CHANNEL, outcome="dismissed", logged_by_user_id=principal.user_id,)`
Verifier notes (2)

Grep confirms no production caller of .dismiss() or .count(): the only AttentionService construction is the /api/attention GET (main.py:698), which uses items() and len(items) — count() is also dead in production. Tests (backend/tests/test_attention_service.py:65-66,103,109) exercise both and assert the weaker, unattributed behavior. The merged-lead regression mechanism checks out: ContactAttempt rows are stored under the passed display_cluster_id and unhandled_replies() groups per-row by that column, so a dismiss under an unresolved raw id would not clear a merged lead's reply. Corrections: none material — file, lines, and evidence quotes are exact. Caveat on severity: this is a latent-drift hazard, not a live production bug (the route is correct; the service method is dead code), but medium remains defensible because the tests cement the drifted behavior and the service is the obvious future seam. _emit progress-callback guard duplicated verbatim between runner.py and pipeline.py

scripts/preprocess/runner.py:321-327 · reviewer: preprocess-quality
What it is
runner.py:321-327 and pipeline.py:115-121 define the same seven-line `_emit(cb, phase, key, info)` function (None-guard, call, catch-and-warn so a UI callback can never abort the pipeline), and both modules also re-declare the identical `ProgressCallback = Callable[[str, str, dict], None]` type alias (runner.py:35, pipeline.py:34).
Why it matters
The swallow-exceptions policy for progress callbacks is a deliberate invariant (pipeline.py's copy carries the noqa comment explaining it); having two copies means a future change to that policy — e.g. logging the traceback, or rate-limiting the warning — can land in one orchestrator and not the other.
How to fix
Keep one _emit + the ProgressCallback alias in pipeline.py (or a tiny shared module) and import it from runner.py.
Expected improvement
One place owns the callbacks-never-abort policy; ~10 duplicate lines removed.
Evidence
runner.py:321-327 and pipeline.py:115-121 both read `def _emit(cb: ProgressCallback | None, phase: str, key: str, info: dict) -> None:\n    if cb is None:\n        return\n    try:\n        cb(phase, key, info)\n    except Exception as e: ...\n        log.warning("progress callback raised: %s", e)`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/scripts/preprocess/runner.py and /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/scripts/preprocess/pipeline.py. Every cited location is exact: runner.py:321-327 and pipeline.py:115-121 both define the identical 7-line `_emit(cb, phase, key, info)` (None-guard, call, except-Exception → log.warning("progress callback raised: %s", e)), and both declare `ProgressCallback = Callable[[str, str, dict], None]` (runner.py:35, pipeline.py:34). The only textual difference is the one the finding itself flags: pipeline.py:120 carries `# noqa: BLE001 — UI callbacks must never abort the pipeline` while runner.py:326's bare `except Exception as e:` does not. Both copies are live — runner.py calls its _emit at lines 73/82/85/102/119/158/174/186/265; pipeline.py calls its own at 84/87/94/97/105. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (grep for _emit/progress in docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md returned nothing; that audit was perf, this is duplication). Proposed fix is feasible: pipeline.py imports runner only under TYPE_CHECKING (pipeline.py:29-32), and runner.py already imports pipeline lazily (runner.py:235), so runner.py importing `_emit`/`ProgressCallback` from pipeline.py creates no runtime cycle. Minor correction/context: scripts/fetch/base.py:48 also declares a `ProgressCallback` alias but with a different signature (Callable[[int, int | None, str], None]) — unrelated, and the finding correctly does not include it. Severity low is appropriate.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read scripts/preprocess/runner.py (lines 25-39, 315-328) and scripts/preprocess/pipeline.py (lines 18-40, 110-124). Confirmed: identical 7-line _emit at runner.py:321-327 and pipeline.py:115-121 (only difference is the noqa BLE001 comment on pipeline.py's except, which the finding correctly attributes), and duplicate ProgressCallback aliases at runner.py:35 / pipeline.py:34 with the same Callable[[str, str, dict], None] signature. Refutation attempts failed: not among the 42 perf-audit findings (grepped docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md — no _emit/ProgressCallback items); no existing shared module; scripts/fetch/base.py's ProgressCallback is a different signature/concept so the finding's scope is correct. Proposed fix is sound and non-circular: runner.py does not import pipeline.py today, and pipeline.py imports runner only under TYPE_CHECKING, so importing _emit/ProgressCallback from pipeline into runner introduces no runtime cycle. Both copies are currently behaviorally identical, so impact is drift-risk only — "low" is the right severity (info would also be defensible). All cited line numbers are accurate.

Two near-identical max-extent scans over the owners index: FilterModelStore.extents vs ScoringStore.extents

app/src/lib/scoring-store.svelte.ts:37-50 · reviewer: frontend-quality
What it is
filter-model.svelte.ts (lines 82-99) and scoring-store.svelte.ts (lines 37-50) each define a $derived that loops the same ~16k-row filterModel.owners array computing maxima — three of the four fields overlap (total_assessed_value, parcel_count, tallest_tower_height_m); the filter store adds structure_count, the scoring store adds tower_count. Relatedly, the score-rank comparator `(scoring.scoreOf(b.display_cluster_id) ?? -Infinity) - (scoring.scoreOf(a.display_cluster_id) ?? -Infinity)` is copy-pasted between Leads.svelte (21-24) and MyLeads.svelte (26-30).
Why it matters
Two derivations that must agree on what 'the extents of the index' means (e.g. whether a null height counts as 0) are maintained separately, and both rescan the full owners array independently on load; the duplicated comparator means a tie-break or null-handling tweak to lead ranking must be made twice.
How to fix
Compute one combined extents record (the union of the five maxima) in a single derived — most naturally on FilterModelStore since it owns `owners` — and have ScoringStore read it; export a `compareByScore(scoring)` comparator (or a `rankOwners()` helper) from scoring-store for Leads/MyLeads.
Expected improvement
One scan instead of two on index load, and one definition of extents/ranking semantics.
Evidence
filter-model.svelte.ts:88: `if (o.total_assessed_value > assessed) assessed = o.total_assessed_value;` vs scoring-store.svelte.ts:43: `if (o.total_assessed_value > assessedMax) assessedMax = o.total_assessed_value;` — same loop body over `filterModel.owners`.
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Read app/src/lib/scoring-store.svelte.ts, app/src/lib/filter-model.svelte.ts, app/src/components/Leads.svelte, app/src/components/MyLeads.svelte, app/src/lib/scoring.ts, and docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md. All factual claims check out: ScoringStore.extents (scoring-store.svelte.ts:37-50) and FilterModelStore.extents (filter-model.svelte.ts:82-99) each loop the same filterModel.owners array computing maxima, with exactly the stated 3-of-4 field overlap (total_assessed_value, parcel_count, tallest_tower_height_m; filter adds structure_count, scoring adds tower_count). The score comparator is duplicated verbatim — Leads.svelte:20-24 (finding said 21-24; scoreOf calls are at 22-23) and MyLeads.svelte:26-30. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit's 42 findings: the audit mentions scoring extents only in PERF-003's execution note ('Keep extents as-is — depends only on once-loaded owners'), which is a don't-memoize-further decision, not a dedup item; no audit finding covers this scan duplication or the comparator. Two corrections/nuances: (1) the 'must agree on what extents means' framing is overstated — the two records have intentionally different contracts: FilterExtents applies Math.ceil to assessed/height (slider bounds) and floors height at 0, while ScoreExtents keeps raw values and floors portfolioMax/towerMax/heightMax at 1 (divide-by-zero guard, since scoring.ts:111-114 divides by them via norm()). A naive 'union of five maxima' merge as proposed would either change scoring normalization (ceil'd denominators) or risk heightMax=0 division; the correct fix computes the raw maxima in one loop and derives both shaped records from it. (2) Materiality is purely maintenance drift — both scans are $derived over a once-loaded ~16k-row array, so the runtime cost is one extra sub-millisecond pass per load; perf impact is nil. This strengthens the "two definitions of the index extents maintained separately" concern, but means the proposed naive union of five maxima would change semantics for one consumer; a shared raw-maxima record with per-consumer post-processing (ceil/floor) would be the safer fix. Checked docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md for duplication with the 42 perf findings: no match — PERF-003 touches scoring-store but explicitly says "Keep extents as-is", and no audit finding addresses the extents duplication or the copy-pasted comparator (this is a duplication/maintainability finding, not a perf one). The "~16k-row" claim matches the audit's measured 16,237 owners. Both scans are O(n) and lazy ($derived), so the runtime cost is negligible — low severity is appropriate.
lowConfirmed frontend effort: small

app/src/components/LeadPage.svelte:563-575 · reviewer: frontend-quality
What it is

Attention/Activity/Stats payloads are declared three times: service dataclass, hand-built dict in the route, and Pydantic schema

backend/app/main.py:706-719, 861-871, 909-927 · reviewer: backend-quality
What it is
For /api/attention the same field list exists as AttentionItem (attention_service.py:32-39), a hand-written dict literal in the route (main.py:708-718), and AttentionItemOut (schemas.py:360-366). For /api/activity it is ActivityEvent (activity_service.py:25-35), the dict at main.py:911-924, and ActivityEventOut (schemas.py:379-388). The /api/stats route copies StatsResult's nine fields one-by-one into the structurally identical StatsResponse (main.py:861-871). Field names already match exactly in every case.
Why it matters
Adding a field requires three synchronized edits; missing the middle one (the route dict) fails silently — the schema default is served and no test or type checker complains. This is the same drift mechanism that already bit AttentionService.dismiss.
How to fix
Replace the hand-built dicts with `dataclasses.asdict(i)` (or construct the Out models in the service), and build StatsResponse with `StatsResponse(**dataclasses.asdict(r))`. Since field names already align, this is a mechanical change.
Expected improvement
Field additions become two edits (dataclass + schema) with any mismatch surfacing as a Pydantic validation error instead of a silent default.
Evidence
main.py:709-717: `{"kind": i.kind, "title": i.title, "when": i.when, "cluster_id": i.cluster_id, "owner_name": i.owner_name, "task_id": i.task_id,}` mirrors attention_service.AttentionItem field-for-field; main.py:861-871: `return StatsResponse(funnel=r.funnel, conversion=r.conversion, reply_rate=r.reply_rate, ...)`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Verified all three sites in current code. (1) /api/attention: main.py:709-716 hand-builds the six-field dict; AttentionItem (attention_service.py:33-39) and AttentionItemOut (schemas.py:360-366) declare the identical fields — three synchronized declarations confirmed. (2) /api/activity: main.py:912-922 hand-builds the nine-field dict; ActivityEvent (activity_service.py:26-35) and ActivityEventOut (schemas.py:379-388) match field-for-field. (3) /api/stats: main.py:861-871 copies all nine StatsResult fields (stats_service.py:43-52) one-by-one into the structurally identical StatsResponse (schemas.py:429-438). Field names align exactly in every case, so the asdict-based fix is mechanical as claimed. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit — that audit touches these routes only via PERF-008 (double tenant_attempts scan) and PERF-009 (effective() memoization), different root causes; this duplication finding is not among the 42. Minor corrections: exact dict-literal lines are 709-716 and 912-922 (cited ranges covered the full return expressions, acceptable); the why_it_matters claim that drift "already bit AttentionService.dismiss" is not verifiable from current code (the dismiss route just calls append_attempt) but is not load-bearing. Severity "low" is appropriate — maintainability-only, no runtime defect today.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Confirmed all three triplications by reading the files. (1) /api/attention: AttentionItem dataclass (backend/app/attention_service.py:32-39), hand-built dict in the route (backend/app/main.py:709-716, within the 706-719 AttentionResponse construction), AttentionItemOut (backend/app/schemas.py:360-366) — six fields match name-for-name. Per-user settings: one new preference key requires four synchronized edits, and the store's typed properties plus get_int are production-dead

backend/app/user_settings_store.py:82-112 · reviewer: backend-quality
What it is
Adding a preference key today touches: user_settings_store.py (constant + _DEFAULTS + KNOWN_KEYS + a typed property), schemas.py (UserSettingsResponse AND UserSettingsUpdate, lines 264-288), and main.py (_user_settings_out's explicit field mapping at 277-287, which also encodes the '== "1"' bool decoding per field, while update_my_settings at 303-310 encodes the inverse). The typed properties (theme/colorblind/basemap/view_mode/home_view/filter_default/filter_default_apply, lines 82-108) and get_int (66-70) have no callers outside tests — the API path uses resolved() and set() exclusively.
Why it matters
The bool-coercion knowledge ('which keys are 0/1') lives in two route-level places that can drift (a new bool key decoded in _user_settings_out but not encoded in update_my_settings round-trips wrong), and 30+ lines of test-only properties suggest an API surface the product does not have.
How to fix
Declare the key spec once in user_settings_store.py (e.g. `KEYS: dict[str, type]` or a small spec tuple of (name, default, is_bool)) and derive _DEFAULTS, KNOWN_KEYS, the response construction, and the update coercion from it. Drop the unused typed properties and get_int, or keep only the ones a future caller actually needs.
Expected improvement
A new preference becomes a one-line spec entry plus the two schema fields; the bool encode/decode pair cannot diverge.
Evidence
main.py:281: `colorblind=r["colorblind"] == "1",` and main.py:310: `s.set(key, ("1" if value else "0") if isinstance(value, bool) else str(value))` — two halves of the same coercion in different files; user_settings_store.py:82-108 properties have no app/ callers (grep)
Verifier notes (2)

Both evidence quotes are exact. (2) Typed properties and get_int (66-70) have no callers in backend/app/ (grep; the only get_int caller is the separate tenant-level settings_store.py calling its own copy); the API path uses resolved()+set()+KNOWN_KEYS only; properties are exercised solely by backend/tests/test_user_settings_store.py. Corrections: properties end at line 108, not 112 (110-112 is resolved()); and the drift mechanism is slightly overstated — update_my_settings's encode is generic via isinstance(value, bool), driven by the UserSettingsUpdate field type, so a properly-typed new bool key encodes automatically; the real drift surface is the three type declarations (Update field type, Response field type, the per-key `== "1"` decode in _user_settings_out) that must agree per key. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit (no user_settings mention in docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md; different category). Mitigating context for severity: 7 stable keys, PRD #121 shipped, and the typed-property facade deliberately mirrors SettingsStore's style per the module docstring — low is appropriate.

list_candidates and list_my_leads in OwnersIndexService are near-identical 25-line loops differing only in the status predicate

backend/app/owners_index_service.py:170-218 · reviewer: backend-quality
What it is
Both methods run the same pipeline: `owners, _ = self.effective(merges)`, `status_by_id = store.status_map()`, iterate the score-sorted owners, attach status, enforce a limit, and break. They differ only in the predicate (`status != INITIAL_STATUS` continue vs `status == INITIAL_STATUS` continue) and in list_my_leads additionally attaching trigger_asr from latest_flags().
Why it matters
The two surfaces are documented as exact complements ('the complement of list_candidates' — and counts() depends on that partition property). With the loop duplicated, a future tweak to one (a new attached field, a changed limit semantics, a status-handling fix) can break the partition invariant silently, which would make /api/counts disagree with the lists it summarizes.
How to fix
Extract one private iterator, e.g. `_owners_with_status(store, merges, *, want_new: bool, limit, extra: Callable | None)` that both methods call — the trigger_asr attachment becomes the extra for list_my_leads. The complement property is then structural.
Expected improvement
~20 fewer duplicated lines and a structurally-guaranteed candidates/my-leads partition that counts() relies on.
Evidence
list_candidates: `for owner in owners: status = status_by_id.get(owner["display_cluster_id"], INITIAL_STATUS); if status != INITIAL_STATUS: continue; out.append({**owner, "status": status})` vs list_my_leads: `if status == INITIAL_STATUS: continue; out.append({**owner, "status": status, "trigger_asr": flags.get(cid, "")})`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Read /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/backend/app/owners_index_service.py in full. The finding is accurate at the cited location: list_candidates is lines 170-191 and list_my_leads is lines 193-218, so the 170-218 range is correct. Both methods run the identical pipeline — `owners, _ = self.effective(merges)` (lines 181/206), `status_by_id = store.status_map()` (182/207), iterate the score-sorted owners, look up status with `INITIAL_STATUS` default (185/212), early-`continue` on the complementary predicate (`!=` at 186 vs `==` at 213), append `{**owner, "status": status}` (188/215, with list_my_leads additionally attaching `"trigger_asr": flags.get(cid, "")` from `store.latest_flags()` at 208), then the same limit+break (189-190/216-217). The evidence snippets match the code verbatim (modulo list_my_leads binding `cid` first). The why-it-matters is also grounded: list_my_leads's docstring says "The complement of ``list_candidates``" (line 201), and counts() (lines 220-237) explicitly relies on the partition property — and computes it independently (O(persisted-leads) per PERF-005) rather than via the two lists, so a one-sided edit to either loop would indeed make /api/counts silently disagree with the lists. Minor corrections: (1) "25-line loops" slightly overstates — the methods are 22 and 26 lines including signature/docstring; the duplicated loop bodies are ~8-10 lines each; (2) checked docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md — these methods appear only in perf findings (Rank 5 counts() optimization, Rank ~23 response_model/spread), none of the 42 proposes extracting a shared iterator, so this does not duplicate the executed audit. Severity low is appropriate: real duplication of two adjacent, well-documented methods, small blast radius, but the partition invariant is enforced only by convention.

NOT a perf-audit duplicate: PERF-005 (Rank 5) covers the same methods but its executed fix was the counts() O(persisted-leads) rewrite (now lines 220-237); the loop duplication itself was not one of the 42 perf findings. PERF-024 mentions the {**owner,'status':status} spread but targets pydantic validation cost, not duplication. Severity 'low' is appropriate: real, unmitigated-at-source duplication of complement logic, but small (8-line loops, adjacent, cross-referencing docstrings) with meaningful test coverage of the partition behavior.

Lead-status ladder constants forked beyond the sanctioned two-file lockstep: stats_service._LADDER/_WORKED, auto_followup_rule._AWAITING, and Stats.svelte FUNNEL_ORDER/LABEL

backend/app/stats_service.py:19-23 · reviewer: cross-duplication
What it is
The status enum is documented as a two-site lockstep pair (backend/app/lead_status.py STATUSES ↔ app/src/lib/lead-status.ts STATUSES/STATUS_LABELS, each file naming the other as its parity target). But three more independent copies exist that neither file mentions: stats_service.py:19-21 hand-rolls `_LADDER = ["new", "researching", "contacted", "interested", "in_business"]`, `_RANK`, and `_WORKED = {"researching", ..., "uninterested"}` instead of deriving them from lead_status.STATUSES/TERMINAL_STATUSES; auto_followup_rule.py:26 hand-rolls `_AWAITING = {"new", "researching", "contacted"}`; and Stats.svelte:48-56 re-declares `FUNNEL_ORDER` (all six statuses) and a `LABEL` map that duplicates STATUS_LABELS from lead-status.ts character-for-character.
Why it matters
If the ladder ever gains or renames a status, the documented procedure ("keep the two in lockstep") updates exactly the two canonical files — and the analytics silently break: _funnel() only emits statuses in its private order list, so leads in the new status vanish from the funnel and the win_rate/conversion denominators go wrong with no error; the auto-followup rule would never schedule a chase for the new status; Stats.svelte would render the new lane unlabeled. All failures are silent data omissions, not crashes.
How to fix
Derive the backend copies from lead_status.py (`_LADDER = [s for s in STATUSES if s != "uninterested"]`, `_WORKED = set(STATUSES) - {INITIAL_STATUS}`, `_AWAITING` as a named constant exported next to ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS); in Stats.svelte import STATUSES and STATUS_LABELS from '../lib/lead-status' instead of re-declaring them.
Expected improvement
A future status change propagates from the two documented lockstep files to analytics, automation, and the Stats UI automatically instead of silently dropping data.
Evidence
stats_service.py:19-21: "_LADDER = [\"new\", \"researching\", \"contacted\", \"interested\", \"in_business\"] ... _WORKED = {\"researching\", ...}"; auto_followup_rule.py:26: "_AWAITING = {\"new\", \"researching\", \"contacted\"}"; Stats.svelte:48-49: "const FUNNEL_ORDER = ['new', 'researching', ...]; const LABEL: Record<string, string> = {" — duplicating lead-status.ts:30-37 STATUS_LABELS

The two manifest implementations drifted on schema_version handling: fetch re-stamps to current on save, preprocess persists the stale loaded version

scripts/preprocess/manifest.py:225-231 · reviewer: cross-duplication
What it is
scripts/fetch/manifest.py (270 lines) and scripts/preprocess/manifest.py (504 lines) are mostly distinct domains (SourceEntry vs Normalize/ComposeEntry), but share ~45 lines of load/save scaffolding (missing-file → fresh, corrupt-JSON → fresh, atomic save) — and that shared logic has diverged. fetch's save() deliberately normalizes the stamp: payload uses the SCHEMA_VERSION constant with the comment "On save, normalize to the current schema_version regardless of what we loaded" (manifest.py:167-171). preprocess's save() writes `"schema_version": self.schema_version` — the value loaded from disk (load() at line 216: `raw.get("schema_version", SCHEMA_VERSION)`). So a preprocess manifest written by an older schema version gets rewritten in the current v3 shape while still claiming the old version number.
Why it matters
Today nothing branches on the preprocess manifest's schema_version, so the bug is latent — but the field exists precisely so a future migration can branch on it, and at that point a v3-shaped file stamped v2 would route through the wrong migration path. It is also a textbook example of twin implementations drifting: the fetch author solved this exact problem and the fix never reached the sibling.
How to fix
Make PreprocessManifest.save() write the SCHEMA_VERSION constant (one-line change, mirroring fetch's comment), and optionally fold both save() bodies into the shared atomic-write helper from the duplication finding above.
Expected improvement
On-disk schema_version always describes the shape actually written, so a future migration can trust it; one less divergence between the twin manifests.
Evidence
fetch/manifest.py:167-171: "# On save, normalize to the current schema_version regardless ... \"schema_version\": SCHEMA_VERSION," vs preprocess/manifest.py:227-228: "payload = {\n            \"schema_version\": self.schema_version,"
lowUnverified backend effort: trivial

auth_service._normalize_email re-implements credentials.normalize, whose docstring claims to be "the one normalization"

backend/app/auth_service.py:125-126 · reviewer: cross-duplication
What it is
credentials.py:61-63 defines `normalize(value) -> value.strip().lower()` with the docstring "Trim + lowercase — the one normalization for usernames and team names", and users.py consistently imports it. But auth_service.py:125-126 defines a private duplicate `_normalize_email(email) -> email.strip().lower()` used by AuthService.get_user_by_email/provision_user — the same identifier (the username, which doubles as the User.email row key) normalized by two independent functions on the two sides of the file→DB seam.
Why it matters
The two implementations are identical today, so there is no live bug — but they normalize the same string at the two points that must agree: the credentials-file lookup (resolve_principal → credentials.normalize) and the User-row lookup/provisioning (AuthService → _normalize_email). If either ever gains a refinement (e.g. Unicode case folding or NFKC), a username could authorize against the file under one canonical form while provisioning or matching a User row under another, silently splitting one person into two user identities (and two settings/attribution scopes).
How to fix
Delete _normalize_email and call credentials.normalize in AuthService.get_user_by_email and provision_user (auth_service.py already imports the credentials module).
Expected improvement
One canonical username normalization on both sides of the file-authority seam, matching the docstring's stated contract.
Evidence
credentials.py:61-63: "def normalize(value: str) -> str:\n    \"\"\"Trim + lowercase — the one normalization for usernames and team names.\"\"\"\n    return value.strip().lower()" vs auth_service.py:125-126: "def _normalize_email(email: str) -> str:\n    return email.strip().lower()"
lowUnverified fetch effort: trivial

usda_persistent_poverty hand-rolls the version_from_head chain but diverges to a silent 'unknown' fallback that pins status ok forever

scripts/fetch/sources/usda_persistent_poverty.py:74-85 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
remote_version duplicates _helpers.version_from_head's Last-Modified → ETag → Content-Length chain line-for-line, but where the shared helper deliberately raises ('better to surface than silently version with timestamps'), the copy returns the literal string "unknown". If ERS ever stops sending all three headers, mark_check compares local_version "unknown" == remote_version "unknown" and stamps the source ok indefinitely — staleness detection is permanently defeated rather than surfacing as check_failed. fcc_asr.py:66-80 carries the same duplicated chain (though it correctly raises).
Why it matters
Divergent copies of the version chain are exactly how one source quietly loses the fail-loud behavior the helper encodes; the "unknown" path turns a publisher misconfiguration into invisible永-fresh data.
How to fix
Replace both bodies with `return version_from_head(DOWNLOAD_URL, label=self.id)` (and `version_from_head(R_TOWER_URL, ...)` in fcc_asr), deleting the duplicated chains and the "unknown" fallback.
Expected improvement
One canonical version-derivation path; header loss surfaces as check_failed in the UI instead of silently freezing freshness.
Evidence
usda_persistent_poverty.py:83-85 `cl = h.get("content_length")\n        if cl:\n            return f"len-{cl}"\n        return "unknown"` vs _helpers.py:34 `raise RuntimeError(f"could not derive remote version…")`

main.py is a 1012-line module mixing eight route domains plus serialization helpers — split along its own section markers into APIRouters

backend/app/main.py:1-1012 · reviewer: backend-quality
What it is
main.py contains auth/login (201-334), per-user settings/me (237-334), leads CRUD + merge/split (336-535), antennas (538-584), tasks (587-685), attention (688-738), tenant settings (741-763), scoring presets (766-810), stats (813-871), activity (874-927), users (940-962), six near-identical dependency factories (132-183), and five serializer helpers (_antenna_state, _preset_out, _task_to_out, _to_detail, _user_settings_out). The natural seams are already drawn by the section comments (`# ── tasks …`, `# ── Dashboard: attention feed …`). It also accrues small cruft from its size: three separate local `from .models import User` imports (lines 238, 267, 933, 948) with no circular-import reason, and `list_users` (946-951) duplicating the tenant-users query that `_tenant_user_names` (930-937) already runs.
Why it matters
Every new feature lands in the same file, so merge conflicts, accidental cross-domain coupling, and review noise all grow with it; the dependency factories and serializers are copy-paste targets for the next domain (the get_* sextet is already six copies of one pattern, with get_store inconsistently a generator while the rest return).
How to fix
Introduce a deps.py with a generic `def tenant_store(cls): def dep(tenant_id=Depends(current_tenant_id), db=Depends(get_db)): return cls(db, tenant_id); return dep` to replace the six factories, then move each commented section into fastapi.APIRouter modules (routers/leads.py, routers/tasks.py, routers/dashboard.py, routers/me.py, routers/presets_settings.py) included from a slim main.py that keeps lifespan/CORS/owners_index. Hoist the local User imports to module level and reuse _tenant_user_names in list_users.
Expected improvement
Each domain becomes a few-hundred-line module with its own serializers; route additions stop touching one global file, and the dependency boilerplate shrinks by ~40 lines.
Evidence
main.py:132-173: six clones of `def get_X(tenant_id: int = Depends(current_tenant_id), db: Session = Depends(get_db)) -> XStore: return XStore(db, tenant_id)`; section markers like `# ── tasks (UI overhaul P2 / #107, filtering #110) ──` already delimit the eight domains

infoConfirmed frontend effort: trivial

MapView.hoverListenerCleanups is populated but never invoked — dead teardown code

app/src/components/MapView.svelte:111-112, 230-236 · reviewer: frontend-components-bugs
What it is
attachHover pushes an off()-pair closure into `hoverListenerCleanups` for every parcel fill layer, but no code path ever iterates or calls the array — including the onMount teardown, which resets the other module flags and calls `m.remove()`. In practice this is harmless today because `map.remove()` drops all listeners with the map instance, but the array signals a teardown contract that does not exist, and if anyone later adds per-layer rebuild logic (removing/re-adding parcel layers) the stale-listener cleanup they would reach for is silently a no-op.
Why it matters
Dead lifecycle code misleads the next editor about what is actually cleaned up; the only unmount path (sign-out) relies entirely on map.remove().
How to fix
Either invoke the cleanups in the onMount teardown (before m.remove()) and clear the array, or delete the array and its push and add a comment that listener teardown is delegated to map.remove().
Expected improvement
The teardown story matches reality; less confusion when parcel-layer lifecycle changes.
Evidence
/** Teardown fns for the per-layer mouseenter/mouseleave listeners. */
const hoverListenerCleanups: Array<() => void> = [];
...
hoverListenerCleanups.push(() => {
  map?.off('mouseenter', layerId, enter);
  map?.off('mouseleave', layerId, leave);
});
— grep shows no other reference; the onMount return never calls them.
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): Confirmed. Read app/src/components/MapView.svelte (lines 95-255) and grepped the whole app source: `hoverListenerCleanups` has exactly two references — declaration at line 112 and the push at lines 232-235 inside attachHover (sole caller: line 324, attachHover(parcelFillId(state)), so 'every parcel fill layer' is accurate). The onMount teardown (lines 171-182) resets the tower/solar/parcel flags and calls m.remove() but never invokes or clears the array. No removeLayer/removeSource exists in MapView, so the stale-listener risk is forward-looking only, as the finding states. Mitigation check: map.remove() does drop all listeners, so no functional leak today — but the finding already concedes that and correctly frames it as misleading dead lifecycle code (info severity is right, not inflated). The array is component-instance-scoped, so no cross-mount accumulation either. Verified the unmount-path claim in App.svelte: MapView mounts inside the auth.verified branch behind the mapEverShown latch (lines 178, 331-332), so sign-out is the only unmount. Not a duplicate of the 2026-06-07 perf audit — docs/PERF_AUDIT_2026-06-07.md has no hover/dead-teardown item. Proposed fixes are both safe. Minor corrections: lines 230-231 in the cited range are the map.on calls, the push is 232-235; otherwise file/lines/description accurate.
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): Verified in /Users/danielgentile/Projects/pilot/app/src/components/MapView.svelte. Architecture (2)

lowUnverified backend effort: trivial

The 'first team owns tenant 1' binding is decided silently by file order at the first login against a given DB, with no seeding, logging, or verification

backend/app/auth_service.py:117-120 · reviewer: identity-tenancy-lifecycle
What it is
Nothing pins tenant 1: `init_db` (db.py:95-107) only creates tables, and Tenant rows are minted lazily — the first login whose team has no Tenant row triggers `register_teams(teams_in_file_order(users))` using the file content at that instant. Confirmed sequences that hand tenant 1 to a different team than intended: (a) any fresh or reset DB (new environment, lost Fly volume, dev machine) paired with a users.txt whose first team has changed — the legacy board carrying tenant_id=1 rows would attach to whoever is first at the next login; (b) editing the file between deploy and the first-ever login (e.g. adding a user line whose new team sorts first); (c) removing all of the first team's users before any login has occurred against that DB. Once minted, the name->id mapping is stable (the IntegrityError race at tenant_service.py:40-46 is handled), so this is purely a first-registration window — but because tenant creation is unlogged, a wrong binding is invisible until data shows up on the wrong board.
Why it matters
Production is past its first registration, so today's exposure is limited to environment rebuilds and restores — exactly the moments when an operator is least likely to remember that file order, not anything in the DB, decides who inherits tenant_id=1 rows. A wrong binding in that window is a tenant-isolation mistake with no breadcrumb.
How to fix
Log every Tenant row creation with its assigned id and the file order that produced it, and add a startup sanity log line (e.g. 'tenant 1 = <name>') so a rebuilt environment makes the binding visible immediately. Optionally seed/assert the expected tenant-1 name via an env var in prod.
Expected improvement
The one-shot, order-dependent binding becomes observable and verifiable instead of an invisible side effect of whoever logs in first.
Evidence
if tenant is None:
    # A team we haven't seen yet — register *all* teams in file order so
    # id assignment stays file-ordered (the first team must own tenant 1).
    tenant = tenant_svc.register_teams(credentials.teams_in_file_order(users))[team]

Backend test suite burns seconds on full-cost 240k-iteration PBKDF2 hashing in fixtures and per-test logins

backend/tests/conftest.py:33-37 · reviewer: perf-new
What it is
Every backend API test authenticates through the real login flow (`_helpers.login()` posts to /api/auth/login, which runs `verify_password` at the iteration count stored in the hash), and the conftest seeds the test credentials file with `hash_password('dev')` at the production default of 240,000 PBKDF2 iterations (backend/app/password.py:28, `DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 240_000`). On this machine one such hash/verify costs ~48 ms (measured); CI runners are typically slower. There are ~85 test functions across the API test files that each call `login()` at least once (isolation tests log in twice), plus ~30+ direct full-cost hash-generation sites in test_users_cli.py, test_teams.py, test_file_authority.py, test_auth.py, test_change_password.py and test_credentials.py (e.g. test_teams.py:112-114 builds three hashes per fixture file). That is on the order of 100-150 full-cost KDF operations, i.e. roughly 5-15+ seconds of pure key-derivation per suite run locally and more on CI — and it grows linearly with every new API test. This is not one of the 42 audit items: the audit covered runtime code paths only (no test-suite findings appear in the list).
Why it matters
Per the project's CI posture, the pytest job that looks like a placeholder actually runs the FULL suite as the merge gate, so this KDF time is paid on every PR. It is the kind of fixed per-test overhead that quietly dominates suite wall-clock as the API surface grows, discouraging people from running the suite locally before pushing.
How to fix
Exploit the self-describing hash format (`pbkdf2_sha256$<iterations>$...` — verify_password honors whatever iteration count is stored): have conftest seed the test users.txt with `hash_password('dev', iterations=1000)`, and in the CLI/teams/file-authority tests either pass a low-iteration hash helper or monkeypatch `backend.app.users.hash_password` / `backend.app.password.DEFAULT_ITERATIONS` via an autouse fixture. test_password.py can keep a couple of tests at the real cost to pin the production default.
Expected improvement
Removes roughly 5-30 seconds from every merge-gate suite run (machine-dependent), and makes per-test auth cost ~0.2 ms instead of ~50-150 ms, so adding API tests stays cheap.
Evidence
backend/app/password.py: `DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 240_000` — backend/tests/conftest.py:36: `_USERS_FILE.write_text(f"dev:{hash_password('dev')}:devteam\n", encoding="utf-8")` — backend/tests/_helpers.py:72: `r = client.post("/api/auth/login", json={"username": username, "password": password})` (called per test in ~11 API test files)

Litestream restore drill (and caddy/rclone/tippecanoe tests) are permanently skipped everywhere CI runs

tests/test_litestream_restore.py:31-39 · reviewer: tests-review
What it is
Four test files gate on external binaries via skipif (correct per the CI ruff/pytest policy), but CI never installs any of the binaries, so they are skipped on every CI run and only execute if a dev machine happens to have them. The most important is test_litestream_restore.py — the backup-restore drill for the production SQLite+Litestream→R2 setup, whose own docstring calls it 'the one real risk of the hosting decision' — which requires litestream AND minio AND mc simultaneously. test_caddy_serving.py (the prod serving config), test_sync_composite.py (rclone), and test_tiles.py (tippecanoe) follow the same pattern. test.yml installs only pip requirements; there is no apt-get/brew step and no scheduled workflow that provisions these binaries.
Why it matters
The litestream test exists specifically so a misconfigured replica/restore path 'goes red — before we ever depend on it in prod'. Since the app went live (2026-06-04) with Litestream as the only backup of tenant lead state, a regression to litestream.yml or the restore path would be caught by no automated run anywhere — the safety-net test passes vacuously as a skip. The skips are visible only in the `-ra` summary of a green CI log, which nobody reads.
How to fix
Add a scheduled (weekly) or manually-dispatchable GitHub Actions job that installs litestream, minio, mc, caddy (all have linux-amd64 release binaries) and runs `pytest tests/test_litestream_restore.py tests/test_caddy_serving.py`. Alternatively run the restore drill as a periodic ops cron against the real R2 replica. At minimum, document in TESTING.md that these four files run nowhere automatically.
Expected improvement
The production data-loss recovery path gets exercised on a schedule instead of never; a litestream.yml or Caddyfile regression fails a job instead of passing as a silent skip.
Evidence
test_litestream_restore.py: `pytestmark = pytest.mark.skipif(\n    not (_LITESTREAM and _MINIO and _MC),\n    reason="litestream, minio and mc must all be installed")`; test_caddy_serving.py:36 `pytestmark = pytest.mark.skipif(_CADDY is None, ...)`; test.yml install step is only `pip install -r requirements.txt` + `-r requirements-dev.txt`.

backend/tests/conftest.py uses os.environ.setdefault — an exported DATABASE_URL or OWNERS_INDEX_PATH leaks into the test run and gets written to

backend/tests/conftest.py:20-37 · reviewer: tests-review
What it is
The conftest's stated intent is that importing the FastAPI app 'never touches the real dev DB or owners_index.json', and it points DATABASE_URL, OWNERS_INDEX_PATH, AUTH_DEV_MODE, AUTH_JWT_SECRET and APP_USERS_FILE at throwaway temp paths — but via `os.environ.setdefault`, which is a no-op when the variable is already exported in the developer's shell. If a dev has DATABASE_URL exported (e.g. pointing at a local Postgres or a copy of the prod volume DB), every `_helpers.api()` test boots TestClient → lifespan → `init_db()` (create_all + additive migrations) against that real database. Worse, `_helpers.api()` begins with `Path(settings.owners_index_path).write_text(json.dumps(sample))` — with OWNERS_INDEX_PATH exported, the suite overwrites the real owners_index.json with a 1-owner test fixture. Same for APP_USERS_FILE: an exported path means tests like `extra_user`/`users_file` rewrite a real credentials file (they restore on exit, but only if the test doesn't crash mid-context).
Why it matters
It directly contradicts the comment one line above it, and it converts `pytest` from a safe command into one that can mutate a real DB, clobber the served owners index, or rewrite a real users.txt depending on invisible shell state. The hosting memory's ops flows make exporting DATABASE_URL locally plausible (e.g. while debugging a restored prod snapshot).
How to fix
Assign unconditionally for the five test-critical variables: `os.environ["DATABASE_URL"] = ...` etc. Tests should never inherit these from the environment; there is no legitimate use case the setdefault preserves (Postgres opt-in already has its own dedicated variables, PILOT_TEST_DATABASE_URL / PILOT_TEST_TESTCONTAINERS).
Expected improvement
Running pytest becomes hermetic regardless of shell state; removes a low-probability but high-blast-radius path to corrupting a real database or the production-shaped owners index from a test run.
Evidence
conftest.py:22-25: `# (for the API test) never touches the real dev DB or owners_index.json.` followed by `os.environ.setdefault("DATABASE_URL", f"sqlite+pysqlite:///{_TMP}/lead_state.sqlite3")`; _helpers.py api(): `Path(settings.owners_index_path).write_text(json.dumps(sample))`; main.py lifespan: `init_db()`.

No builder→backend contract test for the owners index: backend fixtures are hand-written v1–v3 dicts while the builder emits schema v5

backend/tests/test_api_merge.py:37-44 · reviewer: tests-review
What it is
Every backend HTTP test feeds OwnersIndexService a hand-rolled dict: test_auth.py, test_file_authority.py and the isolation suite use `"schema_version": 1`, test_owners_index_service.py uses v2, test_api_merge.py and test_owner_merge_service.py use v3 — while `scripts/preprocess/owners_index.py` now emits `OWNERS_INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION = 5` (CLAUDE.md still says v4). Old-version fixtures are partly intentional (the 'stale index must still serve' invariant, and tests like test_pre_v5_tower_without_score_defaults_to_none cover it deliberately), but nothing anywhere asserts that the CURRENT builder output round-trips through the backend's OwnerSummary/Structure response models. CLAUDE.md §8 documents the exact footgun — 'an undeclared field is silently dropped by response_model' — yet the only guards are ad-hoc per-field assertions added after each incident (test_api_lead_page.py:117 'carriers survive the response_model boundary', :114 per-tower score). The repo already has the right pattern for the composite index: a shared golden fixture (tests/fixtures/catalog/golden_index.json) validated by both tests/test_index_schema.py (Python) and app/tests/unit/index-contract.test.ts (TS).
Why it matters
The next preprocess field added to the owners index (D+E will add lease-doc fields) that isn't also declared on the backend Pydantic model will vanish silently at the response_model boundary — the documented most-violated invariant — and no test goes red. The failure mode is invisible: the API serves 200s, the field is just absent, and the frontend renders '—'.
How to fix
Generate a golden owners_index fixture by running `build_owners_index` (or CrossStateOwnerResolver + the holdings assembly) on the synthetic rows already used in tests/test_owners_index.py, commit it, and add a backend test that serves it through `_helpers.api()` and asserts every owner-level and structure-level key in the fixture appears in the GET /api/candidates and GET /api/leads/{id} responses (allowing an explicit denylist for intentionally-internal keys). Mirror the catalog golden-fixture pattern.

Commit-guard for credentials only matches files named exactly users.txt — a committed copy under any other name passes

backend/tests/test_commit_guard.py:36-38 · reviewer: tests-review
What it is
The guard works as designed for its stated invariant (verified: `git check-ignore -q backend/users.txt` asserts the ignore rule, and `git ls-files` scanning would fail the suite if backend/users.txt were force-added, since ls-files lists tracked files regardless of ignore rules). But the tracked-file sweep matches only `Path(p).name == "users.txt"`. The realistic accident the docstring itself names — 'a credentials file created under another tracked path' — usually arrives with a different name: `users.txt.bak`, `users.prod.txt`, `users-2026.txt`, or a copy pasted into a doc. None of those trip the guard, and .gitignore covers only the canonical path.
Why it matters
The file contains bcrypt password hashes for the production sign-in (the only credential source, re-authorizing every request). A backup-with-suffix committed during an ops session (the documented workflow involves copying users.txt around Fly volumes) would ship hashes to the public GitHub repo while the guard suite stays green.
How to fix
Strengthen test_no_credentials_file_is_tracked_anywhere: scan tracked files whose names match `users*` / `*.txt` (cheap, repo is small) for lines matching the credential shape (e.g. regex `^[a-z0-9_.-]+:\$2[aby]\$\d\d\$` for bcrypt) while exempting users.txt.example via its enforced 'EXAMPLE' tag. This reuses the format knowledge the third test already encodes.
Expected improvement
The guard catches the likely accident (renamed/backup credential file) instead of only the exact canonical filename, at negligible test cost.
Evidence
`tracked = _git("ls-files").stdout.splitlines()\n    offenders = [p for p in tracked if Path(p).name == "users.txt"]\n    assert offenders == []` — exact-name match only; docstring claims coverage of 'a credentials file created under another tracked path'.

verify_raw.py SIGNATURES has drifted from the active source registry — wv_parcels and fdor_nal (both active anchor parcel sources) have no signature and always verify as UNKNOWN

scripts/verify_raw.py:38-226 · reviewer: preprocess-quality
What it is
SIGNATURES is a hand-maintained dict keyed by source_id, separate from scripts/fetch/registry.py. It has drifted in both directions: (1) it is missing entries for `wv_parcels` (WV — a committed anchor state per ADR-060) and `fdor_nal` (the active FL parcel source that replaced fl_fgdl_parcels), so verify_all() reports both as "UNKNOWN — no signature configured" (verify_raw.py:280-287) and can never catch a truncated or wrong download for them; (2) it still carries detailed entries for sources that are deferred or dropped (in_parcels, ut_parcels, mn_parcels, regrid_parcels, fl_fgdl_parcels, lbnl_tts, hifld_towers, the three billboard sources) which verify_all() never exercises since all_sources() returns only the _ACTIVE block. The dict also duplicates knowledge that already lives in each source's validate()/fetch(): the '"FeatureCollection"' content sniff appears both in SIGNATURES (hud_oz/hud_qct/hifld entries, lines 97/206/212) and in those sources' validate() overrides (e.g. hud_oz.py:106-111), the RA.dat requirement is in both SIGNATURES line 42 and fcc_asr.py validate() line 122-127, and the min-byte floors mirror fetch-time checks (hud_oz fetch raises if n < 500_000).
Why it matters
The whole point of verify_raw is to surface bad raw data before preprocessing, and the two sources it silently can't check are the parcel feeds for two of the four committed anchor states — the most consequential raw data in the project. Because the table is maintained by hand in a separate file, this drift is structural: every registry change (the FL source swap, the WV addition) requires remembering to touch verify_raw.py, and nothing fails when you don't.
How to fix
Move the signature next to the source: add an optional `signature: dict | None` class attribute on DataSource (scripts/fetch/base.py) and have verify_raw.py build its table from `all_sources()` so an active source without a signature is impossible to miss (verify_one can flag it loudly, or a unit test can assert every active source declares one). Minimum viable fix: add wv_parcels + fdor_nal entries and a small test asserting `{s.id for s in all_sources()} <= SIGNATURES.keys()`.
Expected improvement
verify_raw actually covers all active sources, the duplicate content checks collapse to one declaration per source, and future registry changes can't silently strand the verifier.
Evidence
Active registry includes FDOR_NAL and STATE_PARCELS_ACTIVE ("MT + AR + WV", registry.py:87-89); `grep -n 'wv_parcels\|fdor_nal' scripts/verify_raw.py` → no matches; verify_raw.py:280-287 returns `VerifyResult(source_id, "UNKNOWN", nbytes, f"{len(files)} file(s) — no signature configured", ...)`; SIGNATURES retains "in_parcels", "ut_parcels", "regrid_parcels" entries (lines 177-201) for sources that are all in _DEFERRED.

All Python dependencies are unbounded `>=` with no lockfile — every prod image build resolves latest; local venv has already drifted to pandas 3 / numpy 2.4

backend/requirements.txt:7-23 · reviewer: deps-config
What it is
Every dependency in requirements.txt, requirements-dev.txt and backend/requirements.txt is an unbounded floor (`fastapi>=0.115`, `sqlalchemy>=2.0`, `pandas>=2.0`, `uvicorn[standard]>=0.30`...) with the single exception of `PySide6==6.7.3`. There is no pip lock/constraints file anywhere (the frontend, by contrast, has package-lock.json + `npm ci`). The Dockerfile (line 39) installs backend/requirements.txt at build time, so every `fly deploy` image build re-resolves fastapi/starlette/pydantic/SQLAlchemy/uvicorn/PyJWT to whatever PyPI serves that day. The drift is already observable locally: under `pandas>=2.0` the venv now holds pandas 3.0.3 and numpy 2.4.5 — a major version the requirement was not written against.
Why it matters
Production builds are not reproducible: a deploy that changes one line of app code can silently ship a new fastapi/pydantic/SQLAlchemy major or a behavior-changing minor, and a rollback of your code does not roll back the dependency set. The same applies to the ETL pipeline — pandas 3 changed defaults (copy-on-write et al.) under a floor written for pandas 2, so output differences can appear without any repo change. Debugging 'works on the old machine, breaks on redeploy' becomes guesswork.
How to fix
Generate a pinned lock (pip-compile or `uv pip compile`) from each requirements file and install the lock in the Dockerfile and CI; keep the loose files as the human-edited inputs. At minimum, add upper bounds on majors for the prod-image set (fastapi<1, sqlalchemy<3, pydantic<3 if declared, pandas<4).
Expected improvement
Deterministic prod images and CI runs; dependency upgrades become deliberate, reviewable diffs instead of side effects of deploying.
Evidence
backend/requirements.txt:7-9 `fastapi>=0.115` / `uvicorn[standard]>=0.30` / `sqlalchemy>=2.0`; requirements.txt:4 `pandas>=2.0` while `pip list` shows `pandas 3.0.3`, `numpy 2.4.5`; Dockerfile:39 `pip install --no-cache-dir -r /tmp/backend-requirements.txt`

No consumer-side generation/schema check on owners_filter.json, parcels.pmtiles, parcels_attr.parquet, or towers.parquet — mixed-generation artifact sets mis-join silently

app/src/lib/owners-filter-index.ts:72-76 · reviewer: builder-to-browser-artifact-contract
What it is
The builder stamps `schema_version: OWNERS_INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION` (currently 5) into owners_filter.json (owners_index.py:661) plus a `generated_at`, but the client parser reads only `json.owners` and discards both (`const json = (await res.json()) as { owners?: unknown }`). parcels.pmtiles and parcels_attr.parquet/towers.parquet carry no version or generation stamp at all, and index.json's schema_version is parsed (catalog.ts:167 `requireInteger(raw.schema_version, …)`) but never compared against the supported INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION. The artifacts are cross-joined at runtime by computed keys: tower `cid` (SHA-1 of normalized parcel_owner from towers.parquet) must agree with `display_cluster_id` in owners_filter.json, and tile properties must agree with the owner rollups built from parcels_attr.parquet. A stale or mixed-generation data dir (one artifact synced/regenerated, the others not — e.g. after a normalization tweak to cluster.normalize_owner_name, or a v<5 owners_filter.json) silently mis-filters: toRecord's tolerant defaults turn missing fields into 0/''/[] (owners-filter-index.ts:32-55), so filters and scoring run on fabricated zeros, and cid lookups quietly miss.
Why it matters
This is the exact failure class the in-repo OWNERS_INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION mechanism exists to prevent on the backend seam — but the three browser-facing artifacts get no equivalent. Drift renders wrong joins and wrong filter results with zero warning, indistinguishable from real data.
How to fix
Cheapest consumer-side checks: (1) in loadOwnersFilterIndex, read `schema_version` and warn/banner when it differs from the version the client was built against (export a SUPPORTED_OWNERS_FILTER_VERSION constant); (2) in parseCompositeIndex, compare schemaVersion to the supported value instead of only type-checking it. For the unversioned parquet/pmtiles set, stamp one pipeline generation id (e.g. the owners-index generated_at) into index.json per-state entries at build time (app_assets.build_index, the single file every boot already reads) and have the client compare it with owners_filter.json's generated_at, surfacing a 'data artifacts are out of sync — re-run preprocess/sync' notice on mismatch.
Expected improvement
A stale local data dir or a half-synced deploy produces one explicit banner instead of silently wrong towers/filters/leads.
Evidence
owners-filter-index.ts:72 `const json = (await res.json()) as { owners?: unknown };` (schema_version never read); owners_index.py:661 `"schema_version": OWNERS_INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION,`; catalog.ts:167 `schemaVersion: requireInteger(raw.schema_version, 'schema_version'),` (parsed, never compared)
lowUnverified fetch effort: trivial

MANUAL_ONLY HifldTowers retains live network fetch()/remote_version() implementations instead of the ManualOnlySource mixin

scripts/fetch/sources/hifld_towers.py:52-109 · reviewer: fetch-review
What it is
When DHS pulled the public layer, the source was flipped to `automation=AutomationPolicy.MANUAL_ONLY` (line 55), but the class still subclasses DataSource directly and keeps fully functional network code: remote_version() GETs the ArcGIS metadata endpoint and fetch() downloads the full GeoJSON query. The MANUAL_ONLY policy is enforced only by caller-side checks in puller.check_one/fetch_one; any other caller (a future CLI/cron driver, a test, registry tooling via get_source — which intentionally resolves deferred sources) that invokes the source directly will auto-fetch a MANUAL_ONLY source. Every other MANUAL_ONLY source in the package (lbnl_tts, fdor_nal, state_parcels, billboards) uses the ManualOnlySource mixin whose fetch() raises RuntimeError, which CLAUDE.md §5 names as the canonical pair.
Why it matters
MANUAL_ONLY is documented as a legal boundary enforced in depth, not a UI hint. A source whose policy says manual but whose code happily auto-fetches is a footgun for exactly the future-CLI consumer the package docstring anticipates; here it would also hammer a dead DHS endpoint and could 'succeed' with an empty FeatureCollection (validate only checks for the literal string '"FeatureCollection"' in the head).
How to fix
Rebase HifldTowers on ManualOnlySource and delete the dead remote_version/fetch/validate network code (the notes already tell the user the manual workflow). If the metadata probe is worth keeping for 'did DHS republish?' checks, move it into a comment or docs instead of a live fetch path.
Expected improvement
Policy and code agree; defense-in-depth for the legal boundary instead of caller-only enforcement; ~50 lines of dead network code removed.
Evidence
hifld_towers.py:55 `automation=AutomationPolicy.MANUAL_ONLY,` coexisting with :99-104 `n = download(\n            GEOJSON_URL,\n            out, …` in a live fetch()
lowUnverified deploy effort: trivial

deploy.sh defaults FLY_APP to 'pilot-app' but production is 'morphy-consulting' — bare invocations target the wrong app and smoke-test the wrong host

deploy.sh:29-33 · reviewer: deploy-security
What it is
deploy.sh defaults `FLY_APP=pilot-app` and derives `APP_URL=https://${FLY_APP}.fly.dev` from it, while fly.toml line 8 declares `app = "morphy-consulting"` and DEPLOYMENT.md has to prefix every command with `FLY_APP=morphy-consulting`. Because deploy_code passes `--app "${FLY_APP}"` explicitly, the flag overrides fly.toml — so a bare `./deploy.sh code` attempts to deploy to a nonexistent (or worse, a leftover) 'pilot-app', `./deploy.sh data` would `fly ssh` into the wrong machine, and `./deploy.sh smoke` probes https://pilot-app.fly.dev and reports failure against a healthy production deploy.
Why it matters
Today this fails closed only because 'pilot-app' presumably doesn't exist; if an app by that name is ever created (the PRD's original working name), a forgotten env var ships production code to the wrong place silently. Even in the benign case, every operator invocation depends on remembering an env var that contradicts the in-repo default.
How to fix
Default FLY_APP to morphy-consulting, or better, parse it from fly.toml (`FLY_APP=${FLY_APP:-$(awk -F'"' '/^app =/{print $2}' "${REPO_ROOT}/fly.toml")}`) so the script can never disagree with the deploy config.
Expected improvement
Bare deploy.sh invocations target production correctly; one source of truth for the app name.
Evidence
deploy.sh line 29: `FLY_APP="${FLY_APP:-pilot-app}"`; line 33: `APP_URL="${APP_URL:-https://${FLY_APP}.fly.dev}"`; line 71: `fly deploy --app "${FLY_APP}"`. fly.toml line 8: `app = "morphy-consulting"`. DEPLOYMENT.md: 'All commands assume FLY_APP=morphy-consulting'.
lowUnverified repo effort: small

CI workflows lack permissions: blocks, install an unpinned ruff as the merge gate, and test.yml mislabels itself as a placeholder

.github/workflows/lint.yml:14-34 · reviewer: deploy-security
What it is
Three hygiene gaps across both workflows. (1) Neither lint.yml nor test.yml sets a `permissions:` block, so the GITHUB_TOKEN gets the repository default — for many repos that is read/write to contents, far more than checkout+run needs. (2) lint.yml installs `pip install "ruff>=0.6"` (and requirements-dev.txt likewise pins only `ruff>=0.6`), so every new ruff release can change lint/format verdicts and flip the merge gate red on untouched code — this is the gate the team relies on ('ruff check + ruff format --check is the merge gate'). (3) test.yml's header still reads 'the job is a no-op that always passes so PRs aren't blocked' and carries a dead `has_tests` conditional (tests/ has existed for weeks), while in reality it runs the full suite — the file's own documentation says the opposite of what the gate does. Actions are also pinned by major tag (@v4/@v5) rather than commit SHA, though for first-party actions/* that is a minor exposure.
Why it matters
Over-scoped CI tokens are the standard supply-chain hardening miss; an unpinned linter as the merge gate causes spurious red CI on unrelated PRs (and version-skew between local and CI ruff); the stale placeholder comment misleads anyone deciding whether CI actually protects a change.
How to fix
Add `permissions: contents: read` at the top level of both workflows; pin ruff to an exact version in one place (requirements-dev.txt) and have lint.yml install from it; delete the has_tests conditional and rewrite test.yml's header to describe the real full-suite gate.
Expected improvement
Least-privilege CI tokens, a deterministic merge gate, and workflow files that tell the truth about what they enforce.
Evidence
lint.yml/test.yml: no `permissions:` key anywhere. lint.yml line 28: `run: pip install "ruff>=0.6"`. test.yml lines 4-6: 'Until Section 6 ships, the job is a no-op that always passes' followed by line 53 `run: pytest --cov=scripts.preprocess --cov-report=term-missing` gated on `if [ -d tests ]`.

The ruff merge gate installs an unpinned `ruff>=0.6` — gate behavior changes whenever ruff publishes a release

.github/workflows/lint.yml:27-34 · reviewer: deps-config
What it is
lint.yml — the hard merge gate for this repo — installs ruff with `pip install "ruff>=0.6"`, and requirements-dev.txt mirrors the same open floor (`ruff>=0.6`). The venv currently holds ruff 0.15.13, nine minor versions past the floor; CI and local versions agree today only by coincidence of when each last installed. Both `ruff check` (new/changed diagnostics within the selected E/F/W/I/B/UP codes) and especially `ruff format --check` (formatting output evolves across minors) can change behavior on a new ruff release, turning main red with zero code changes.
Why it matters
A ruff release can block every open PR until someone diagnoses that the failure is tooling drift, and a contributor's locally-green `ruff format` can disagree with CI's newer ruff. For a gate this central, version skew is the classic random-Tuesday breakage.
How to fix
Pin an exact version (`ruff==0.15.13`) in requirements-dev.txt and have lint.yml install from that file (or repeat the same pin), bumping deliberately in dedicated PRs.
Expected improvement
The lint/format gate only changes when the repo chooses to upgrade ruff; local and CI results match by construction.

jsonschema is a runtime dependency of the preprocess pipeline but lives only in requirements-dev.txt, so runtime-only installs silently skip artifact validation

requirements-dev.txt:11-13 · reviewer: deps-config
What it is
scripts/preprocess/typed_artifact.py validates typed artifacts against JSON Schemas via a lazy `import jsonschema` (line 74) with a documented soft fallback: if the package is missing it logs 'jsonschema not installed; skipping %s validation' and proceeds. jsonschema is declared only in requirements-dev.txt — whose own comment concedes it is used 'at runtime' by typed_artifact.py. The documented runtime install path (`pip install -r requirements.txt`, per requirements-dev.txt's framing that dev deps are 'not needed at runtime') therefore produces a pipeline that quietly performs zero schema validation on every compose run.
Why it matters
Schema validation is the guard that catches malformed typed artifacts before they reach the app's schema-completeness contract. In an environment built from requirements.txt alone, that guard is off and the only signal is a log line that's easy to miss in a long preprocess run — a silent-degradation mode created purely by which file the dep sits in.
How to fix
Move `jsonschema>=4.21` into requirements.txt (it is small and pure-Python), leaving the soft-fallback as a defensive measure rather than the de facto configuration of runtime installs.
Expected improvement
Every documented install actually validates artifacts; the soft-skip path becomes a true edge case instead of the default for runtime-only environments.
Evidence
requirements-dev.txt:12-13 "used by tests/test_typed_artifact.py and scripts/preprocess/typed_artifact.py at runtime"; typed_artifact.py:74-77 `import jsonschema` … "jsonschema not installed; skipping %s validation."
lowUnverified frontend effort: trivial

Node toolchain skew: Docker builds on node:22, package.json declares no engines, and @types/node is pinned to Node 25

app/package.json:21 · reviewer: deps-config
What it is
Three Node version signals disagree. The production image builds the SPA on `FROM node:22-slim` (Dockerfile:6). app/package.json declares no `engines` field, so a dev machine on e.g. Node 20.18 (below vite 8's requirement of `^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0`, per the lockfile engines entry) only gets a soft npm warning. Meanwhile `@types/node` is `^25.9.1` (resolved 25.9.1 in package-lock.json), so vite.config.ts and playwright.config.ts type-check against the Node 25 API surface (tsconfig.node.json `types: ["node"]`) — two majors ahead of the runtime that actually executes the build.
Why it matters
Code in the config files (or future Node-side scripts) that uses a Node-25-only API would pass `svelte-check`/tsc cleanly and then fail at build time inside the node:22 Docker stage; conversely, an under-version local Node produces confusing vite failures with no hard stop. Version intent currently lives only implicitly in the Dockerfile.
How to fix
Add `"engines": { "node": ">=22.12 <23" }` (plus optionally an .nvmrc with 22) to app/package.json and downgrade `@types/node` to `^22`, matching the Docker build stage.
Expected improvement
Type checking, local dev, and the production image agree on one Node major; incompatible local Node versions fail fast instead of warning.
Evidence
Dockerfile:6 `FROM node:22-slim`; app/package.json:21 `"@types/node": "^25.9.1"` (lockfile resolves 25.9.1) and no `engines` key; lockfile vite engines `^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0`

Production app ships @duckdb/duckdb-wasm from a dev prerelease channel (^1.33.1-dev45.0)

app/package.json:31 · reviewer: deps-config
What it is
The runtime dependency `@duckdb/duckdb-wasm` is specified as `^1.33.1-dev45.0` and resolves to `1.33.1-dev45.0` — a continuously-published dev build, not a tagged stable release. The lockfile plus `npm ci` in the Dockerfile (line 10) make today's builds reproducible, but the caret-on-prerelease range means any `npm update` or lockfile regeneration can slide to a newer dev snapshot (dev46, dev47, …) whose changes carry no release notes or stability promises, and the prod bundle (the `duckdb` manualChunk) is built from whatever snapshot is locked.
Why it matters
DuckDB-WASM is the query engine the entire Explore/Leads data path runs on. Tracking an untagged dev channel means upgrades are diffs against unreviewed snapshots; a regression in a dev build would land via routine lockfile maintenance rather than a deliberate version bump. (Mitigating context: duckdb-wasm's npm cadence is dev-build-heavy, so this may have been the newest usable version when added.)
How to fix
Pin the exact version (`"@duckdb/duckdb-wasm": "1.33.1-dev45.0"`, no caret) so updates are explicit, or move to the nearest stable tag if one now covers the features in use.
Expected improvement
DuckDB engine upgrades become deliberate, testable events instead of incidental lockfile drift on a prerelease channel.
Evidence
app/package.json:31 `"@duckdb/duckdb-wasm": "^1.33.1-dev45.0"`; package-lock.json resolves `1.33.1-dev45.0`; Dockerfile:10 `RUN npm ci`

Stale User model docstring claims the JWT carries tenant_id, contradicting the ADR-063 identity-only invariant

backend/app/models.py:273-275 · reviewer: identity-tenancy-lifecycle
What it is
The `User` class docstring still says 'The JWT issued at sign-in carries this user's ``tenant_id``; every route then scopes to it' — a leftover from the pre-#88 design. The actual code is the opposite: the token is identity-only (main.py:229-232 'Identity-only token (#88): just the username') and the tenant is re-resolved from users.txt on every request (resolve_principal). The same docstring also says the tenant is 're-synced ... on every login' when it is re-synced on every request.
Why it matters
backend/AGENTS.md lists 'never put tenant/team claims back in the JWT' as a do-not-relax invariant; a model-level docstring asserting the old behavior is exactly the kind of authoritative-looking misdirection that leads a future session to reintroduce tenant claims.
How to fix
Rewrite the two sentences: the JWT carries identity only (the username); tenant_id is resolved from the credentials file on every request via resolve_principal -> provision_user, which re-homes the user when their team changes.
